When Every Rollout Fails: Splicing Learner-Conditioned Options into Long-Horizon Agent RL

📅 2026-11-25 17:38
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards often yields all-fail groups on long-horizon agent tasks: every sampled trajectory receives zero reward, leaving group-relative methods without a reward-based policy gradient. A common remedy supplies complete solutions from a stronger model, but full trajectories are expensive and expose the learner to long off-policy histories. We introduce SPLICE, which constructs local training targets from failed current-policy trajectories online. For each all-fail group, a proposal model selects a splice point in a current-policy trajectory and generates several bounded options from that point. We estimate each option's value using repeated current-policy continuations, convert positive estimates into a return-weighted local target, and fit the current policy on the options. We evaluate SPLICE across two policy families on two long-horizon agent tasks, Software Engineering with SWE-bench Verified and Deep Research with BrowseComp-Plus. SPLICE outperforms GRPO and full-trajectory guidance baselines in all four settings, improving over GRPO by 2.8--9.8 percentage points. On initially failed SWE tasks, it raises GRPO's graduation rate by 10.8 percentage points. Further experiments show that SPLICE improves performance while reducing costly proposal-model computation.

When Every Rollout Fails: Splicing Learner-Conditioned Options into Long-Horizon Agent RL

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards often yields all-fail groups on long-horizon agent tasks: every sampled trajectory receives zero reward, leaving group-relative methods without a reward-based policy gradient. Do We Still Need Reinforcement Learning When We Can Read the World Model?

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reinforcement learning has achieved remarkable success in sequential decision-making, but learning effective policies often requires substantial interaction experience. Model-based reinforcement learning alleviates this dependence by leveraging world models, which are typically used to support planning or policy optimization. In this paper, we investigate an alternative paradigm in which autonomous LLM-based coding agents directly synthesize control policies by reasoning over readable world models. By examining environment source code, these agents translate environmental knowledge into executable decision rules and iteratively refine their policies using online feedback or offline data, without task-specific RL training. Experiments across diverse decision-making benchmarks show that synthesized policies match or outperform conventional RL methods while requiring substantially less experience. Further analyses reveal interpretable decision rules and how they evolve through feedback-driven refinement. Comparisons with policies generated without source code access and experiments under challenging conditions further demonstrate the benefits of readable world models. These findings suggest that direct policy synthesis can reduce reliance on trial and error, challenging the necessity of task-specific RL training.

vSFT: Learning to Verbally Tune Agents at Test Time

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model agents are increasingly improved via agentic reinforcement learning, which can be on-policy but suffers from expensive rollouts and sparse rewards, limiting its application at test time. In this paper, we present a verbally Supervised Fine-Tuning (vSFT) framework that enables the conjunction of dense feedback in natural language and weight updates at test time. Specifically, our vSFT forms a closed loop between an agent and a supervisor via a hypernetwork, in which the supervisor generates verbal supervision from the agent's failed trajectories, and the hypernetwork converts that supervision into low-rank weight updates to tune the agent. We validate that this test-time in-parameter evolving process outperforms both its in-context counterparts and prevalent reinforcement learning methods. In particular, our vSFT using Qwen3-4B outperforms GRPO and Reflexion by 8.7% and 13.9% in success rate on ALFWorld, and by 4.8% and 18.2% on WebShop, respectively. We believe that learning how to adapt a model, rather than an adapted model, offers a promising path towards more capable agents that improve themselves.

Maco-RL: Scaling Agent Capabilities with Mixed-Task Reinforcement Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Mixed-task reinforcement learning (MixRL) directly trains a unified policy across domains to continually expand agent capabilities without training separate specialists or requiring an extra distillation stage. Despite its appeal, scaling MixRL poses significant challenges in achieving stable and efficient mixed-task learning, effectively allocating rollout compute, and supporting continual domain expansion. To address these challenges, we propose Maco-RL, an approach that effectively scales MixRL. Specifically, Maco-RL employs a quota-based scheduler for asynchronous rollouts across multiple task domains, thereby stabilizing training by balancing gradient contributions and mitigating off-policy effects. Along an orthogonal axis, Maco-RL further introduces Tri-Buffer Sampling (TBS) to allocate compute among samples with varying levels of mastery, promoting sustained frontier exploration while preserving acquired capabilities. Experiments on five benchmarks show Maco-RL raises Qwen3.5-27B's overall score from 43.6 to 54.2 and then to 60.1 with continual expansion, reaching the frontier held by Qwen3.8-27B, which is further pushed outward by +5.7. Maco-RL also outperforms single-task RL with less compute for policy updates. These results support MixRL as a practical and compute-efficient post-training approach with substantial potential to strengthen general agent capabilities.

Learning to Reason in Real-Time

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI agents that operate in the real world must reason correctly and on-time, yet they currently do not account for how time should alter their reasoning. Modern agents learn to do tasks in environments that are entirely synchronous: where the world waits for the agent to act before changing. A real-time environment, by contrast, changes independent of the agent. Existing approaches try to bridge this gap by imposing a ratio of thinking to acting. However, for an agent to succeed in real-time it must learn to act quickly when delay is costly and to reason longer when the benefit outweighs the missed opportunities. To this end, we introduce real-time reasoning training (RRT) as the class of training techniques for real-time reasoning agents. We develop three standard RRT techniques: (1) using real-time data, (2) decoupling acting and observing, and (3) curriculum learning in time. Together, these simple techniques substantially outperform strong baselines. Across time pressures, we outperform the strongest non-RRT baseline by an average of 33% reward on in-distribution and 55% reward on out-of-distribution tasks in two real-time environments: Frogger and Snake. These results demonstrate that we can teach agents to balance reasoning and responsiveness under real-time constraints, and that RRT opens a broad, new set of capabilities for real-time agents.

EXPOSURE: Explaining Sources of Uncertainty in Reinforcement Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Shapley values in reinforcement learning are used to explain which observation features drive an agent's behavior and expected return, but not its certainty. Explaining certainty requires understanding how observation features influence different sources of return uncertainty, and Shapley values need the agent's prediction for every subset of visible features. We propose EXPOSURE, which uses one mask-aware belief network for both action selection and Shapley explanations. It explains the variance of the return, split into an observational part due to missing features and an inherent part due to the environment's own randomness, and the entropy of its belief over the masked features. In tic-tac-toe and Minesweeper, where exact Shapley values are computable, EXPOSURE gives more accurate explanations of expected return and its uncertainty, while uniquely explaining belief uncertainty.

Collect for Coverage, Replay with Evidence: Adaptive and Efficient On-Policy Distillation for Long-Horizon Agents

📅 2026-11-25 17:27
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Fully online on-policy distillation (OPD) is costly for long-horizon agents because each update depends on fresh environment rollouts. Prefix replay offers an efficient alternative by sampling current-student responses from stored histories, reducing idle time for long-tail rollouts and allowing each collected trajectory to support multiple updates. However, our analysis of the complete prefix replay pipeline identifies two limitations in long-horizon settings. In trajectory collection, teacher-only trajectories miss student-induced histories, while broader history coverage can reduce teacher reliability. In trajectory reuse, fixed position-based schedules cannot identify critical decisions that may occur at any trajectory depth. We introduce CARE-OPD (Collect for coverAge, Replay with Evidence) to address both limitations. Student-seeded teacher completions and response-level confidence gating expand history coverage while filtering unreliable updates. Hindsight replay allocation then uses downstream execution evidence to select consequential turns. Across long-horizon tasks Software Engineering and Deep Research, CARE-OPD outperforms online OPD and prior prefix replay baselines. On SWE-bench Verified, it raises task success to 59.5% while reducing end-to-end training time by 45% and environment actions by 44% relative to asynchronous online OPD.

Keep or Roll Back? Out-of-Loop Judges for Self-Improving VLA and End-to-End Embodied Policies

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Self-improvement is now a practical recipe for embodied policies: a policy collects experience, is retrained, and is kept when a judge rates it higher. However, the judge is usually trained or chosen inside the same loop, so its evidence is not independent of the policy. We introduce SealedLoop, an evaluation framework that adds a sealed judge: an evaluator outside the loop that the improver cannot see or optimize against. A new policy is kept only when a sequential test on the sealed judge confirms the gain, with a bounded probability of keeping a worse one. Running the same loop under each judge, we separate two sources of a false gain, which we call judge error (the loop's judge is wrong on its own data) and transfer gap (a real gain there does not carry over to unseen conditions). We evaluate on LIBERO manipulation, NAVSIM driving, and a full-scale real vehicle. In manipulation, a success classifier accepted 65 of 196 failures in one generation. In driving, policies that gain on the loop's own frames lose in an unseen city, so even an exact in-loop judge would keep 8/15 of them. When the sealed test decides, a driving loop built to improve ends with a better policy than under its own critic (0.674 against 0.649 PDMS, from 0.638), and on driving pairs with known sealed gain the test accepted 11 of 12 real gains and none of 59 losses. In a real-world field experiment on a published improvement step, the reinforced version drove further into a curve than the version it started from in all 6 blind pairs, though neither completed it. These results show that evidence from inside the loop, however accurate, is not enough to decide whether a policy has improved. SealedLoop offers a foundation for trustworthy self-improvement of embodied policies, in which progress is judged by evidence the improving system cannot influence.

Post-Training Policy Replication for Strategy-Following Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning across Team Sizes

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Although multi-agent deep reinforcement learning enables coordinated behavior, aligning it with managerial intent remains difficult due to complex agent interactions. Although the strategy-following distributed attentional actor architecture (sfDA7-X) improves controllability via regional instructions, instruction combinations scale exponentially with team and instruction sizes, leading to prohibitive training costs and poor transferability. To reduce this computational cost, we introduce a team-size-independent observation architecture and reconstruct collective behavior by replicating a single trained policy across agents in teams of different sizes, thereby eliminating the need to train from scratch. Additionally, we evaluate warm-start retraining by initializing policy networks with a pre-trained policy to significantly lower computational overhead. In an object collection game, a policy trained in a four-agent team is deployed to teams of three to eight agents. Experiments demonstrate that training with the proposed aggregated observation matrix achieves performance comparable to that of the baseline method, sfDA7-X. Furthermore, policy replication preserves performance without substantial degradation in three- and five-agent teams, and warm-start retraining outperforms training from scratch while reducing required training episodes by up to 90%. Deployments to six- and eight-agent teams further confirm effective spatial region allocation following given instructions. These results demonstrate that the proposed method enables zero-shot collective behavior reconstruction via policy replication.

Attributing Agentic Failure in Reasoning Vision-Language Models with Interactive 2D Environments

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agentic benchmarks report a success rate that confounds distinct causes of failure: misreading the environment, never working out how components of the environment operates, operating the components in the wrong order, and failing to recover from a mistake, each of which calls for a different fix. We present an early preview of MultiNet v2.0-Gridworld - an interactive maze environment built for attribution rather than ranking. Four different types of mechanisms, namely key-door pairs, switch-gate pairs, distractors and decoys, and ordered combinations of these mechanisms, can be added, modified, or removed independently while the renderer, the action space, and the scoring function stay fixed. A breadth-first search over the joint state of position, facing direction, inventory, and mechanism state gives every instance an exact optimal action count, which sets its difficulty and grades unfinished episodes. We fix the evaluation protocol on 15 held-out mazes over 540 episodes, then evaluate three vision-language models on 50 mazes. They solve 6 of 150 episodes, 45 of the 50 mazes were not solved by any model, and no episode containing a switch-gate mechanism was solved.

PROVE: Mitigating Long-Horizon LLM Collusion with Reinforcement Learning from Verified Evidence

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

When LLM agents verify each other's work over a long interaction, they drift into collusion: they come to accept work without the evidence that the verification protocol requires. Collusion is absent at the start and builds up episode by episode, which we trace to accurate feedback accumulating in memory. An agent that follows the rule is told that its verdict was wrong whenever its peer was right, so breaking the rule is the reward-maximizing choice, and agents talk themselves into it in their private reflections. Existing mitigations leave the agents unchanged; we train the behavior out. PROVE (Protocol Reinforcement from Verified Evidence) is a multi-agent reinforcement learning method that rewards verdicts by the evidence behind them rather than by their outcome, and credits every call an agent makes, including the calls that solve its own task, with the outcome that call controls. On Qwen3.6-27B, a LoRA adapter trained on six-episode interactions takes the collusion rate on held-out task sequences at the original ten-episode horizon from 65.0% to 1.0%, with no detectable change in task success. Collusion that converged in every interaction now converges in none, and the gain holds four episodes beyond the longest interaction seen in training. The trained 27B model colludes less than every model evaluated in the original study, closed-weight frontier models included. Code is available at https://github.com/Haochen-Luo/PROVE.

RePace: Reasoning about Demonstration Speedup for Faster Vision-Language-Action Policies

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Since vision-language-action (VLA) policies can inherit the slow pace of human demonstrations, accelerating these demonstrations before fine-tuning can lead to faster VLA policies without an additional speed-selection module at deployment. Several offline speedup methods have focused on identifying segments that require less precision and assigning predefined speedup factors to the segments. However, even within a single segment, a speedup factor suitable for steady motion may be too high when precise alignment is required. To this end, this paper presents RePace, an offline reasoning framework that determines how much to accelerate each part of a demonstration. For the same recorded movement, RePace measures how much each candidate speedup factor shortens the sequence and compares the resulting observation-action pairs used for training. Given the task instruction, a pretrained language model uses the visual context and original motion of successive windows to interpret these candidate retiming effects and select a speedup profile for the complete episode. Experimental results demonstrate that RePace outperforms the baselines in overall task execution efficiency on both LIBERO and RoboCasa-Kitchen. On all three tasks with a gripper-equipped Franka Research 3 arm, RePace matches or exceeds the acceleration baselines in success rate and completes successful trials faster.

Diffusion Steering via Adaptive Reward Estimation and Resampling

📅 2026-11-25 18:18
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Text-to-image diffusion models can generate images that satisfy complex prompts, yet often produce images that omit requested objects, attributes, or relationships. We ask if models can more reliably generate text-aligned images by redistributing probability toward their more successful outcomes. We propose TrailMix, a training-free method that uses the base model's distribution of possible outcomes to adaptively guide generation toward better text alignment. During denoising, TrailMix generates inexpensive lookahead images from parallel trajectories (trails) and scores their alignment with the prompt using an external judge. The resulting distribution of reward scores allows us to estimate how strongly to steer generation toward a desired alignment level. We use these estimates to reweight and resample trajectories, favoring more promising outcomes. Across three text-to-image benchmarks, TrailMix outperforms existing baselines, with particularly strong gains on rare multi-object compositions. We further extend TrailMix to targeted image editing and introduce SEAM-Bench (Semantic Editing and Attribute Maintenance), a systematic benchmark evaluating whether semantic edits preserve other image content. Experiments on SEAM-Bench and existing benchmark PIE-Bench demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach for controlled image editing. Our results show how a model's own possible outcomes can inform reliable and controllable generation.

Poisoning Self-Evolving Security Policies in LLM Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Self-evolving defenses improve LLM-agent security by learning reusable policies from observed attacks. However, we demonstrate that this learning process itself can become an attack surface. Using SED (Self-Evolving Defense) in the AgentDojo benchmark, we construct a single prompt-injection episode that causes the defense to learn an over-broad policy and subsequently refuse legitimate requests. Across 10 Nemotron runs, every poisoned run over-refuses at least one legitimate task, while unrelated-task performance remains unchanged and the original prompt-injection defense remains effective. The attack also transfers to gpt-oss-120b; however, its poisoning mechanism depends on a successful initial prompt injection and therefore fails to activate when the target agent resists the underlying injection. In contrast, integrity-oriented attacks targeting SED's judge, policy synthesis, retrieval, and organization mechanisms fail in our evaluated settings, identifying over-refusal as the primary exploitable failure mode observed in our experiments. We further identify self-poisoning from false-positive harm judgments without an attacker. Finally, contrastive policy synthesis and an automatic broadness-check gate substantially reduce, but do not eliminate over-refusal, without compromising security in our evaluated settings.

RoboEvo$^2$: Recursive Robot Self-Improvement via Harness-Policy Co-Evolution

📅 2026-11-25 16:39
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Robots should adapt to unfamiliar environments and acquire new skills through interaction, then execute those skills efficiently with practice. Vision-language-action policies provide efficient control, but often need additional human demonstrations to handle unfamiliar settings; reasoning agents can discover solutions using broad prior knowledge and robot-control APIs, but repeated deliberation and repair are costly. Inspired by human learning through reasoning and practice, we propose RoboEvo$^2$, a framework for recursive harness-policy co-evolution. In Policy-to-Harness (P2H) evolution, policy failures guide a coding agent to search complementary Code, Code+ICL, and Code+Policy routes, consolidating verified solutions into reusable skills, routing strategies, and experience memory. In Harness-to-Policy (H2P) evolution, verified trajectories provide supervised training, while Counterfactual Preference Distillation (CPD) teaches the policy to favor successful corrections over failures from the same starting state. The improved policy provides stronger motor skills for subsequent search, while its remaining failures redirect harness evolution, closing the reciprocal loop. Through recursive co-evolution without additional human demonstrations, RoboEvo$^2$ improves standalone success from 11.0% to 84.1% and hybrid success from 30.4% to 90.5% on LIBERO-PRO, with similar gains and reduced agentic costs on RoboTwin C2R. Further experiments demonstrate autonomous task acquisition, cross-embodiment harness transfer, and 95.0% hybrid success on the Unitree G1-D. Our models and code will be publicly released.

HumblePRM: Improving VLM Abstention in Ambiguous Situations through Process Reward Modeling

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reliable deployment of Vision Language Models (VLMs) requires that a model's expressed confidence remain grounded in what it can actually see, and that it recognize when the visual evidence is too ambiguous to commit to a confident response. Standard post-training uses outcome reward models (ORMs) that supervise only the final decision, rewarding a confident answer regardless of whether the image supports one, while existing methods either tend to detect uncertainty post-hoc or assume an objective verdict always exists. We instead split multimodal reasoning into two stages: perception, where the model must factually describe the scene, and calibration, where it must judge whether that evidence is sufficient to commit to a conclusion. We introduce HumblePRM, a lightweight process reward model (PRM) that scores each stage independently, rewarding agreement between evidence and expressed confidence rather than outcome correctness alone. Training with HumblePRM increases abstention due to uncertainty on ambiguous inputs considerably while leaving accuracy on clear images roughly unchanged. We also find that the improvement transfers to Gemma 4 E4B, a larger, popular edge model. Our findings show that process reward supervision is a promising route to VLMs with better calibrated abstention, ensuring more reliability rather than unpredictable behavior.

How Many Samples Is RL Worth? Best-of-N versus RL Post-Training for Text-to-Image Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reinforcement learning (RL) on a reward that can be checked automatically has become a common last step in the training of text-to-image models. A simpler way to get more correct images is best-of-N: draw N images from the base model and keep the one that a picker prefers. We ask how many base model samples one RL sample is worth, and call this number the exchange rate. We measure it for Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium and its public Flow-GRPO checkpoint on 398 new prompts in the style of GenEval, the benchmark behind the RL reward, with human labels on every prompt and two automatic judges other than the reward. The answer depends on the prompt. Four fifths of the prompts do not ask for a position (for example, one object left of another). On these, the base model catches up: best-of-16 with an off-the-shelf vision-language model (VLM) as the picker is about as good as one RL image. A person who looks through up to 3 base images finds a correct one as often as one RL image is correct. On the prompts that ask for a position, RL is far ahead: a person finds a correct image among 8 base images for only 52% of the prompts, against 92% for one RL image. Over all prompts, our primary test, which we fixed in advance, finds best-of-16 with the VLM picker 9.3 points behind one RL image when both automatic judges must pass an image. People see a smaller gap, 5.5 and 6.5 points in two checks. FLUX.1-dev shows a similar gap in the same test. RL also solves 20 prompts that the base model misses in 128 tries, and 1,024 tries recover half of them. The automatic judges are wrong in opposite directions: a detector-based judge like the reward, as used in our primary test, reports a larger gap than people see, and VLM judges report little or none. Comparisons of RL with best-of-N should therefore check the selected images by hand.

Tune for Retention, Not Just Gain: SFT Forgets Far Less than You Think

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Catastrophic forgetting, the drastic loss of previously acquired capabilities when learning new ones, is a central challenge in language model fine-tuning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is widely believed to be prone to such forgetting. Recent works have attributed this to the off-policy nature of SFT, arguing that on-policy learning is key to continual learning. In this paper, we challenge this premise. We show that while maximizing new-task performance can induce catastrophic forgetting, alternative hyperparameter choices (lower learning rate and batch size) can yield similar gains with little to no forgetting. With hyperparameters selected jointly for gain and retention, vanilla SFT retains prior capabilities far better than previously suggested and can be competitive with Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT), an on-policy approach designed to mitigate forgetting. While SDFT can be more robust to hyperparameter choices, it also requires a retention-aware criterion to identify hyperparameter configurations that avoid catastrophic forgetting. Our results challenge the view that on-policy training is what enables learning with retention and raise broader questions about what drives catastrophic forgetting during fine-tuning.

Best-of-$N$ Test-Time Steering Can Lead to Reward Hacking in Vision-Language-Action Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown promise for general-purpose robotic manipulation, but adapting to changing environmental conditions still remains challenging. Recent test-time steering methods have shown promise in enabling adaptive VLA policies by utilizing additional computation during deployment. In this paper, we consider best-of-$N$ test-time steering for VLA models, where multiple action candidates are sampled from a fixed policy and a verifier is used to select the best one during deployment. We show that increasing $N$ does not always improve performance when the verifier is inaccurate, and can favor candidates whose scores exploit prediction errors, a form of reward hacking. We study this effect on 60 LIBERO-Safety task-level cases using a $π_{0.5}$ policy, with ten repetitions per case. We introduce controlled errors in a MuJoCo trajectory verifier and show that as $N$ increases, selected candidate rewards diverge from the accurate verifier rewards, leading to reward hacking. We also show that verifier calibration and softmax-based candidate selection can mitigate reward hacking in some settings.

EvoLink-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Self-Evolving Agents in Long-Horizon Interdependent Task Streams

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model (LLM) agents are evolving from passive chatbots into autonomous systems that operate continuously in dynamic environments. Such agents need not only solve current tasks but also learn from past interactions to improve future performance. However, existing streaming benchmarks often rely on short task sequences or largely independent tasks, providing limited evidence of whether agents can effectively reuse experience when later tasks depend on earlier outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{EvoLink-Bench}, a benchmark for evaluating self-evolving agents in long-horizon, interdependent task streams. It covers daily office work, multi-turn dialogue, and AutoResearch-style workflows, with persistent workspaces and dependencies across tasks. The benchmark includes two complementary levels: \textbf{L1}, which evaluates the acquisition and revision of language-expressible knowledge, and \textbf{L2}, which assesses the development of task-level capabilities through execution and feedback. Its evaluation further distinguishes within-task recovery, persistent-state updates, and experience reuse in later tasks. We evaluate five agent frameworks across three backbone configurations under three persistence conditions, covering 19,635 scored task slots from selected evaluation cohorts. Results show that no framework consistently performs best across both levels, retries recover only a limited proportion of failed tasks, and persistent-state updates do not necessarily improve subsequent performance. Furthermore, the effects of evolution differ between knowledge- and capability-level tasks, suggesting that these two forms of evolution require different learning mechanisms. Performance gains also vary across domains and task streams, with improvements on some tasks not necessarily extending to others.

Beyond the Happy Path: Generalizing Robot World Models from Simulated Failures

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Robot world models are increasingly used to evaluate and plan policies, yet robot training data are dominated by successful demonstrations. Off the happy path, when a grasp is misaligned or mistimed and the object is pushed, tilted, or slips, their predictions break down. Across five pretrained action-conditioned models, object-motion error is at most 10.4% lower than that of a static baseline that copies the first frame, and two models are worse than this baseline. We present WorldReforge, which teaches world models what happens when manipulation goes wrong. We construct the Reforge dataset of 62k simulated trajectories by perturbing reference grasps in position, orientation, and closure timing, recording each resulting push, slip, or failed pickup with ground-truth object pose, contact, and surface points. WorldReforge turns this ground truth into two training signals without additional inference cost. Interaction Alignment focuses reconstruction on the small object and contact regions that a global loss underweights, and Surface Correspondence trains backbone features to follow the same physical surface points across frames. On Cosmos3 Nano, WorldReforge reduces object trajectory error by 28.2% overall and by 35.0% on unseen objects, and it lowers the error in predicting how two actions' outcomes diverge by 43.3%. Although trained only in simulation, it also improves reconstruction on DROID and on real failure videos. Controlled data studies show that failed contact and successful holding teach complementary physics: failed contact teaches how objects respond, and holding teaches how a grasped state persists. Our code, data, and models are available at aim-uofa.github.io/WorldReforge/.

LatentCP-DPO: Conformal Pessimism for DPO with Finite-Sample Guarantees

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) learns a response policy from human preference data, but with a limited dataset it can overfit to noisy or weakly informative preference labels. We introduce LatentCP-DPO, a framework that combines conformal prediction with pessimistic preference optimization for Bradley-Terry (BT) pairwise feedback and Plackett-Luce (PL) ranking feedback. Building on LatentCP's construction of uncertainty sets for unobserved model parameters, we address how to incorporate this uncertainty into preference optimization and characterize its effect on policy performance. Under exchangeability and a correctly specified preference model, we construct uncertainty sets for latent relative rewards with finite-sample marginal coverage from conformal prediction sets for observable preferences. We then learn a response policy by maximizing the worst-case expected preference log-likelihood over these uncertainty sets, using an independent collection of prompts and candidate responses without preference labels. Under suitable assumptions, we establish finite-sample upper bounds on regularized policy suboptimality that separate policy-class approximation error, conformal uncertainty, and statistical estimation error. For BT feedback, we further establish a worst-case lower bound for LatentCP-DPO that matches the upper bound's reciprocal dependence on the number of unlabeled optimization examples. The lower bound also shows that the uncertainty sets can induce a bias that persists as the optimization sample grows. Together, these results characterize both the statistical accuracy and potential limitations of preference optimization using conformal uncertainty sets. Experiments support the benefits of the proposed framework in finite preference datasets.

Budget, Readout or Reasoning? Where Small Reasoning Models Lose Value in Auction Bidding

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Ad platforms and marketplaces show bidders a bid landscape, the estimated chance of winning at each candidate bid, and increasingly let agents built on reasoning language models choose the bid. We give six open reasoning models of 4B to 14B parameters such landscapes, with win curves from eBay closing prices and from a simulated advertising market (AuctionNet), and score their bids on held-out auctions against an exact calculator. Because the agent is handed the estimates, the lost value can be split by cause. Two failures belong to the models. They pass over the best option even when the values written in their own trace already rank it first, and their wrong answers lean toward the highest bid on the menu. The lean is about the bid's size, not its place in the list, and it survives sampling. At 768 forced tokens on eBay, under pay-your-bid pricing, the wrong answers cost bidders 7-17% of the surplus the calculator's bids earn. Arithmetic errors are rare. Two further losses come from operator choices. A token cap cuts traces short: allowed to stop on their own within 4,096 tokens, five models recover 91-100% of the calculator's lead over a model that does no reasoning. Asking for the answer while the reasoning block is still open misreads it: closing the block first lifts the weakest model from 42% to 84% on eBay and from 24% to 81% on AuctionNet. Finally, an audit that flags bids disagreeing with the trace's own numbers, and overrides them with the calculator's bid, raises the share of the calculator's lead recovered at 768 forced tokens from 42-85% to 84-100% on eBay under nested cross-validation, and the same reader settings transfer from eBay to AuctionNet.

Inverse Reinforcement Learning with State-Dependent Foresight for Human Decision-Making

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Computational modeling of behavior represents individuals with a model fitted to their observed behavior and characterizes them through its parameters. Naturalistic tasks incorporate the complexity of real environments and yield high-dimensional behavioral trajectories. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) infers the latent reward function underlying these trajectories. Standard IRL, however, assumes effectively infinite-horizon planning, whereas human foresight is both bounded and state-dependent. Here we introduce SC-AIRL (State-Conditional Adversarial IRL), which builds these two cognitive properties into the planner. Per-depth policies share one reward but differ in how far ahead they sum it explicitly. A state-conditional router mixes them according to the current state. Across held-out episodes in pedestrian crossing and highway overtaking tasks, SC-AIRL improves imitation over Standard AIRL, raising top-1 accuracy from 0.549 to 0.647 and from 0.629 to 0.718, respectively, while more closely reproducing participant behavior in rollouts. Inferred planning depth correlates with self-reported anxiety and motor impulsivity. SC-AIRL thus reproduces human behavior more closely than Standard AIRL while providing an interpretable, model-derived measure of state-dependent foresight.

EGSE: Evidence-Grounded Skill Evolution for Medical Reasoning Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Medical reasoning agents combine foundation models with specialized tools to interpret medical images, yet reusing their historical experience remains challenging. A correct final answer can conceal flawed reasoning or tool use, synthesized advice may lack source support, and superficially similar cases may require different diagnostic procedures. In this paper, we propose EGSE, an evidence-grounded skill evolution framework that distills historical dialogues from similar cases into reusable conditional workflows and progressively refines a multi-faceted skill library through supported and validated updates to improve medical agents. EGSE evaluates intermediate reasoning and tool use and contrasts repeated successful and failed rollouts to extract useful practices and evidence-supported avoidance guidance. These experiences are synthesized into workflows with applicability conditions and checks traceable to source dialogues. Library additions and merges require source-grounded review and execution validation. At inference time, EGSE routes queries to skill nodes organized by imaging modality, question task type, and disease category, then retrieves and selects applicable workflows based on their conditions, the current images, and the question to guide evidence acquisition and interpretation. Experiments across four chest X-ray benchmarks show that EGSE outperforms representative medical agents and skill evolution methods in reasoning accuracy while reducing tool calls. It also delivers consistent accuracy gains across seven foundation models and supports skill transfer across datasets.

DecisionReplay: A Corpus and Offline Simulator for Ensembling Decision Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Comparing ensembles, routers and cascades requires the output of every model on every item, and its cost. Stored collections built for routing keep no per-option probabilities. We provide a dataset on which ensemble methods, routers and cascades are simulated quickly, without new model calls. DecisionReplay holds the probability vectors of 17 commercial decision models, endpoints that return a probability for every answer option, on 35 benchmarks, with the token counts, billed cost and measured latency of every call, all released. A deterministic simulator replays any policy that selects, combines or cascades them. One well-chosen model per benchmark is hard to beat: pooling all models loses 2.7 accuracy points to it, and stacking gains 1.0 points at 3.4 times its cost. The models are wrong together: two that are both wrong choose the same wrong option in 72% of cases. The best router matches that model at 64% of its cost, and certified early exit cuts the cost of a stacked ensemble by 43% without changing an answer. DecisionReplay makes it cheap and fast to test whether a new method changes these results.

FEAST: Fair and Efficient Adaptive Sampling for Task-Free On-Policy Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Language agents trained from self-generated experience must allocate a limited rollout budget across heterogeneous capabilities. A common strategy is to prioritize capabilities with strong current learning signals, but in on-policy learning these signals are endogenous: past allocation changes the policy and therefore the evidence available for future allocation. This can create a feedback loop in which under-trained capabilities receive increasingly fewer opportunities to improve. We formalize this effect through the Quota-Efficiency Law (QEL), which shows that allocation history can change learning efficiency even under matched capability-specific exposure. Motivated by this, we introduce FEAST, a task-free adaptive sampling framework that separates opportunity allocation from compute allocation. FEAST dynamically discovers capability clusters while preserving their learning histories, maintains continued sampling opportunities across clusters, and reserves a rollout budget for each selected cluster. Within each budget, it progressively concentrates computation on prompts with informative reward variation. Across GURU-18K and SMC, FEAST improves average performance over competitive adaptive sampling baselines under matched rollout budgets, while better preserving learning progress on weaker capabilities. Ablation studies further support the complementary roles of cross-cluster opportunity protection and within-cluster selective allocation. Code will be released upon acceptance.

Triage Before Fine-Tuning: Feasibility-Gated Selection and Monitored Adaptation of Embodied Foundation Models for a Fixed Physical Platform

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Embodied foundation models are increasingly deployed by selection: a practitioner picks a public checkpoint by benchmark rank and fine-tunes it on local data. The platform is fixed before the model is chosen, with little or no local data. We ask what such a checkpoint loses on a fixed platform and which part of the loss local data can buy back. We split the gap by what can repair it. A sensing gap, information the sensors do not capture, cannot be closed with their data; a latency gap, output arriving after the control loop needs it, belongs to the loop, not the weights; only a distribution gap is fine-tuning's to repair. TRIAGE turns this into feasibility-gated selection and monitored adaptation in five stages: gates on the sensor contract and on end-to-end latency that no score can override, probes on ten minutes of local logs, a ranking by target-relevant score among the candidates that pass, and fine-tuning that is monitored and checked in closed loop before release. On driving benchmarks, a deadline below a leading closed-loop model's latency makes it collide on 80 to 90% of routes, and losing field of view moves plans 2.2 to 5.7 times more than losing resolution at equal pixels. Local frames close 75% of a stability gap but raise perturbation sensitivity at small budgets, and picking by score violates a gate in two of four deployment profiles. On a full-scale vehicle, the factory camera failed the sensing gate and an added camera repaired it. The latency gate removed the two highest-scoring candidates, neither of which completed a closed-loop trip. The selected model passed acceptance in 4 of 5 closed-loop trips, and the closed-loop check withheld a fine-tuned model that had improved offline. TRIAGE lets organizations that cannot train foundation models from scratch find out whether a public checkpoint can run on the robot or vehicle they own, and what to fix if not.

Not all world models can doubt: how training objective and architecture shape epistemic uncertainty in JEPA and RSSM world models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World models predict how an environment responds to actions, so that candidate actions can be evaluated before execution. Executed actions, or decisions, taken in this manner are only as reliable as the model's predictive capabilities, and predictions in turn are only reliable within the model's experience. Epistemic uncertainty (EU) is the signal that marks where that experience ends. In this work we offer an extensive empirical study on how training objectives and architectures shape epistemic uncertainty of world models. More precisely, we present a controlled comparison of post-hoc EU (last-layer Laplace and ensembles) between two dominant models of different paradigms: 1) LeWorldModel (LeWM): A joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), which predicts the next embedding without a decoder, and 2) DreamerV3: A recurrent state-space model (RSSM), which reconstructs the observation from a recurrent latent state. In ten simulated visual-control environments, we follow a controlled state perturbation of the environment state through every stage of the models up to the final EU estimate. We find that the RSSM attenuates the state perturbation through two mechanisms before it reaches an estimator on the predictor feature. Its recurrent update averages over context, so a change in a single frame reaches the state at a fraction of its size and recovers only as the change persists. Its reconstruction objective weights an entity by the pixels it covers, so a small object can be lost due to the training objective. The JEPA has neither mechanism and passes the change through all stages to the uncertainty. We further show that this translates to gaps in five uncertainty related downstream tasks, from unfamiliar-state detection to uncertainty-penalised planning: the RSSM uncertainty misses deviations that its own posterior registers, whereas the JEPA uncertainty reports what its model sees.

What Is a Theory Worth to an LLM Solver? Measuring, Inducing, and Merging Theories in Context

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Mathematicians solve problems faster when they know the right theory. We ask how much a stated theory is worth to a large language model (LLM) solver, and whether a model can write that theory itself. In a length-matched protocol, adding the right theory to the context of Qwen3-14B raises pass@1 on pattern waiting-time problems from 0.37 to 0.83 and cuts the median tokens of correct solutions from 10.2k to 6.3k, while worked examples of the same length do not help. The gain shrinks as the solver and its token budget grow (+0.46, +0.26, +0.07), and at a long budget it comes almost entirely from finishing within budget: solutions that finish are correct 98--100\% of the time in every condition. To separate induction from recall, we plant unpublished theorems in custom probability families and measure how much of the oracle theory's gain an induced theory recovers. A strong open model, required to explore the training problems with code before writing, recovers 60\% of the gain on one planted family, 48\% on a second, and almost none on a third, where it finds the right form but not the condition that makes the form computable. No single induced note is complete, but a strong model that merges several notes recovers 61\% of the gain on the classic family, above the best single note (47\%). Theories are worth the reasoning they save, and models can find their pieces before they can assemble them.

Procedure Inference as Memory: Learning Recruiter Decision Procedures from OKF-Structured Episodes in Multi-Session Hiring Assistants

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multi-session hiring assistants must infer evolving recruiter preferences and decision patterns across projects and time. We introduce Open Knowledge Format (OKF)-indexed episodic memory to organize interactions and retrieve related events through their lineage. We also introduce Procedure Memory, which learns reusable decision procedures from these episodes through prompted reflection or reinforcement learning. We compare these approaches with no memory, full-history context, and conversation search, and evaluate Jev-class models for index navigation and GPT-5.6 Luna as a lower-cost alternative to GPT-5.4. In our evaluation, OKF-indexed retrieval achieves low-priority preference F1 scores of 0.93-0.95 at medium and high preference overlap between hiring projects. It significantly outperforms full-history context and conversation search in both settings, and no memory at high overlap, while using 69% fewer context tokens than full history. Procedure Memory reduces conversational turns by up to 39% relative to no memory. Qualitative and long-horizon evaluations provide evidence of temporal reasoning, with RL-learned procedures achieving 60% on both correct abstention and exact-field identification. Jev-class models improve conversational efficiency but more frequently miss temporally relevant evidence, highlighting the importance of evidence selection. On PersonaMem-v2, Procedure Memory with OKF retrieval achieves 50.2% accuracy versus 48.6% for full-history context and 29.0% for no memory, using 90.5% fewer reader input tokens than full history. GPT-5.6 Luna performs comparably to GPT-5.4 at lower cost. These findings highlight the value of structured episodic memory and learned decision procedures for efficient personalization and temporal reasoning.

Sequentially Acquiring Concept Knowledge to Guide Continual Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The goal of continual learning (CL) is to adapt to new data (plasticity) while retaining the knowledge acquired from old data (stability). Existing methods are explicitly designed to focus on balancing stability and plasticity to mitigate the challenge of catastrophic forgetting while promoting learning. However, the impact of order and nature of samples from the new data that the network is trained on remains an under explored factor. We argue that a CL algorithm should have the ability to rank incoming samples in terms of their relationship with prior data and their effect on the learning process. In this work, we investigate if scoring and prioritizing incoming data based on their semantic relationships with the model's current knowledge can benefit CL. We propose SACK, short for Sequentially Acquiring Concept Knowledge, a plug and play technique for continual learning methods. SACK dissects categorical knowledge of the model into fine-grained concepts, computes the relationships between previously learned concepts and new concepts in each experience, and uses this relationship knowledge for prioritizing new samples. Experiments across several types of CL methods (regularization, replay, and prompt based) in class incremental and task incremental settings demonstrate that integration of SACK not only improves accuracy and reduces forgetting but also handles long tail distribution, helps focus on semantically interpretable regions, and yields better calibrated and robust models compared to prior approaches.

Dynamic Reasoning in Next-Gen Detection and Response: An Empirical Study of LLM Triage, Reinforcement Tuning, and Adversarial Evasion

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present an end-to-end empirical study of dynamic reasoning in next-generation detection and response pipelines, evaluating how static classifiers, graph-based signal fusion, and large language model (LLM) triage agents perform under strict human-analyst attention budgets (Recall at B alerts/day). Across 56 reproducible experiments spanning a multi-layer enterprise-and-cloud simulator and four public datasets (SOREL-20M, BODMAS, LANL, and DARPA Transparent Computing), we test twelve architectural claims governing modern fleet-scale defense. First, we show that fleet growth halves recall at a fixed analyst budget even at negligible compute cost (<$0.0003 per million events), and that rarity-weighted graph risk propagation outperforms GraphSAGE without neural training. Second, evaluating frontier reasoning models (Gemini) and fine-tuned open-weight models (Qwen3-4B) for alert triage reveals that unconstrained LLM auto-closure eliminates 90% of false positives while silently discarding 31-44% of true attack cases. Third, post-training with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) causes severe reward hacking on unseen attack classes, suppressing up to 86% of novel attacks compared to two-epoch supervised fine-tuning. Finally, against an adaptive adversary generating plausible cover stories in benign telemetry fields, LLM triage evasion rises sharply unless gated by a deterministic policy harness prior to model invocation.

Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces for Hard Math Problems

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Post-training large language models (LLMs) for mathematical reasoning often relies on accepted traces whose final answers pass a verifier. For hard math problems, however, such traces can be rare under independent Monte Carlo (MC) sampling. To address this, we propose Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces (DART), a trace-mining method that starts from a rejected trace, decomposes it into reasoning steps, and ranks them by position-weighted uncertainty. It then generates deliberately distinct alternative candidates for highly ranked steps using reference-answer guidance and a meta-thought before completing the resulting traces. On greedy-unsolved DeepMath-103K problems, DART recovers 37% more problems than MC sampling at a matched number of generated traces. Its estimated generation cost per recovered problem is nearly identical, while its cost per accepted trace is 30% lower. Post-training Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on traces mined by DART achieves the highest point estimate in five of six evaluations across MATH-500 and AIME benchmarks. These results demonstrate that revisiting rejected traces with DART can expand problem coverage and provide effective post-training data.
