PRECEPT: Security Self-Improvement of Coding Agents via Evolving Principles

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI coding agents increasingly write and submit code with limited human review, and much of it passes functional tests while remaining insecure. We show that these failures persist. Across 14 task sequences, Codex, Claude Code, and Qwen Code fixed a vulnerability 85.7% of the time when shown the failing security test, then reintroduced the same vulnerability class in a new session 26.7% of the time, about as often as agents that had never seen it (27.5%). The capability is present; the retention is not. Neither recording the repair in the agent's persistent context nor supplying published secure coding principles closed the gap: the recorded fix stayed tied to the family of the task it came from, and the principles, written for human developers, remained too general to apply to the code at hand. The knowledge exists; it is not activated. We call the missing ability security self-improvement: an agent learns from its own security failures so that each lesson persists across sessions, applies to new code, and does not break what works. We present PRECEPT, which equips coding agents with this ability through an evolving library that starts from authoritative secure coding principles and revises them against the agent's own failures, so that each principle comes to fire on the code where that agent actually goes wrong. A revision is kept only if fresh executions show it makes code more secure while keeping functional success within a small tolerance of the agent without the library. Improvement continues across evolution steps rather than saturating after the first. On task families never seen during evolution, PRECEPT's weakest agent reaches 97.1% secure working code, above the strongest unaided agent at 81.9% and the strongest baseline at 87.7%.

General-Purpose Decision Models for AI Configuration Tuning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI configuration tuning searches for settings that improve the performance of AI systems under a limited budget. Existing tuners use different sampling and search policies, making the choice of decision strategy central to both tuning quality and cost. We investigate whether general-purpose decision models can guide tuning, using Jev as a deployed representative that returns structured choices and numeric scores across diverse tasks. To assess where it helps, we compare its use as an optimizer, performance predictor, and region guider against conventional methods and a large language model (LLM) on eight AI tuning tasks. Conventional optimizers attain rank 1 in six of eight comparisons. Similarly, established predictors outperform Jev in seven. Through region guidance, however, it finds configurations with lower perplexity than those selected by the native tuner, offering a quality gain when additional decision time and API fees are acceptable. These findings yield actionable recommendations on selecting the decision role, retaining conventional predictors, and balancing tuning quality against cost.

A Failure-Driven Data Flywheel for Post-Training Vision-Language-Action Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models rarely satisfy all behavioral requirements of a new platform, and teams without a large deployed fleet lack the data to address the resulting failures. We propose a failure-driven data flywheel for post-training such models. It constructs target scenarios absent from the available data by inserting task-relevant entities into reconstructed real-world scenes, and evaluates the model against each behavioral requirement of the target task. Failure rates form a failure map across task conditions and difficulty levels, which is re-evaluated after each round and assigns larger data generation quotas to conditions where the model still fails. We instantiate the framework in autonomous driving for occluded-pedestrian emergence and yielding at stop-sign-controlled intersections, and evaluate it on two VLA models and one non-VLA planner in simulation and on a real vehicle. For occluded-pedestrian emergence, five rounds of post-training improve the critical-phase collision-free rate on the sealed test set from 0.261 to 0.579 for AutoVLA and from 0.390 to 0.765 for ReCogDrive, while the unnecessary braking rate in paired control scenarios never exceeds 0.013. On a full-scale vehicle without real-world training, post-trained ReCogDrive achieves 4 of 5 safe passages, versus 1 of 5 for the base model. Fine-grained evaluation reveals a pattern obscured by the collision-free rate alone: both VLA models learn anticipatory deceleration before reaching the occluding vehicle, while braking after pedestrian emergence remains limited. For stop-sign yielding, the stop-before-line rate rises from 0.100 to 0.897 for AutoVLA and from 0.000 to 0.667 for ReCogDrive. With sequential training from the pedestrian-capability models, it reaches 0.599 and 0.358, respectively, while the pedestrian collision-free rate decreases by at most 0.032.

Do We Really Need an LLM for Weight-Space Programming? A Deterministic Forward-Only Paradigm and Its Capability Boundaries

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recent work on Weight-Space Programming uses LLMs to edit neural weights with forward passes only, conflating LLM scripting with the underlying algebra of forward-only editing. We show that for core structured editing regimes, an LLM optimizer is unnecessary, and introduce Forward Weight-Space Programming (FWSP): a deterministic pipeline combining probing, localization, stratified operators (O1-O4), automatic blind routing (83.3% vs. 33.3% for a 27B LLM with the same tool library), and paired verification with zero additional per-token computation. For reasoning LLMs, we propose Protected FisherGate, a closed-form covariance-whitened readout edit orthogonal to normal-reasoning states: on DeepSeek-R1-0528-8B, a single frozen math-calibrated patch achieves 84.2% HumanEval pass@1 (1K budget; 75.4 +/- 9.4% across 3/5 Stage-4-passing subsets), beating 512-token-reserved budget forcing by +14.6 pp (-23.4% tokens) while transferring across HF-to-vLLM backends on full GSM8K and MATH-500. Across five modalities and thirteen models (50K to 27B dense and FP8 MoE), we further prove and verify two operator boundaries, subspace entanglement and frozen representation span, where FWSP-localized LoRA bridges forward-only and gradient adaptation.

LSRP*-LA: Scalable and Anytime Multi-Agent Path Finding for Large Agents with Asynchronous Actions

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multi-Agent Path Finding (MAPF) seeks collision-free paths for multiple agents from their start locations to their goal locations while minimizing path costs. Most MAPF algorithms assume that each agent occupies a single vertex and that the actions of all agents are synchronized. These assumptions may limit the use of MAPF planners in practice. Existing planners that get rid of both assumptions either handle only a few agents or cannot improve their solutions. Recently, a planner called LSRP was proposed, which is scalable and anytime with asynchronous actions, but still limited by that each agent occupies a single vertex. This paper develops a new planner called LSRP-LA for MAPF for large agents with asynchronous actions (LA-MAPF-AA), where an agent can occupy several cells. LSRP-LA is scalable and complete, and it keeps improving solution quality after the initial solution is obtained until the runtime budget depletes. We analyze the properties of LSRP-LA and test it against several baselines with up to 800 agents on various maps. LSRP*-LA can handle twice as many agents as baseline methods and its solution cost is about half that of recent synchronous planners for large agents.

Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent: Self-Structuring Learning through Question Answering

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We consider the problem of constructing knowledge bases for applications based on large language models, in which the structure of a knowledge base determines how an application can retrieve and connect the information needed to answer a question. We study Self-Structuring Learning, a setting in which questions and expected answers guide the construction of a persistent knowledge base whose editable structure can be a graph schema or the organization of linked knowledge documents. We propose Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent, which diagnoses discrepancies between expected answers and responses obtained through the current knowledge base. At each round, the method proposes a structural edit, rebuilds the knowledge base, and adopts the edit when the learning loss decreases. The retained structure is evaluated on other questions about the same sources. By randomizing over candidate edits, we express the loss change of an edit as a derivative with respect to the probability of selecting it, and we evaluate joint edits as single candidates because their changes interact. Schema Descent instantiates the method for graph schemas. In experiments on large documents, each a bundle of research papers, the updated knowledge cards and graph schemas obtain higher mean Answer F1 on held-out generated questions than their initial structures, with schema gains in every document. On human questions, mean F1 rises for schemas and falls for cards: the updated structures serve the kind of question they were learned from.

ZeroHero: Training Small Language Agents from Scratch

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large pretrained language models provide capable policies for interactive tasks, but how much model capacity is necessary for a specialized language agent? We introduce ZeroHero, a framework for training small language agents (SLAs) from scratch. In each environment, a large language model acts as the expert, and we collect its successful trajectories. We pretrain each SLA, which has fewer than 50 million parameters, to predict the expert's responses given the interaction history. We then post-train the agent on its own interactions using proximal policy optimization (PPO). During post-training, reasoning alignment supplements environment rewards with expert supervision on the language the agent generates alongside its actions. We evaluate the agents in three multistep environments where acting requires both understanding and generating language. In Crafter, agents interpret textual observations and generate reasoning before acting. In WebShop, they translate shopping instructions into search queries. In Alfworld-Dialogue, they ask clarifying questions and use the answers to guide household actions. The agents approach expert performance on WebShop and surpass their respective demonstration experts on Crafter training rollouts and held-out Alfworld-Dialogue tasks. An interactive demo is available at https://zoyav.github.io/-ZoyaV-zerohero-demo.

EVA: Evidence-seeking Visual Agent for Hallucination-Resistant Multimodal Reasoning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often arise from visual assumptions that go unchecked. Self-reflection, multi-agent debate, and counterfactual verification can reinforce erroneous conclusions when they rely on the same unverified visual assumptions. External visual tools can provide verifiable evidence, but models may select the wrong tools, supply incorrect parameters, or ignore or contradict the evidence they obtain. In this work, we propose EVA, an Evidence-seeking Visual Agent that reframes multimodal reasoning as an explicit process of evidence acquisition and verification. At the core of EVA is a unified evidence-grounded execution pipeline designed to address the above challenges through two tightly coupled mechanisms. First, we introduce Iterative Tool Replanning, which dynamically refines tool selection and parameters based on intermediate observations, improving the reliability of tool usage. Second, we develop Evidence Consistency Verification, an answer validation mechanism that improves alignment between the final prediction and the collected evidence, mitigating evidence--reasoning inconsistencies. In addition, to balance robustness and efficiency, EVA incorporates Selective Evidence Routing, an adaptive fast--slow reasoning strategy that determines when external evidence is necessary. Visually straightforward queries are handled via direct reasoning, while complex or high-risk cases trigger tool-based evidence acquisition. By jointly addressing how to reliably acquire evidence and how to use it consistently, EVA transforms multimodal reasoning from passive answer validation into explicit evidence-backed decision making. Experimental results demonstrate that EVA achieves significantly improved reasoning reliability over direct VLM inference and multi-agent methods, while maintaining efficiency through selective tool invocation.

Interactive Proofs as a Unified Framework for Evaluating and Leveraging Verifiable Proof Generation by LLM Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recent advances in LLM-assisted theorem proving and program verification have attracted widespread attention. Beyond determining whether a proposition is true, LLM agents must produce evidence that can be independently checked by humans or formal verification tools. To evaluate the underlying, protocol-agnostic ability of LLM agents to construct verifiable proofs-rather than their proficiency with any particular proof language or verification tool, we introduce Merlin-Bench. Drawing on interactive proof systems from computational complexity theory, we cast an LLM agent as the prover: under a designated protocol, the agent interacts with a formal verifier to substantiate its claimed answer to a problem instance. We develop an automated framework for constructing test cases and use it to create 100 cases across 15 problem families, each formulated within this prover-verifier framework. Experiments with 10 frontier LLMs across multiple agent harnesses show that reliably producing verifiable proofs in accordance with specified protocols remains a substantial challenge. Our case studies further provide actionable insights into improving agents' ability to generate verifier-accepted proofs: tool use is essential for completing practical proof tasks; different forms of guidance, ranging from engineering instructions to problem-specific knowledge, substantially affect success rates on interactive-proof tasks; and proof-solving experience distilled from abstract tasks can transfer to concrete, real-world verification tasks.

Semantic Density Does Not Predict Agent-Memory Collapse

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Persistent textual memory can improve LLM agents, but repeated updates can erode task performance. We ask whether memory-store statistics warn before held-out accuracy declines. We registered mean pairwise cosine similarity ($ρ$) and 13 additional geometry, retrieval, and graph statistics on 805 snapshots from 13 memory methods across ALFWorld, AppWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, and two ARC-AGI settings. These methods store trajectories, rules, workflows, strategies, or linked records, using selective retrieval or whole-store prompting. No such statistic reliably predicted early signs of task accuracy decline, and $ρ$ had zero median lead. With the checkpoint after peak accuracy as a secondary decline point, measures of highly similar pairs and nearest-neighbor crowding changed earlier but did not consistently outperform stored-item count. We trained classifiers on this corpus and applied them unchanged to PropWorld runs of up to 5,000 crafting tasks. They falsely flagged 56.9% of checkpoints from size-limited stores with stable accuracy and detected only 18% of checkpoints after task success fell to zero. Thus, global semantic density did not provide a reliable warning, while local crowding did not transfer consistently across memory designs. In these long-horizon runs, size-limited stores remained stable, while the sole failure arose when unbounded whole-store prompting exceeded model input capacity, which token growth predicted directly.

Aegis-VLA: Reliability-Gated Action Refinement via a Trajectory World Model for Dynamic Object Manipulation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Dynamic object manipulation requires maintaining spatial and temporal alignment with targets that continue to move during inference and execution. Although future prediction can inform action correction, an accurate forecast does not establish whether modifying an existing action would be beneficial. We present Aegis-VLA, a world-model-guided vision-language-action (VLA) framework that generates predictive action corrections and separately determines whether and how strongly to apply them. A Trajectory World Model combines kinematic extrapolation with learned residual dynamics to predict object motion and estimate positional uncertainty. Discrepancy-Guided Action Refinement uses differences between history-only and base-action-conditioned end-effector forecasts to propose bounded arm corrections. A Reliability Gate learns when to admit corrections based on demonstration-relative improvements over base actions, and regulates their application during inference using predictive uncertainty and anticipated object--end-effector proximity. Rejected corrections preserve the corresponding base actions. Aegis-VLA introduces only approximately 2.45M trainable parameters. On the simulated Dynamic Object Manipulation (DOM) benchmark, Aegis-VLA achieves 56.72\% success over 1,800 evaluation trials, exceeding our reproduced DynamicVLA baseline of 45.44\% by 11.28 percentage points.

LatentRL-QAS: Transfer via Latent Reinforcement Learning for Quantum Architecture Search

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reinforcement learning (RL) emerges as a prominent approach to quantum architecture search (QAS), the automated design of quantum circuits. However, transferring what an agent has learned from one number of qubits to another is still weakly explored: the state, the action space and the neural network of a state-of-the-art RL-QAS agent all grow with the number of qubits, which makes transfer learning across qubit counts prohibitive. We make this transfer possible by introducing LatentRL-QAS, a latent space transfer and refresh framework for RL-based QAS. At its core, a circuit variational autoencoder encodes a circuit as one token per gate and maps circuits on any number of qubits into the same latent space. An RL agent navigates this space instead of adding gates, so its state, actions and network have the same size at every qubit count. The latent space is periodically refreshed with the agent's new discoveries and, for a larger system, grown from the solutions of the smaller one, so what is transferred is the representation of circuits, not network weights or past experience. To show transfer, we consider absolutely maximally entangled (AME) states, a resource for quantum error correction. LatentRL-QAS discovers the five- and six-qubit AME states without being shown them and prepares them in 89-99% of evaluation episodes, whereas the baseline RL-QAS fails to find them with the same budget, even with a network 10 to 17 times larger. LatentRL-QAS remains robust under depolarizing noise with single-qubit gate errors amplified 10x and two-qubit gate errors 5x compared with IBM quantum hardware. By decoupling the RL agent from the number of qubits, LatentRL-QAS lets quantum architecture search reuse circuit representations learned on smaller problems, providing a foundation for the transferable design of circuits across quantum optimization problems.

When Skills Fail to Compose: Compatibility and Retention in Language Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Skill acquisition alone does not establish a reusable skill library: task proficiency does not specify a compositional interface. Motivated by agents that acquire and refine skills, we study independently learned soft-prompt skills on frozen language models, with skill identities and execution order supplied. Across four digit-task bases and three record-editing models, mean composition accuracy in one call is at most 7.6%, compared with 88.2% to 100% over two calls that add computation and an explicit intermediate output. Neutral-insertion training improves robustness but leaves substantial interference, which block length and per-token norm alone cannot explain on two of four digit bases. Blocks of the same operation need not be interchangeable: after training next to frozen learned partners, a post hoc test on two Qwen bases shows that replacing those partners with independently learned blocks of the same operations lowers median composition accuracy from 98.0% and 95.3% to 12.7% and 0.5%, although every replacement scores at least 90% alone. Repair can improve a composition while impairing the original skill, and rehearsal improves retention while transfer remains limited on Qwen2.5-0.5B. Internal interventions constrain explanations without identifying a shared failure mechanism. These findings motivate evaluating compatibility, retention and transfer alongside acquisition when building skill libraries for self-evolving agents. Code is available at https://github.com/YanLin-Quinne/SkillCompatibility.

CHASE: Curiosity-Guided Skill Acquisition for Embodied Task Execution

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Robots executing tasks in partially observed environments must resolve uncertainty while operating with incomplete skills. Inspired by human curiosity, we investigate how robots can formulate task-relevant hypotheses, seek evidence, and acquire missing skills during task execution. We propose CHASE, a Curiosity-guided Harness for Acquiring Skills from Evidence, which operationalizes curiosity as selecting investigations that resolve task uncertainty and expand executable capabilities. Four modules connect dynamic scene understanding, hypothesis-driven planning, on-demand skill acquisition, and verified execution. The planner balances information value, skill reuse, and acquisition/execution cost. Missing skills trigger bounded exploration, learning from verified segments, and held-out admission during acquisition; separate checks verify physical effects and acquired evidence. Newly admitted skills expand the executable investigation set, closing the loop between resolving uncertainty and acquiring capabilities. Simulation and real-robot experiments evaluate investigation, skill acquisition, and task execution. CHASE achieves 50-80% task success under combined information and capability gaps, with 70-90% evidence resolution.

MINE: A Training-Free Defense against Indirect Prompt Injection via Mutual Information and Embedding Variance

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Existing defenses against indirect prompt injection in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) are inherently reactive: prompt-engineering methods are often overridden by the injected instructions, while supervised detectors must be retrained for each new attack style. We propose a different paradigm: defend by maximizing relevance, not by detecting attacks. Our method, MINE, is training-free and attack-agnostic, and it also serves as a query-conditioned reranker. To evaluate defenses under realistic conditions, we introduce LC-Inj-SQuAD, a long-context benchmark with an average of 4,032 words per context. We evaluate on four datasets spanning short-context QA (Inj-SQuAD, Inj-TriviaQA), long-context QA (LC-Inj-SQuAD), and agent tool-call injection (InjecAgent). Across all four, MINE achieves the best accuracy--security trade-off among training-free defenses. It is also the only defense that stays usable against a fully adaptive attacker, generalizes to agent tool-call injection, and achieves the lowest inference latency on long contexts.

Long-Horizon Planning with Latent World Models via Graph Anchors

📅 2026-11-25 14:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Latent world models plan in a compact latent space by predicting the outcomes of actions. Their planners judge a plan by the latent distance between its
predicted end state and the goal image. We show that this distance fails in two distinct ways. First, it loses directional signal a few steps from the
goal, causing distant goals to fail. Second, it weighs the robot arm over the manipulated object, causing manipulation to fail at contact. We measure
both failures directly on recorded data by asking whether a lower latent distance corresponds to true progress toward the goal. Across five
environments, six visual representations (including raw pixels and V-JEPA 2), and nine Meta-World tasks, this ranking test cleanly separates the two
failures, and the graph distance to a goal predicts where closed-loop planning breaks down. Both failures share a single remedy: replacing the distant
goal with a nearby target that lies on a recorded trajectory. We realize this principle with Graph Anchors, a training-free planner for frozen latent
world models. It builds a sparse graph once over the model's own training frames and aims each planning step at a node a few moves ahead along the
shortest path to the goal, keeping paths on recorded transitions wherever contact matters. On goals up to 400 steps away, Graph Anchors raise LeWM's
success from 13% to 85% on PointMaze-Medium, from 8% to 87% on PointMaze-Large, and from 5% to 71% on Push-T, where stronger search toward the goal
fails and where prior training-free planners break down under missing step horizons, subsampling shortcuts, or fragmented contact.

Long-Horizon Planning with Latent World Models via Graph Anchors

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Latent world models plan in a compact latent space by predicting the outcomes of actions. Their planners judge a plan by the latent distance between its predicted end state and the goal image. It builds a sparse graph once over the model's own training frames and aims each planning step at a node a few moves ahead along the shortest path to the goal, keeping paths on recorded transitions wherever contact matters. On goals up to 400 steps away, Graph Anchors raise LeWM's success from 13% to 85% on PointMaze-Medium, from 8% to 87% on PointMaze-Large, and from 5% to 71% on Push-T, where stronger search toward the goal fails and where prior training-free planners break down under missing step horizons, subsampling shortcuts, or fragmented contact.

Fine-Tuning Without Gradients? Closed-Form, LLM-Written Classifiers on a Frozen Encoder

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Can a classifier computed in closed form on a frozen encoder match gradient fine-tuning? We present closed-form weight-space programming (WSP): a large language model (LLM), used as a design assistant, writes programs that read a frozen masked language model through cloze-style prompt templates at several layers, fit closed-form heads (shrinkage discriminant analysis, kernel ridge regression and the encoder's label-word prior) on labelled data, and select among a pool of candidate heads by cross-fitting. No gradient is computed and no encoder weight changes. We instantiate the framework on RoBERTa-large. On 11 development datasets with 512 to 8,192 training labels, WSP is ahead of full fine-tuning (FT) and LoRA at every label budget (by 0.3 to 0.6 points on average; the registered non-inferiority tests pass) and within 0.5 points of prompt-based fine-tuning (PFT); these datasets also shaped the recipe. With fewer labels its lead over FT and LoRA grows: 1.2 to 3.5 points with 64 and 128 labels, and 3.8 to 5.3 points in the few-shot setting of 16 labels per class, where WSP is again within half a point of PFT on the development datasets. On six held-out datasets, WSP stays ahead of LoRA and within half a point of FT on average, although with six datasets no registered test passes, and PFT is ahead, by 1.30 points with 8,192 labels. WSP never collapses and is deterministic, but it needs one encoder pass per template (17 here). We report all eight recipe versions, every decision that saw test results, and 27 ideas that did not help.

Strategic and Robust Task Allocation for Combat Objectives in Adversarial Multi-Agent Environments

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Task allocation in contested-objective team games requires coordinated decisions under uncertainty, spatial constraints, and changing tactical conditions. In such settings, multiple agents must anticipate both teammate behavior and opponent actions. We study a hybrid predict-then-optimize approach for assigning controllable agents to objectives in real time. A masked multi-head model predicts tactical signals for each agent, including survival chance, local superiority, combat interaction, travel time, and objective contribution. These signals are combined with rule-based terms in a score matrix, and a minimum-cost flow solver computes coordinated global assignments constrained by capacity, occupancy, stability, and defensive strategies. Learning is limited to tactical scoring, while explicit rules and solver structure preserve controllability and interpretability. We evaluate the system using predictive metrics, offline assignment diagnostics, online A/B evaluation, and latency measurements. The system is an R\&D research prototype built on a commercial game and is not deployed in the shipped game. The results indicate that learned tactical estimates can support stable, feasible, and inspectable objective allocation under strict real-time constraints.

Faster and Better Query (FBQ): Learning Future-Position Probes from the First Generated Token

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

When a large language model (LLM) evicts prompt keys and values at the end of prefill, it must decide what to keep before it sees the queries from subsequent decoding steps. Generating a draft continuation can approximate those queries at the cost of extra autoregressive steps. Can we use the embedding of the first output token, already available from the final prefill logits, to construct low-cost pseudo queries that approximate future decoding queries? We start with FTQ-8, which places eight copies of the first token ID at consecutive future positions and reads their attention to the prompt in one causal forward pass. Each copy sees a different prefix, but a generic opening such as the offers a poor literal guess for seven successive tokens. Faster and Better Query (FBQ) preserves the first embedding and learns an additive offset at each of the seven remaining positions. We train these offsets to predict the average prompt attention of a full-cache continuation. On LongBench, FBQ performs comparably to eight-step Lookahead Q-Cache at the same cache budget, using a single forward pass to compute all probe queries.

Find the Clue, Not the Scene: Rethinking Reasoning for Entity Recognition in Vision-Language Models

📅 2026-11-25 16:44
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Answering a knowledge-based visual question requires first recognizing the entity in the image and then recalling knowledge about it. Thinking vision-language models (VLMs) reason for thousands of tokens before answering, yet it remains underexplored how well such thinking VLMs perform each of these two steps. We therefore analyze thinking VLMs on this task and make two key observations. (1) Recognition is the bottleneck, since 76.3% of errors across four models on ViQuAE stem from misrecognizing the entity, and 84.9% of these failures are resolved once the entity name is provided in the question. (2) Recognition is driven by entity-recall clues rather than by general descriptions of the image. An entity-recall clue is a distinctive visual detail that helps the model recall the entity, such as keys, a traditional attribute of Saint Peter. Based on these findings, we propose ClueRL, a reinforcement learning approach that extends Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with an advantage localized to the entity-recall clue in the thinking trace. Rather than injecting new entity knowledge, ClueRL changes how VLMs think: it leads them to attend more closely to visual details and spot these clues, helping them use the knowledge they already have. Consequently, it outperforms both GRPO and a perception-reward baseline on three entity recognition benchmarks. Moreover, this clue-seeking behavior generalizes beyond entity recognition, the only task used for training. Since recognition is the bottleneck, the gains carry over to knowledge-based visual question answering. They even extend to general multimodal reasoning, raising Qwen3.5-4B by 4.4 and 4.0 points on the ScienceQA and MMMU subsets of MMStar, respectively.

TASK Memory: Beyond Text-Only Memory with Activation Steering and Knowledge Editing against Memory Hallucination

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM agents increasingly rely on external memory, yet they still hallucinate about what they remember. Often the correct memory is retrieved and placed in the prompt, but the answer contradicts it or the memory is applied to the wrong question. We call these use-time memory hallucinations. Across five open models, we find that they arise from the conflict between parametric knowledge and external memory and take three forms: memory-ignoring, where the model returns to a stale fact in its weights (up to 46% of queries); memory-misapplying, where it attributes the memory to unrelated entities (up to 24%, and 49% when retrieval is combined with weight editing); and version confusion, where 53% of answers about revised facts assert the superseded value when both versions remain stored. We propose TASK memory, which goes beyond text-only memory by combining a Text note with Activation Steering and Knowledge editing that share one in-model address. The text holds the content. A removable knowledge edit supplies the address through its subject key and supports multi-hop composition. A pair of steering vectors decides, query by query, whether the model adopts the memory or keeps its own answer; the vectors store no content and control how the text memory is used. On frozen models under a pre-registered, test-once protocol, TASK reduces memory-ignoring hallucinations to at most 1.5% in all ten model-dataset cells and cuts neighbour misuse from 39.2% to 7.5% (+10.8 pp composite on CounterFact). It improves a 300-memory library over BM25 RAG by 16.4 pp, an advantage that holds at 3,000 memories, resolves fact revisions without fact IDs, and adds 7.5 pp of multi-hop accuracy on MQuAKE. 21 of 23 cross-model comparisons (8B-27B, four families) are significant, and TASK beats three recent context-steering methods in 9 of 10 model-dataset cells.

Solve Vision with Code: Coding Agents Are Stronger Visual Reasoners Than Video Models

📅 2026-11-25 18:01
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Video generation models are increasingly evaluated as visual reasoners: given a first frame and a natural-language prompt, can the model produce the video in which the specified task is carried out? The paradigm is considered advantageous because the reasoning is native to the visual medium, unfolding directly in the frames rather than through language or code. Whether this native route is in fact the more reliable path to visual reasoning has not been tested against a symbolic alternative under the same conditions. We therefore pose the same tasks, with identical inputs, output specification and evaluator, to both media: rather than denoising pixels, a coding agent writes a program that renders the answer video, which an unmodified rule-based evaluator scores. On VBVR-Pro-Bench (Xu et al., 2026), a 500-instance suite spanning five cognitive categories with symbolic ground truth, the agent receives the same first frame and prompt as the video model, with no access to ground truth, metadata or generation models, and one attempt per instance. Three closed-model agents (Codex with gpt-6-astra, Claude Code with Fable 5.1 and Gemini CLI with Gemini 3.1 Pro) score 0.923, 0.894 and 0.893, each more than 0.22 above the strongest video model. Among 23 runnable open-weight models, the best, GLM-5, reaches 0.572, above all video models except the RL-trained one. Together, we show that for tasks whose solutions can be computed from a first frame and a prompt, program synthesis is currently a substantially more reliable route to visual reasoning than video generation. We further distinguish depictive programs, which navigate the image to recover a transferable scheme, from propositional programs, which operate on hard-coded values, revealing how visual reasoning unfolds in agent-written programs and what it implies for its nature of reasoning in general.

The Detour Is the Danger: Preserving Security During LLM Agent Recovery

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM agents increasingly rely on external tools and reusable Skills to execute complex workflows, autonomously replanning when operations fail. However, such recovery may preserve task functionality while silently discarding the security protections required by the original workflow. In this paper, we investigate workflow security downgrade, a failure-induced security threat in which agents execute sensitive operations without satisfying their original protection requirements. Unlike prompt injection, this threat exploits autonomous recovery decisions triggered by execution failures, without modifying instructions or tool definitions. We systematically evaluate this behavior through fault injection in an executable MCP/Skill environment spanning seven LLMs, twelve task templates, and three security-sensitive domains. To address this threat, we propose ARGUS, an obligation-preserving recovery framework that combines resource-bound security evidence, isolated recovery-plan validation, and execution-time enforcement. In a separate 864-run evaluation, ARGUS prevents all observed protection-loss events under enforcement, while recovery assistance yields model-dependent improvements in protected task completion over blocking alone. Our findings demonstrate that secure agent recovery requires preserving security obligations across workflow changes, rather than merely restoring task functionality.

SEAM: A Self-Evolving and Adaptive Probing Framework for Automatic MLLM Hallucination Evaluation

📅 2026-11-25 15:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The severe susceptibility of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to hallucinations poses a critical threat to their reliability, making a rigorous evaluation framework indispensable. However, existing evaluation pipelines largely rely on predefined test items, suffering from high construction costs, rapid obsolescence as MLLMs evolve, and ineffectiveness in probing model-specific weaknesses. To address these limitations, we propose SEAM, a self-evolving and adaptive probing framework for automatic MLLM hallucination evaluation. Specifically, SEAM introduces a composable Hallucination Probe Construction Library comprising ten hallucination-probing skills, three elicitation wrappers, and five question formats. Building on this library, SEAM establishes a dual-loop framework. In the inner loop (adaptive probing), skill-guided visual analysis, question composition, and hierarchical quality verification are coordinated with verifier and target-response feedback to refine test items and uncover model-specific hallucinations. In the outer loop (skill evolution), a skill updater distills historical probing trajectories into localized, reusable rule updates, which undergo held-out validation and are frozen before testing. Driven by these innovative structural designs, SEAM establishes an automated and self-evolving evaluation paradigm. Experiments across four target MLLMs and ten hallucination types demonstrate that SEAM substantially outperforms representative static benchmarks, achieving an 89.2\% relative improvement in mean valid hallucination discovery rate across 12 matched comparisons.

REFORGE: LLM-Assisted RTL Bug Repair with Structured Context and Verification Feedback

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) can write and repair RTL, but successful compilation or testbench execution does not establish defect removal, behavioral preservation, security, or implementation feasibility. Existing LLM-based RTL repair methods often report a single repair rate without retaining separate evidence for these properties, limiting reproducible comparison and integration into hardware design flows. We present REFORGE, which, to our knowledge, is the first LLM-assisted RTL repair framework that maintains and accumulates structured repair evidence across iterations, allowing it to generate fixes that satisfy multiple objectives simultaneously, including functionality, behavioral equivalence, security, and cost efficiency. REFORGE profiles buggy RTL, identifies structural and security-relevant features, localizes failing behavior, and uses verification-guided diagnosis to generate complete RTL repair variants. Each variant undergoes syntax checking, simulation, formal analysis, off-trigger preservation, security re-scan, and synthesis-based power, performance, and area analysis. We implement REFORGE as a backend-independent repair-and-verification flow and evaluate it in 288 runs across three LLM backends and three benchmark suites. On CirFix, the proposed context and diagnosis increase runs delivering a checked repair from 32/63 to 51/63, while every configuration repairs the Hack@DAC OpenTitan defect. Evidence records also show that a testbench-only criterion reports 3.9 times more repairs than the full set of applicable checks, security-clean outputs may lack functional verification, and different RTL repairs can implement equivalent hardware. These results show that LLM-generated RTL repairs require explicit multi-stage verification evidence rather than a single pass/fail outcome.

Extraneous Cognitive Load in Large Language Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Cognitive Load Theory (CLT) shows that human problem-solving is affected by both intrinsic load, arising from task difficulty, and extraneous load, arising from how task information is presented. Large language models (LLMs) show analogous effects: presentation alone can affect model performance, but these effects have not yet been systematically isolated from task difficulty. To address this gap, we introduce CogLoadBench, a controlled benchmark that varies five CLT-inspired presentation factors across three reasoning task families. Across models, we find that higher extraneous load conditions often reduce accuracy by either adding competition or complicating the reasoning path. To support diagnosis and mitigation of load-related effects, we propose the Extraneous Load Score (ELS), a prompt-time metric computed from activations that estimates a model's extraneous load, and show that it generalizes across reasoning task types and load levels. We further show that shifting model representations toward lower-load directions at inference time can improve average model performance without changing prompt content.

Certify What You Plant: Shortcut-Free Proof Depth in Logical Reasoning Data

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Procedurally generated logic problems are used to both evaluate and train reasoning in language models. Their difficulty is usually set by planting a proof, that is, by sampling a proof of the desired depth and stating its premises as the problem. However, a planted proof is only one possible proof. A problem can also have shorter proofs, or several unrelated ones, and its stated depth then overstates what solving it requires. We measure what a problem actually requires, relative to a stated set of inference rules. For each problem, we compute the steps every proof must make, the shortest proof, the number of minimal proofs and the indispensable premises, and export certificates that an independent checker verifies. Audits of eight datasets find depth that is forced (ProofWriter, Blocksworld), problems labelled depth 5 that require about two specific rule applications (FLDx2), and answers that need no particular step (ZebraLogic). We then construct problems whose every proof has a requested structure, using a catalog of shortcuts proved once per rule set. Every constructed problem is checked in full and comes with its own certificate. In training, the best data mixes depths and problem types. At equal size, such mixtures of certified problem types, including model-checking problems that forward generation does not produce, transfer better than FLDx2-style derivation data for Qwen3 models from 0.6B to 8B.

Partial Routing: Trace-Tuned Delegation for Resource-Efficient Agentic Workflows

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI models vary enormously in capability and price. Model routing exploits this by sending easier tasks to cheaper models. However, a single agentic task often combines easy and difficult subtasks, making one model choice for the entire task inefficient. We introduce partial routing, which guides a frontier lead model to delegate suitable subtasks to a cheaper worker model. From both models' past runs, we identify recurring subtasks and measure each model's quality and cost on them. These measurements produce delegation guidance, with a parameter, $δ$, controlling how much is delegated. The lead decides when and what to delegate during execution, without training either model. On held-out tasks from ViBench and Terminal-Bench 4.0, partial routing achieves higher mean task performance than the lead alone and task-level routing. With $δ= 0.75$, it raises the lead's ViBench score from 70.2 to 78.5 at 19% lower cost, and its Terminal-Bench pass rate from 54% to 61% at similar cost. Giving the lead only the subtask breakdown, without a worker, also reduces cost on both benchmarks and raises the Terminal-Bench pass rate. These results show that analysis of past runs can support more efficient planning and more effective delegation, with little cost and implementation overhead.

MetaDecomp: Adaptive Task Decomposition via Meta-Learned Procedural Memory

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reusing past experience in long-horizon tasks requires more than storing successful procedures: Language agents must distinguish which procedures apply and what work remains as execution progresses. We introduce MetaDecomp, an in-context meta-learning framework for adaptive task decomposition that jointly learns a task-relative state space and reusable procedures. Its outer loop builds procedural memory from successful training executions, representing task progress as abstract states and procedures between states as directed acyclic graphs of operations and dependencies. As new progress distinctions are learned, MetaDecomp revisits historical execution evidence to reassess state assignments and reorganize the procedures they support. With memory fixed at test time, the inner loop refines an initial decomposition and revises it from environmental feedback, combining applicable procedure fragments with task-specific steps missing from memory. Evaluated on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld over three seeds, MetaDecomp achieves the highest mean performance among the compared methods in four of five settings, for example, an 86.3% success rate on ALFWorld with Qwen3-4B. Fixing the initial schema or omitting historical reprojection reduces performance in component ablations. MetaDecomp also uses fewer inference tokens than ReAct and AutoRefine in all five settings. Cross-benchmark analysis identifies a low-performance area where partial coverage degrades success relative to hidden memory; high coverage improves success, while low coverage changes little. Code will be released upon acceptance.

VulnSafe: Measuring Vulnerability-Aware Relational Safety in Large Language Models

📅 2026-11-25 18:03
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Emotionally supportive interactions require large language models not only to avoid overtly harmful content, but also to recognize vulnerability-related risk, regulate their relational stance, preserve user agency, and provide context-appropriate support. Existing safety benchmarks often measure isolated violations, leaving unclear what underlying response competence their scores represent. We introduce VulnSafe, a Chinese capability assessment centered on Vulnerability-Aware Response Competence (VARC). VARC organizes relational safety into five complementary constructs: Risk Perception, Safety Preservation, Autonomy Protection, Supportive Response, and Boundary Management. Following an evidence-centered design, 41 scenarios elicit construct-relevant behavior through 2,050 balanced prompts and scenario-specific scoring rubrics. The assessment further separates proactive should-do behavior from prohibitive should-not behavior. Across eight representative LLMs, results show distinct competence profiles and larger disparities than prohibitive requirements. VulnSafe therefore makes relational safety interpretable as a multidimensional model capability rather than as refusal compliance alone.

Most of the Way There: Turning a Strong Video Prior into an Action-Conditioned World Model

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Modern video generators excel at producing visually plausible futures guided by text and image prompts. Yet their emphasis on semantic plausibility does not guarantee fidelity to the physical consequences of specific actions. Action-conditioned world models (ACWMs), in contrast, aim to simulate how the world responds to robot actions, enabling scalable policy evaluation and improvement without costly real-world rollouts. The challenge is not merely to generate a plausible future, but to predict what follows from a specific physical action. As video priors grow stronger, we ask how little adaptation is truly needed to bridge this gap. We introduce Dianjing, a minimal recipe that preserves and unlocks the capabilities of strong video priors through unified physical action conditioning, training concentrated at the high noise levels where motion and layout, and thus the consequences of actions, are decided, and native history conditioning. Built upon MiniMax H3, a 33B video foundation model, Dianjing freezes the pretrained backbone and trains only 0.34% of its parameters using approximately 65 hours of robot video across two embodiments, without additional robot-specific pretraining or camera calibration. Controlled ablations show that where training is allocated across noise levels matters more than how actions enter the network. On WorldOracle, which evaluates predictions against paired real-world executions, Dianjing achieves the best scores on four of the five evaluation tracks and ranks second on AgiBot-Long. Our findings suggest that strong video priors already bring us most of the way to world modeling; lightweight physical action grounding can unlock their potential for real-world prediction.

Beyond Logits: Coherent Hallucination Mitigation via Attention Contrastive Decoding

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate impressive multimodal capabilities, yet suffer from hallucination - generating factually inaccurate content. Contrastive Decoding (CD) mitigates this by contrasting amateur and expert branches at the logit level. However, our investigation reveals that such logit-level interventions fundamentally compromise generation coherence, necessitating restrictive penalty constraints unrelated to hallucination suppression. We introduce Attention Contrastive Decoding (ACD), a training-free plug-in that complements logit-level CD by relocating part of the contrastive operations to the attention mechanism. Operating at an earlier stage of the forward pass, ACD performs smooth semantic-preserving interventions through an Adaptive Subtraction Strategy (ASS), which attenuates hallucination-associated attention patterns while amplifying critical visual information. Extensive experiments demonstrate that combining ACD with existing CD methods (e.g., VCD+ACD) produces substantially more coherent outputs with further reduced hallucinations, eliminating restrictive penalties while enabling trustworthy multimodal generation.

SOLVE: Sparse, Ordered Keyposes Make LLM Agents Versatile and Efficient Robot Manipulator

📅 2026-11-25 17:25
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM agents have made remarkable progress in robot manipulation, yet they suffer from expensive inference, dense step-by-step control, and tool bottlenecks. We introduce SOLVE, a training-free, versatile and efficient agent policy: a multimodal LLM agent writes a sparse, ordered sequence of 6-DoF end-effector keyposes per call, observes the result, and writes the next, while a motion planner interpolates between them. The agent decides the number and spacing of keyposes for both fast long-range motion and fine-grained manipulation. This granularity matches the sparsity of teleoperated manipulation: most demonstrations in DROID can be compressed into a few keyposes instead of dense actions. SOLVE grounds keyposes with sparse depth queries answered by noisy sources, such as multi-view triangulation, stereo, or monocular depth, and copes with that noise itself. It also improves from execution feedback, reflecting on its own rollouts and distilling a few rules per task. SOLVE reaches state-of-the-art performance of 74.5% on RoboDojo and 84.5% on RoboLab, with only $1.7 per episode on average, 12x less than previous dense-control agent policies. On a real robot, the same agent achieves an 88% success rate (75 of 85 episodes) over 16 challenging zero-shot tasks. Our project page is at https://serenos.github.io/solve-website.

Few-Shot Adaptation of Vision-Language Models via Agentic Model Editing

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Few-shot adaptation transfers pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to new domains using a small set of labeled images. Most methods fit prompts, representations, or classifiers within an adaptation procedure specified in advance. Yet the visual distinctions needed for recognition can differ substantially across domains, motivating the question: Can an LLM agent use few-shot examples and model observations to design the adaptation procedure itself? We propose Agentic Model Editing (AME), an agentic framework that jointly designs input transformations, encoder edits, feature computation, and classifier fitting as an executable, domain-customized adaptation program. The agent inspects images, class semantics, model parameters, intermediate representations, and activations to formulate hypotheses about the target task. It implements and tests candidate programs on the support set, with aggregate validation accuracy guiding refinement and selection. Each program fits a complete domain model that is fixed before testing. The agent and its tools are shared across domains, while each domain receives its own adaptation program. Across eleven datasets with CLIP ViT-B/16, AME achieves the highest average accuracies of 81.2% without back-propagation and 81.9% with back-propagation among the compared methods. Further experiments demonstrate applicability across VLM backbones and LLM agents.

Compact Vision-Language Navigation in Continuous Environmentsvia On-Policy Training and Route-Bearing Output

📅 2026-11-25 15:32
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Instruction-following robots need navigation policies that reach high accuracy and remain inexpensive to train and to run on a single graphics processing unit (GPU). The most accurate vision-language navigation policies for continuous environments rely on language models with 4 to 8 billion parameters, long observation histories, and large auxiliary corpora. Two properties of the standard training setup limit smaller policies. Imitation labels cover the states that an expert visits, whereas the running policy reaches states that its own errors produce. In addition, an image-plane waypoint exists only while the route lies inside the camera view. We present \textbf{\paper}, a monocular red-green-blue (RGB) policy with 2 billion (2B) parameters that addresses both limitations. \paper adapts Qwen3-VL-2B with low-rank adapters and processes an eight-frame memory and the current view in one forward pass. The policy predicts the route direction as a bearing in the body frame of the agent, which remains defined for every heading, and a deterministic controller converts this bearing into motion. This design allows training to relabel the states that the policy itself visits. On-policy correction improves success, while bearing decoding outperforms image-plane waypoint scanning across all evaluated start headings with identical weights. On the \valunseen split, \paper achieves \rFinSR\% success on Room-to-Room in Continuous Environments (\rtor), averaged over three seeds, and \rRxFinSR\% on its Room-Across-Room counterpart (\rxr). Inference requires \rDecMs\,ms and \rInferGB\,GiB of memory, while training takes \rCostTrainTyp\ GPU-hours on one 12\,GB consumer GPU.

From Prediction to Decision: Decision-Focused Fine-Tuning of Autoregressive Tabular Transformers for Cost-Sensitive Financial Events

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Autoregressive transformers trained on tokenized tabular event streams are a simple and strong baseline for predicting financial events such as fraud. Yet institutions do not act on probabilities; they act on decisions whose consequences are asymmetric and heteroscedastic: the cost of a missed high-value fraud and that of a wrongly declined small purchase are not the same. The standard bridge is post-hoc Bayes-optimal thresholding of calibrated probabilities under a cost matrix. We ask whether fine-tuning the model to the decision objective can do better. We fine-tune the same pretrained model so that its restricted softmax over label tokens (already a distribution over business actions) maximizes expected utility under an institution-specified, amount-scaled cost matrix. Because the reward is a known function of (action, outcome, amount) and the outcome is observed in the data, the expected-utility objective and its gradient are available in closed form. This is example-dependent cost-sensitive learning whose per-example loss is linear in the predicted probability, with no sampling, critics, or advantage estimation. A KL anchor to the pretrained model and an auxiliary language-modeling term preserve calibration and the generative event model. A sampled, group-normalized (GRPO-style) variant provides the extension point to trajectory-level rewards.

Repairing the Trustworthiness of Distilled Language Models

📅 2026-11-25 18:12
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Knowledge distillation enables the deployment of large language models through smaller student models, but the compression process can introduce regressions in trustworthiness even when downstream performance is preserved. Repairing such failures is challenging because the original distillation corpus is often unavailable, while direct preference optimization may alter capabilities inherited from the teacher. We propose a targeted repair framework based on the proximal Bregman response function (PBRF) that addresses both limitations. We estimate the curvature required for PBRF using a surrogate proxy dataset, removing dependence on the original transfer corpus, and extend the framework to preference-based supervision through an odds-ratio objective. This enables the use of preferred and dispreferred response pairs while constraining changes to the distilled model. Our results show that the proposed approach improves targeted trustworthiness behaviors for both pre-training and post-training models while largely preserving downstream performance, providing a practical alternative to re-distillation or unconstrained preference optimization. Our experiment results show that across different distilled models, the trustworthy regression can be improved by up to 8% with less than 1% decrement in perplexity.
