When the Knowledge Runs Out: A Part-Level Pilot Evaluation of How Language Models Handle Partially Covered Questions

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Assistants that answer from supplied reference material often receive material that covers some parts of a user's question and not others. Existing evaluations of insufficient context mostly score one answer per question, and often treat partially sufficient context as simply insufficient. We describe a small, open evaluation that scores each part of a three-part question separately, with a verdict for parts that are answered correctly from the material but carry an added unsupported claim. On 30 cases (five scenarios, six coverage patterns, five samples each) we test claude-haiku-4-5 and llama3.1:8b. Without an explicit instruction to stay within the material, both models fill most gaps from their own knowledge (unsupported-claim rates of 0.71 and 0.90). One added sentence instructing them to answer only from the material brings these rates to 0.00 and 0.06, consistent with prior work. What remains is less visible: answers that strengthen the material, for example turning "many job sites prohibit bots" into a flat "no". We also report on the judge. Three rounds of blind hand labelling show that this added-claim verdict is the hardest to apply, for the judge and the author alike; RAGAS faithfulness is unsuitable for this task because it penalises correct withholding; and a difference between the two models that is clear under one judge disappears when the judge model is replaced. The dataset, code, hand labels and per-run figures are public.

Commit2Drive: Perception-Grounded Language Decisions as Commitments for Trajectory Planning in Vision-Language-Action Driving Models

📅 2026-11-25 16:01
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models introduce language reasoning into end-to-end autonomous driving. They output a language decision together with a trajectory, which makes the planner more interpretable. However, in existing methods the language decision is either an independent output or an index that selects among similar candidate trajectories, and its annotations are not verified against the trajectory. As a result, the language decision and the trajectory are often inconsistent in closed-loop driving. To address this, we propose Commit2Drive, a framework that imposes the language decision as a consistency constraint on trajectory planning. The decision is a commitment, a maneuver class with a continuous parameter that one deterministic rule reads from any trajectory, and it is grounded in facts reported by the perception of the model itself. A Maneuver Bank maps the commitment to a code with a kinematic meaning, and consistency losses train the planner to execute every admissible decision of a scene, so that the trajectory follows the decision. An automatic data pipeline supplies the supervision. Decisions are labeled from the future motion of the expert, counterfactual executions train the decision and the trajectory together, and rule-based functions with a Vision-Language Model (VLM) generate, review and refine the language annotations. The code is a lookup that involves no hidden state of the language model, so Commit2Drive is extensible to rule-based decisions, which the same planner executes without retraining. Commit2Drive uses a 0.5B language model and runs closed-loop inference online on a single consumer graphics card. On Bench2Drive, Commit2Drive reaches a Driving Score (DS) of 78.50 and a Success Rate (SR) of 55.91% with a lightweight model, which is competitive with the state of the art, and greatly reduces the language-action inconsistency. Code will be released soon.

P-FedSkill: A Personalized Federated Skill Learning Framework under Task Heterogeneity

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Skill learning has become an important way to improve agent performance. However, agent trajectories often contain sensitive information and cannot be shared across clients, hindering collaborative skill learning. To tackle this issue, federated skill learning (FSL) has emerged as a solution by distilling trajectories into compact skill representations and uploading them to a central server for sharing. However, existing FSL methods typically share skills based on skill similarity without considering their contributions to heterogeneous tasks across clients, thereby degrading agent performance. To address this challenge, we propose P-FedSkill, a personalized FSL framework that comprises three key components: local skill extraction (LSE), personalized skill selection (PSS), and task-aware skill routing (TASR). Specifically, LSE first extracts skill patches (i.e., task applicability conditions and execution strategies) and task execution outcomes from trajectories. PSS then uses these outcomes to evaluate each skill patch's performance on client tasks and incorporates its task applicability condition to construct a personalized skill set for each client. Finally, TASR adaptively selects a task-relevant subset of skill patches based on their applicability conditions to guide agent execution. We further provide a theoretical guarantee for personalized skill selection in P-FedSkill, and extensive experiments also demonstrate its effectiveness.

PatternMiner: Automated Mathematical Discovery Through LLM-Guided Training and Interpretation of Small Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

There are several approaches to AI-driven mathematical discovery. One of these involves the use of large language models (LLMs) to resolve well-specified conjectures via text-based reasoning, while another uses narrow models and interpretability for more open-ended, human-in-the-loop exploration using object-based mathematical data. The latter is limited in its ability to move beyond simple empirical pattern detection while the former is not well-designed for direct, open-ended exploration of large mathematical datasets. In this paper we introduce PatternMiner, a system that combines these two paradigms. In PatternMiner, an LLM agent generates mathematical datasets based on an initial broad question, trains narrow machine learning models on them, uses interpretability techniques to extract patterns from these trained models, and uses these patterns to formulate conjectures. Once an initial conjecture has been formulated, PatternMiner instructs the agent to search for counterexamples using calls to mathematical software. If a set of counterexamples can be found, these are used as feedback for conjecture refinement or another round of training and interpretability. The conjecture progressively evolves through this iterative sequence with the agent allowed to try to prove it at any point. We describe the results of applying PatternMiner to five well-known positivity problems in algebraic combinatorics. PatternMiner proves several new theorems related to Schubert positivity and projection, a character-row evaluation formula, a restricted version of the Kazhdan--Lusztig combinatorial invariance conjecture, and Schur positivity for a family of immanant coefficients. We end by analyzing the most common routes to conjecture generation that are used by PatternMiner in our case studies as well as documenting the most common failure modes.

Improving Building Energy Efficiency with an Indoor Environmental World Model and Counterfactual Adaptive Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Optimizing indoor climate systems plays a critical role in achieving the United Nations' Sustainable Development Goals 11 and 13. However, indoor delayed thermodynamic responses and partial observability severely hinder existing reinforcement learning (RL) methods, which are primarily limited by implicit thermal inertia, occupancy dynamic prediction, and cumulative prediction errors during long-horizon rollouts. Thus, we propose a novel occupant-centric indoor climate control framework. It develops indoor environmental world models to predict future physical thermal baselines and dynamic occupancy, explicitly capturing latent thermal inertia. By formulating adaptive regulation as counterfactual advantage learning against future environments via a Branching Dueling Q-Network, and integrating a Held-Action TD-λ target, it effectively mitigates delayed credit assignment. Additionally, a proposed physics-aligned smoothness regularization suppresses high-frequency actuator fluctuations, potentially extending equipment service life. Experiments on SemiBuildingSim and Sinergym demonstrate that our method achieves a superior multi-objective trade-off. Compared to the best-performing methods, it further reduces energy consumption by 11.2%, occupant discomfort by 44.2%, and action fluctuation by 47.3%, achieving better sample efficiency and cross-region effectiveness. This interdisciplinary study offers an energy-efficient, occupant-centric AI solution for control science, building science, and energy sustainability.

RefMix: Expanding Endpoint Proposals for Diversity-Preserving Reward Fine-Tuning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Online forward matching aligns flow-based generative models by repeatedly generating, rewarding, and fitting model-produced endpoints. However, this behavior creates an on-policy feedback loop: as high-reward modes become increasingly favored, alternative modes become less likely to appear in subsequent training pools, progressively reducing visual diversity. We introduce RefMix, a reference-assisted endpoint proposal strategy that addresses this limitation by injecting alternative candidates into the training pool while retaining the original task reward and forward matching objective. In particular, RefMix uses a reference model during early high-noise steps to expose alternative visual modes, followed by the current reward-tuned model for endpoint completion. Across state-of-the-art online forward matching methods, RefMix consistently improves visual diversity while preserving task reward performance. Controlled inference experiments further demonstrate that training-time endpoint exposure is critical to improve reward--diversity trade-offs, beyond the impact of reference-assisted inference. By rethinking how training endpoints are proposed, RefMix achieves up to 44% higher diversity over forward matching baselines at comparable reward levels.

CypherMAS: Benchmarking Routing Reliability in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) distribute reasoning across specialized agents that must exchange messages and invoke tools reliably. Existing evaluations typically couple communication with open domain tasks, making it difficult to determine whether a failure arises from reasoning, malformed tool use, lost messages, or forgotten requests. Prior benchmarks do not isolate communication execution or measure routing redundancy against a known optimal path. We introduce CypherMAS, a synthetic benchmark that isolates communication and routing in fixed multi-agent graphs. Agents in chain, star, bipartite, or ring graphs route cyphered messages to remote peers holding the matching decoders; since the optimal route is known, redundant LLM calls can be measured and routing failures may be easily tracked. Across eleven open-weight models from 4B to 753B, small models fail at routing and tool use, dense models above 27B degrade with diameter, and successful runs exceed minimum LLM calls. These findings identify communication reliability as a distinct scaling bottleneck for multi-agent systems and provide a controlled diagnostic for complex multi-agent systems. The source code is freely available: https://github.com/Andoree/CypherMAS.

WorldPhy: Benchmarking Fine-Grained Physical Plausibility of World Models in Robotics

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World models are increasingly used in robotics to improve the performance of embodied models. Compared with general-purpose video generation, robotics places more stringent requirements on world models. Generated videos must satisfy fine-grained physical constraints on robot motion and object interactions. However, existing world model benchmarks can overlook robotics specific physical details and struggle to assess them accurately. To address this problem, we introduce WorldPhy, a world model benchmark for accurate, fine-grained evaluation of physical plausibility in robotics. Our benchmark contains 300 tasks, each consisting of an initial-frame image and a task instruction, covering diverse robotics scenes, task categories, viewpoints, and robot configurations. We further collect over 12K high-quality human annotations, and use them to train WorldPhyReward to accurately provide scores and reasons across six fine-grained physical dimensions and an instruction-following dimension. Comparisons with general VLMs and existing reward models show that WorldPhyReward better aligns with human ratings. We system- atically evaluate 13 frontier open and closed-source world models. The results reveal that current world models struggle to consistently maintain fine-grained physical plausibility across different robotics scenarios. Our code is available at https://github.com/chuziyuan139/ WorldPhy.

WhatIf: Counterfactual World Models over Action-Grounded Causal Graphs for Robotic Manipulation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World models predict what happens under an action, but choosing between actions requires comparing their outcomes in the same physical situation. Factual transitions alone determine action-wise marginal futures and average effects, but not individual effects or the probability that switching actions causes harm. We introduce WhatIf, a counterfactual world model for manipulation that couples candidate futures through shared latent physics and transition noise inferred from factual history. Each candidate action induces a sparse object-factor causal graph describing the physical pathways it activates. Rolling out these graphs under the shared situation anchors predictions to observed outcomes and confines action contrasts to the objects each action reaches. Shared type-conditioned functions support varying numbers of objects, while a round-trip loss enforces action-invariant abduction without counterfactual labels. We show that replacing an individual effect with its conditional mean incurs mean-squared error equal to its conditional variance, motivating comparisons between coupled futures. Building on these comparisons, Counterfactual Advantage Planning (CAP) ranks plans by their advantage over an incumbent within shared situations and constrains the probability of harm. Paired advantages reduce estimator variance by a factor of $1-ρ$, where $ρ$ is the correlation between branch outcomes, and cancel model errors shared by both branches. Each executed transition provides hindsight advantages to update the physics posterior, the next plan, and an amortized policy without a learned critic. We further establish when observational transitions identify interventional dynamics. Paired simulator interventions evaluate counterfactual accuracy, graph recovery, compositional generalization, and decision quality.

MedSkill-E$^{2}$: Evaluating and Evolving Skills for Medical Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills to access domain knowledge and complete real-world tasks. We observe that even when a skill specifies the rules and procedures needed for a task, agents may fail to apply them effectively during autonomous execution, a phenomenon we call skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). This issue is particularly consequential in medical tasks, where overlooking a single critical constraint can yield a plausible but incorrect result. To evaluate and address EBU, we introduce MedSkill-E$^{2}$, a framework for evaluating and evolving skills for medical agents. We construct EBU-Bench, a challenging benchmark covering 12 medically relevant task groups and associated skills, to evaluate agents' ability to use available skills autonomously. We quantify the EBU gap as the difference in skill-use accuracy between autonomous use and explicitly guided reading and application of the same skill. On EBU-Bench, all evaluated model-harness combinations exhibit EBU, with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points (pp). For the same model, skill-use accuracy under autonomous use differs by up to 38.96 pp across harnesses. Motivated by these observations, we propose EBU-Evolve, which uses execution feedback from autonomous and explicitly guided skill use to distill effective guidance into reusable skill instructions. Relative to their pre-evolution versions, the revised skills improve skill-use accuracy during autonomous execution by 33.33 to 55.17 pp and continue to yield improvements when transferred across multiple models and harnesses. Our results show that autonomous skill-use accuracy varies across harnesses with the model and skill held fixed, and model rankings can change. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened. EBU-Evolve improves autonomous skill-use accuracy on unseen tasks within the same skill, with gains transferring across models and harnesses.

Latent-in-the-Middle: On the Security of Cross-Model Latent Communication in Multi-Agent Systems

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

As large language models increasingly collaborate in heterogeneous multi-agent systems, latent communication has emerged as an efficient alternative to exchanging natural-language messages. By sharing internal representations, agents can avoid repeatedly processing the same context, reducing communication overhead and prefill computation. One such mechanism is cross-model Key-Value (KV) cache transfer, which uses a learned mapper to translate a source model's KV cache into one a target model can use. However, this handoff means the target model consumes context it did not encode itself, thus calling into question whether its safety alignment still holds. In this work, we investigate the security implications of latent handoffs by introducing Chasm, a framework characterizing cross-model handoff attacks across three new attack surfaces: the source model, mapper, and handoff mechanism itself. Our experiments reveal that latent handoffs substantially undermine safety alignment and prompt-injection defenses. Notably, a target model that follows only 0.4% of benchmark prompt injections natively, follows up to 44.2% when transferred through a mapper from a source model. On the other hand, for jailbreak attacks, transfer alone raises attack success by up to 24.6 pp within a model family and zero-shot attacks by up to 69.2 pp, whereas white-box optimization through the mapper boosts the attack success rate to 92.5%, against 37.5% for ordinary GCG on the same target. To address these vulnerabilities, we propose a lightweight target-side cache guard that corrects transferred caches to restore the target model's refusal, considerably mitigating these attacks.

Temporal Selective Exploration for Reinforcement Learning-Guided Continuous-Discrete Flow Matching in 3D Molecular Design

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Flow matching has shown strong potential in generative tasks, while its optimization via Reinforcement Learning (RL) remains underexplored, especially in continuous-discrete mixed settings. Moreover, flow matching is formulated as an Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE), whose deterministic trajectories do not naturally support RL optimization. Introducing stochasticity by converting the entire trajectory into a Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) is a common practice. Nevertheless, not all timesteps contribute to the final outcomes, and redundant exploration introduces additional noise. Furthermore, in multi-objective settings, some objectives may dominate the optimization while others receive insufficient updates. In this study, we propose an RL-guided continuous-discrete flow matching framework with temporal selective exploration for 3D de novo molecular design. Specifically, our method jointly optimizes continuous and discrete flow matching for atomic coordinates and molecular identities, respectively, while restricting exploration to timesteps that primarily contribute to target properties, reducing ineffective exploration. We also introduce a reward-based advantage calibration mechanism to mitigate objective dominance in multi-objective optimization. The framework is applied to design inhibitors for both the well-studied target protein EGFR and the challenging Pin1 protein. We identify some novel candidate inhibitors, supported by in silico validation.

KiRA: Kronecker Input-Gated Rank Adaptation for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of Pretrained Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Full fine-tuning of large pretrained models is expensive, and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) is the standard alternative. Most PEFT methods use low-rank updates, whose rank lies far below that of full fine-tuning and is the same for every input. Input-adaptive variants vary the capacity per input but remain within the low-rank bottleneck, and whether a task benefits from input conditioning is not known before training. This paper introduces Kronecker input-gated rank adaptation (KiRA). Instead of the conventional low-rank product, the update $ΔW$ is the Kronecker product of a frozen random $p\times p$ matrix $A$ and a trainable factor $M(x)$, which overcomes the rank ceiling: because rank is multiplicative under this product, $A$ multiplies the rank of $M(x)$ by $p$ without adding trainable parameters. The factor $M(x)$ is further decomposed as $B_1\,\mathrm{diag}(g(x))\,B_2$, where the input gate $g(x)$, computed once per sample by a network shared across all adapted layers, adjusts the rank of the update to each input in steps of $p$. Where input conditioning is unnecessary, the gate is folded into $B_1$ at deployment. Because the gate changes the rank only in discrete steps, the benefit of input conditioning can be predicted before training from a few hundred labeled inputs. Across six medical imaging and language benchmarks, KiRA attains the highest mean score against five published baselines with 8.7--9.7 times fewer trainable parameters than low-rank adaptation, and the pre-training criterion agrees with the measured benefit of input conditioning on all six tasks.

FlyingStart: Learned Cross-Token Warm Starts for Fixed-Point Decoding in Recurrent-Depth Language Models

📅 2026-11-25 14:51
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recurrent-depth language models generate each token by iterating a shared block of layers until its hidden state converges. Inference methods for these models decide when to stop the iteration, yet every token still starts from random noise and pays the full cost of converging. We show that where the iteration starts is a second, independent lever. A token's fixed point is predictable from the state at which the previous token stopped, after a learned change of coordinates, and we introduce FlyingStart, a linear warm start that is fitted in closed form on the frozen model and costs under 5% of one loop. The iteration and its stopping rule are unchanged, so the exit criterion certifies the same distance to the fixed point from any start: a better start removes loops at unchanged accuracy. On Huginn-0125, FlyingStart matches the accuracy of the strongest training-free baseline with 14--19% fewer decoding layers, raises batched throughput by 16--18% in two serving engines, and adds a further 10--14% on top of self-speculative decoding. A contraction analysis predicts the number of loops saved on all four models we study, including one where the prediction was recorded before any evaluation, and token by token on Huginn, and it identifies when a warm start pays off: the saving grows with the number of loops a model needs to converge.

One Model, Many Modes: Evolving System Prompt Ensembles for Creative Generation

📅 2026-11-25 17:29
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Preference-aligned LLMs can exhibit mode collapse, concentrating their output distributions around a narrow set of high-reward responses. This limits creative generation, which requires quality, novelty, and diversity. Existing test-time methods often emphasize diversity, giving less attention to whether individual responses are both high-quality and novel. This distinction matters in practical use, where users may seek a single compelling, original response. We formalize creative generation as a multi-objective distributional optimization problem and introduce SEPO, a training-free framework for System Prompt Ensemble Optimization. The framework combines prompt evolution with greedy max-min selection to identify behaviorally distinct system prompts under a quality constraint, then uniformly samples one prompt per response at inference time. Across four creative generation tasks with increasing quality requirements and three open-weight models, SEPO ranks first in 8 of 12 settings on the harmonic mean of quality, diversity, and novelty, while no baseline leads in more than two. On average, it raises LLM-judged diversity from 1.92 to 3.43 and novelty from 1.00 to 3.34 over base sampling (1-5 scale), with quality decreasing from 3.72 to 3.30. We further show that expanding the optimized ensemble increases separation among prompt-conditioned response distributions. These results support system prompt ensemble optimization as a promising approach to improving LLM creativity at inference time.

EATS: Scaling Tool-Use Experience for Medical Reasoning Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Medical reasoning agents combine large language model (LLM) reasoning with clinical workflows and specialized neural tools. However, general-purpose LLMs receive limited training exposure to domain-specific medical tools, and the uncertainty and distributional shifts of neural tool outputs make selecting tools from simple descriptions significantly more challenging than in deterministic settings such as calculators or dictionary lookups. In this paper, we propose EATS, an experience-augmented tool selection strategy that leverages accumulated tool-use trajectories to improve tool selection in medical reasoning agents. EATS constructs a memory of past interactions, including queries, tool choices, arguments, and self-evaluated feedback, and retrieves relevant experiences to guide tool selection for new queries. As memory construction does not require ground-truth supervision, EATS scales naturally with test-time experience. We evaluate EATS on tool-augmented medical reasoning tasks, including closed-ended visual question answering and free-form report generation. Experiments on four public datasets, ChestAgentBench, CheXbench, MIMIC-CXR, and SLAKE-VQA, across three multimodal LLMs, Qwen3-VL 2B, Qwen3-VL 32B, and GPT-5.5, show consistent gains in tool selection behavior and downstream medical reasoning performance. Code is available at https://github.com/Limingyuan001/EATS.

What Does Skill Lift Actually Measure? A Source-Code-Level Audit and Noise-Aware Reanalysis of SkillsBench

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agent skills are directories of instructions, reference materials, and scripts loaded on demand by coding agents. According to SkillsBench, although human-curated skills can raise the passing rate by about 16.6 percentage points (pp) on average, they have actually reduced the results for some tasks. Later research has started to gate or eliminate skills according to the above per-task gains. We have audited the above benchmark at the source-code level and analysed 7,554 public trials to form 1,421 paired "(configuration, task) cells". Source-code audit identified two controlled-verified cases of grader dependence and one operational definition gap; the average lift on these three tasks was 74.6 pp, and the overall average lift for the full set was 18.7 pp. The audit also found that four tasks contained placeholder skill directories, and a certain execution harness additionally added 48 built-in skills in most runs. No task-level reduction achieved statistical significance in the pooled reanalysis with multiple-testing correction. As there were at most three runs per condition for each cell, the observed negative differences are very susceptible to run-sampling noise, and arithmetically, 58% of the cells could not have shown a decrease. Therefore, the raw numbers of negative cells cannot show that skills cause harm. A gating strategy based on the skill-free score seems to improve by 2-3 pp in the logs; however, even after shuffling condition labels to remove any skill effect, it still achieves an increase of 4-7 pp. On the held-out runs not used for fitting and decision-making, there was no statistically significant improvement. The audit ledger and analysis scripts have been published by us.

VisualMontage: Manipulating the Beliefs of Vision-Language Agents with Authentic Image Sequences

📅 2026-11-25 15:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

As agents based on vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly interpret social-media content, errors in their analysis may lend credibility to rumors and amplify their spread. In this paper, we study the problem of visual cognitive collusion, in which coordinated posts combine authentic images and locally faithful captions to induce VLM agents to infer and accept attacker-chosen target claims through their own reasoning. To enable systematic evaluation of this cognitive threat, we introduce a new benchmark comprising 544 target cases and 33.2K candidate images across eight application domains. We further propose VisualMontage, a multi-agent framework for simulating such attacks through narrative planning, image selection and ordering, and caption assignment under iterative review. We evaluate this threat through extensive experiments across 12 vision-language models, examining modality effects, production-team components, and downstream defenses. Using identical attack feeds across models without victim-specific optimization, we demonstrate cross-model transferability, achieving mean attack success rates of 61.93% for proprietary models and 68.71% for open-weight models. Moreover, 92.65% of generated feeds pass independent model-based validity auditing. Even after downstream review, residual attack success rates range from 35.3% to 76.7% across the evaluated configurations. Our additional controlled comparisons further reveal that captions can improve model-rated posting naturalness while reducing attack success. Together, these findings reveal a novel cognitive threat in social-media interpretation and motivate safeguards that assess how authentic posts jointly shape agents' reasoning and conclusions.

Evaluating Aviation Safety Knowledge Extraction with SmolLM2: From Zero-Shot to Retrieval-Grounded Prompting

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Aviation accident narratives contain safety knowledge that is difficult to capture using structured occurrence fields alone. This study evaluates prompting-based knowledge extraction across four dimensions: concept accuracy, verbatim evidence grounding, structural compliance, and directed relation extraction. SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct was evaluated under zero-shot, one-shot, fixed few-shot, and retrieval-grounded prompting using 300 National Transportation Safety Board narratives from 2000-2020, containing 3,763 reference concepts and 467 directed relations. Evaluation combined structural checks, verbatim evidence matching, expert-reviewed semantic matching, and paired bootstrap confidence intervals. No prompting strategy performed best across all dimensions. Retrieval-grounded prompting achieved the highest concept micro-F1 of 32.27%, although its advantage over fixed few-shot prompting was uncertain (95% CI: -0.92 to 2.40 percentage points). One-shot prompting achieved the highest relation F1 of 6.95%, identifying directed relation extraction as the principal SmolLM2 bottleneck. Compared with fixed few-shot prompting, retrieval improved verbatim grounding (59.45% vs. 54.02%) but reduced complete structural compliance (33.3% vs. 42.7%). Verbatim matching establishes source presence rather than evidential relevance. An exploratory comparison using 35 benchmark narratives showed that Qwen3-8B achieved higher concept micro-F1 across all prompting conditions and higher relation F1 under demonstration-based prompting, reaching 51.37% and 34.62%, respectively, under one-shot prompting. The small subset limits generalization. These findings support evaluating semantic correctness, source traceability, structural reliability, and directed relation accuracy separately.

InstructTTS-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Objective Instruction-Following TTS

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Instruction-following text-to-speech (Instruct-TTS) renders a target transcript in the manner described by a free-form natural-language instruction, and a good rendition must succeed on three fronts at once: following the instruction, pronouncing the transcript correctly, and sounding natural. Reinforcement learning has become the standard way to post-train such generative models toward human preferences, and TTS systems increasingly adopt it. When the three objectives are optimized together under a single scalar reward, typically with group relative policy optimization (GRPO), training tends to be unstable: the objectives compete, and the shared update drifts toward whichever one is easiest to improve, producing a seesaw among them. We instead adapt on-policy distillation (OPD), which fuses several single-objective teachers by matching their velocity fields on the student's own rollouts. Realizing this for Instruct-TTS first calls for a sound reward on each axis, which we analyze carefully: a distilled Qwen-Omni proxy for the costly Gemini judge, dialect-free data for the WER reward, and DNSMOS in place of the language-biased UTMOS. We then train three single-objective teachers with GRPO and fuse them with OPD, routing each rollout to the instruction-following or WER teacher by its data domain and letting every rollout additionally follow the MOS teacher for quality. This lets instruction following and intelligibility improve together without a trade-off, but the quality target conflicts with instruction following, so following it additively reintroduces the seesaw. We therefore propose conflict-aware velocity fusion, which projects the quality target off the direction that opposes the primary objective before fusing, and show that it markedly reduces the instruction-following/quality trade-off. The fused model improves over scalar-reward GRPO on all three objectives at once.

Lost in the Grid: Where Multimodal LLMs Fail Between Seeing and Planning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) excel at static visual understanding and are increasingly applied to spatial planning, where a model interprets a visual layout and outputs an action sequence to reach a goal. Grid games are a common testbed for this ability because their rules are explicit and a solver can verify every move. Yet existing benchmarks mostly report end-to-end success rates, which hide whether a failure stems from visual perception, rule application, or multi-step planning. We introduce GridProbe, a diagnostic benchmark that 1) locates the failing stage between visual perception and planning and 2) verifies the diagnosis by targeted fine-tuning. On Sokoban, GridProbe pairs end-to-end solving with four probes (recognition, localization, single-step prediction, and state tracking) and gives every board both as an image and as an equivalent text grid. Evaluating seven MLLMs on Sokoban and a maze game reveals distinct bottlenecks behind similar success rates: proprietary models are limited by multi-step planning, whereas open-source models are limited by rule application or by visual grid indexing, i.e., counting rows and columns. Planning failures also hide perception failures: when planning is easier, the visual cost of the same model grows from 7.6 to 37.7 points. The diagnosis identifies what to train: fine-tuning only the vision module of the perception-limited model on board reading raises its interactive Sokoban success 3.5-fold (5.8% to 20.4%) and transfers zero-shot to the unseen maze game (single-turn success 13.8% to 67.1%), whereas the same data does not help a rule-limited model and training on solver solutions brings no gain. We will release the benchmark and code at https://github.com/suat-llm/GridProbe.

Confidence scores for gated automation of large language models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI agents built on large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong capabilities in automated decision-making. However, LLMs may produce incorrect answers with high confidence, limiting their applicability in decisions that affect individuals and organizations. Reliable confidence scores are necessary to gate such decisions, and existing estimates are frequently unavailable or too coarse to support effective gating. This work uses supervised learning to train models to predict the correctness of LLM-generated answers, enabling automated confident responses and routing uncertain ones for human review. A linear confidence score head is fitted to the final-layer hidden state of the frozen LLM backbone, and text-only metamodels are trained as an alternative for closed models. All results come from a single 2B-parameter backbone, on graded answers that are about 98% SQuAD and the remainder TruthfulQA. Against multiple baselines, the confidence score head outperforms all baselines and metamodels, automating the largest fraction of the workload at high accuracy targets. Prompted confidence scores can appear well calibrated yet lack the resolution needed for thresholding. Although transferability to unseen datasets is modest, the confidence score head remains the most robust approach. The proposed confidence scoring framework enhances the safety of automating LLM-based agents and can be adapted to gate other properties, including safety or compliance.

REI: Learning Ideation from Repository Evolution

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

As AI systems become increasingly capable of executing and evaluating experiments, ideation is emerging as a bottleneck in automated scientific and engineering iteration. In many real-world domains, useful ideas are sparse and their validation is costly, making direct online exploration inefficient. We observe that repository evolution provides a scalable source of reusable technical experience: code changes record concrete technical hypotheses, while subsequent revisions, adoption, and reuse provide signals about their utility. Based on this observation, we propose REI, a Repository Evolution-driven Ideation framework that learns reusable ideation policies from repository evolution, injecting domain-specific technical experience into model parameters and reducing reliance on costly trial-and-error. We further identify a hidden failure mode in quality-driven ideation optimization: semantic diversity collapse. Although generated ideas can remain lexically diverse, they increasingly concentrate in a small number of dominant method families, limiting cross-task generalization and long-tail exploration. To address this problem, we introduce Semantic Prior Optimization and Debiasing (SPOD), a diversity-preserving alignment mechanism that makes method identity explicit, corrects category-level reward bias, and limits redundant accumulation within the same method family while retaining strong representatives. Experiments on public benchmarks and a real-world industrial setting demonstrate improvements in ideation quality, method diversity, cross-task generalization, and long-tail exploration.

PRAM: Activating Latent Multimodal Knowledge through Partwise Reasoning

📅 2026-11-25 16:21
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Emergent cross-modal classification and retrieval enable transfer between modalities without direct paired supervision. However, existing methods often prioritize sample-level alignment, overlooking local discriminative information and partwise statistical relations. To this end, we propose partwise reasoning across modalities (PRAM), which activates latent multimodal knowledge in frozen encoders by composing partwise statistical relations through a shared pivot modality. Specifically, we first present a multi-granularity covariance regularization module that estimates covariances from global and local vectors in two collections of modality-pivot pairs, stabilizing local estimates through adaptive blending with their cross-part means. Second, we devise a pivot-based spectral relational reasoning module that infers global and partwise statistical relations between target modalities through regularized covariance composition and extracts paired projection directions and their correlation strengths via whitening and spectral decomposition. Finally, we introduce a multi-granularity score fusion module that standardizes and fuses matching scores from global and local relations, incorporating task-specific information on pivot associations and class semantics for class prediction and candidate ranking. Extensive experiments across ten datasets demonstrate that PRAM achieves state-of-the-art performance in 16 classification settings and 19 retrieval settings, while exhibiting highly competitive performance in three sensing scenarios.

Not Every Input Needs Everything: Unifying Expert Gating, Model Cascades, and Agent Orchestration

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Executing monolithic neural weights, frontier foundation models, or entire multi-agent workflows uniformly on every input incurs substantial computational, latency, and monetary overhead. Conditional computation mitigates this cost by deciding dynamically which internal experts, model endpoints, or specialized agents to invoke per request. Yet token-level gating in sparse neural architectures, query-level model cascades, and task-level multi-agent coordination have largely progressed in disconnected research silos, obscuring recurring system failures and transferable fixes. In this systematic review, we synthesize 2,289 studies published between 2017 and September 2026 (selected from 13,884 unique records) across 20 methodological families under a unified two-step inspect-then-allocate framework. Discrete hard selection accounts for 70%, 83%, and 91% of token-, query-, and task-scale literature respectively, and computational overhead appears as a top operational bottleneck across every layer. Engineering safeguards remain sharply asymmetric: intra-layer gating prioritizes load balancing while rarely incorporating uncertainty estimation, whereas model selection and agent orchestration rely on confidence thresholds and budget rules while confronting miscalibration and cascading errors. At the same time, publication volume is accelerating fastest in agentic task delegation, where routing mechanisms are least often isolated experimentally and methodological bias risk is highest.

The Geometry Is the Memory: Task-Density Routing for Continual LoRA

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Freezing older adapters does not ensure that a continual learner will select and use them properly at inference time. When multiple adapters exist, routing among them and identifying the adapter whose representation space matches the query's answer can be difficult. We propose CONTLORA, which replaces stored examples with compact class statistics for adapter selection and classification. Gaussian class models in a frozen representation select LoRA adapters, while accumulated feature moments exactly recover a class-balanced ridge classifier for fixed training features. Trained without retaining images or feature vectors, CONTLORA reaches 88.55% accuracy on 20-task CIFAR-100 and 86.99% on CUB-200. Across three benchmarks in the matched component study, its mean final accuracy differs from an independently trained stored-feature counterpart by at most 0.07 percentage points. Ablations show that improved task coverage need not improve classification and adapter composition also matters. On held-out classes, competitor-centered likelihood scores improve novelty AUROC over raw likelihood without altering routing ranks. These results support statistical memory as an alternative to example-retention approaches in the evaluated settings, while identifying task ambiguity as an ongoing limitation.

StateWeaver: Multi-Agent Coordination of Story-State Transitions for Long-Horizon Video Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Modern video foundation models generate high-quality short clips, but preserving long-video consistency and correctly depicting event-driven state changes remain challenging. Existing agentic workflows face these challenges because continual planning and repair accumulate context, while reference updates remain limited. We introduce StateWeaver, which combines Active States and Hierarchical Collaboration to connect review-driven visual reference updates with cross-shot state inheritance. Active States translate state discrepancies identified during review into concrete visual asset requirements that guide reference construction, validation, and regeneration, preserving identity and already-correct content while repairing target attributes. Confirmed generation results are linked to the corresponding asset states, story time, and reference versions for verification and reuse by subsequent shots. Hierarchical Collaboration isolates execution contexts by task, clip, and repair round, and uses hierarchical memory to organize this process across multiple sets. Across multiple benchmarks, StateWeaver outperforms existing methods and produces long videos with sustained cross-shot consistency under predefined and autonomously planned storyboards.

When Are Parallel Subagents Effective for Coding? An Empirical Study on Repository Construction

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM-based coding agents are increasingly used for long-horizon tasks such as building an entire program from a specification. Products like Codex and Claude Code let an agent delegate work to parallel subagents, multiplying run cost, yet it remains unclear whether and when delegation produces a better program. We compare a single agent with a main agent delegating to subagents on repository-construction tasks from NL2Repo and ProgramBench (which also provides an executable reference program). We compare scores at equal time and equal cost, run intervention experiments, and analyze fine-grained behavior. On randomly drawn tasks, parallel subagents raise hidden-test pass rates by only about 1 to 5 percentage points at up to 3.8x the cost of a single agent. Their gain is larger on hard tasks, where we find evidence of three mechanisms. Self-checking: with GPT-5.6 Sol, one sentence telling a single agent to probe the reference program first recovers most of this gain at a fraction of the cost; from GPT-5.6 Sol to GPT-6.1 Sol, the gain shrinks as single agents check their own work more. Fresh context: in extended runs, a single agent that periodically starts a new session with an empty conversation makes faster early progress in every comparison than one that resumes its conversation. Modularity: multi-agent runs produce more modular code (more files and shorter functions), closer to the upstream source. We observe similar patterns in a smaller replication with Opus 5.5 on Claude Code. Overall, subagents help when (i) a single agent stops checking too early, (ii) a long session keeps editing with little improvement, or (iii) modular code helps on the task; otherwise, they mainly add cost.

Was the Model Given the Right Action? Auditing and Repairing Action Interfaces in Navigation World Models

📅 2026-11-25 17:18
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World models are evaluated by predicting future observations from actions. These evaluations assume that actions are prepared the same way as during training, but an unchecked mismatch can make a model appear less accurate than it is. We audit this assumption in Navigation World Models (NWM) and find three interacting differences between its training and rollout code. Correcting the inputs, without retraining, reduces mean visual prediction error by 42%, measured with DreamSim on the RECON navigation dataset. The correction also changes the ranking of NWM checkpoints in an exploratory analysis and removes or reduces two alternative models' measured advantages. Correcting only the normalization offset instead increases error, showing why interacting mismatches must be checked together. To test whether sensitivity to input preparation extends beyond NWM, we deliberately introduce encoding errors into two alternative models; both become less accurate. We introduce ActRosetta, a tool that checks whether action encoding matches training, identifies supported causes of disagreement, and repairs inputs when sufficient information is available. Repair helps little where predictions barely respond to actions, the tool's diagnosis has limited coverage, and improved control remains unproven. For any action-conditioned world model, the lesson is the same: before reporting rollout error, verify that evaluation encodes actions exactly as training did.

DuoMI: Dual Mutual Information Regularization for Expert Reuse in Looped Transformers

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We introduce Dual Mutual Information Regularization (DuoMI), a method for regulating expert routing across rounds in looped mixture-of-experts (MoE) Transformers. When two rounds of computation share an expert pool, their routers may favor different expert subsets, limiting the reuse of individual expert parameters across rounds. Even when aggregate expert usage is identical across rounds, we would still prefer each token to retain some experts while switching to others. DuoMI addresses both aspects of routing: it uses round-level marginal mutual information to limit the dependence of aggregate expert usage on the round, and token-level conditional mutual information to penalize average cross-round routing changes that fall outside a target interval. During training, DuoMI computes differentiable penalties from soft routing distributions over all experts. It adds no model parameters and leaves sparse Top-$k$ routing strategy unchanged at inference. On a looped OLMoE backbone in which paired adjacent layers share experts, DuoMI improves the average score across $15$ downstream tasks from $50.08\%$ to $51.82\%$, a gain of $1.74$ percentage points over the looped baseline.

Mixture-of-Loops Transformer

📅 2026-11-25 18:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Looped Transformers boost expressivity by reusing shared layers across loops, yet at the cost of efficiency. Pushing their expressivity-efficiency frontier requires rethinking the core mechanism connecting adjacent loops: What should be passed as a Transformer loops? We introduce Mixture-of-Loops Transformer (MoLT), whose Mixture-of-Loops Attention (MoLA) uses keys and values (KV) as memory for selective reuse across depths and loops. Under loop residual, MoLT-R retains the residual stream and reads local KV across loops for expressivity. Under loop KV, MoLT-KV instead restarts from embeddings and reads current-loop local KV with preceding-loop memory KV at earlier positions for efficiency. MoLT-KV eliminates same-position cross-loop dependence and, paired with loop forcing for parallel training, unlocks single-traversal Mixture-of-Loops (MoL) decoding beyond the training loop horizon. To balance efficiency and quality, we introduce U-shaped MoLT (U-MoLT), which separates memory construction from prediction readout and skips unused decoder traversals without changing predictions. In 400B-token pretraining, U-MoLT outperforms OLMo3 across 700M/1.5B dense and 3B MoE models, achieving 1.7x validation cross-entropy (CE) token efficiency at 700M while improving 3B MoE 10-domain perplexity by 0.75 and 10-task accuracy by 2.1 percentage points. In 100B-token continued pretraining of Qwen3.5-0.8B-Base, our U-MoLT adaptation lowers CE on all 11 domains over the matched baseline with 1.4x mean token efficiency. At inference, U-MoLT with MoL-Hybrid-2 matches non-looped OLMo3 latency at 996.6 vs. 1,000.6 ms on S-size (~113M) models. Code is available at https://github.com/hustvl/MoLT

Puzzling-Bench: Evaluating Language Models on 23,777 Human-Written Puzzles with Community-Verified Answers

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We introduce Puzzling-Bench, a benchmark of 23,777 puzzles with community-verified answers drawn from the Puzzling Stack Exchange archive (2014-2026): riddles, ciphers, logic grids, lateral-thinking problems, wordplay and recreational mathematics, a third of them with images. Every puzzle carries the answer accepted by its author, a short reference answer taken from it (LLM-extracted for the evaluation sets and checked on a 50-puzzle human review), the author's own hints, community tags and a creation date, so that the benchmark supports hint ablations, category analysis and a rolling post-cutoff slice (254 puzzles posted after the training cutoff of six of the nine models we evaluate, including the best one) as a contamination check. We evaluate nine frontier and open models with a two-tier scorer (normalized exact match, then an LLM judge) and validate the judge with three judges from two model families: they agree on 95-97% of verdicts (kappa 0.82-0.96), move no model by more than 1.6 points, and show no own-family preference. The best model, GPT-6.1, reaches 74.8% on a shared 255-puzzle set; a hard core of 17 puzzles defeats all nine models; model failures are strongly correlated, and the second-best model contributes a single puzzle to a greedy ensemble, yet an oracle over all nine reaches 91%. On a 100-puzzle multimodal pilot, withholding the question's image costs the two best models 24 and 35 points. We release the dataset, the scoring pipeline and all per-row provenance.

MeterRouter: Budget-Aware Dynamic Routing for Multi-Agent Systems

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) outperform single-agent systems, but inter-agent communication incurs substantial token overhead. While assigning a large LLM to every agent is common, it results in inefficient resource utilization, leaving the system far from the Pareto frontier. The cost of answering a query is set jointly by how many agents are recruited, the role and LLM assigned to each, and communication topology among them. Most existing routers treat cost only implicitly rather than as an explicit optimization constraint. A fixed accuracy-cost preference also cannot express how much a caller is willing to pay for a particular query. We introduce MeterRouter, a budget-aware dynamic router that incrementally synthesizes a collaboration graph by selecting each agent's role, LLM, incoming connections, and termination conditioned on the query and a per-query monetary budget. We initialize the router by imitating successful execution graphs, then jointly refine its decisions with a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) whose reward favors correct answers and penalizes spending and budget overruns. Across five benchmarks and a three-tier LLM pool, MeterRouter is (1) accurate, achieving performance comparable to the strongest MAS; (2) economical, reaching that accuracy at 82% lower cost than the strongest MAS router and extending the accuracy-cost frontier beyond the ceiling of single-agent systems; and (3) controllable, adapting its spending to the specified budget while rarely exceeding it.

Teaching Students to Stand on Their Own: Distilling Vision Foundation Models for Fine-Grained Few-Shot Class-Incremental Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Fine-grained few-shot class-incremental learning (FG-FSCIL) aims to incrementally learn visually similar new classes from limited images without catastrophic forgetting. Prior work typically learns strong representations on base classes or leverages vision foundation models (VFMs) directly as backbones or via knowledge distillation. However, VFM backbones incur high computational costs and do not guarantee fine-grained recognition. Although knowledge distillation reduces inference cost, our preliminary experiments reveal that distilled students still lag substantially behind their VFM teachers, particularly on new classes. To address this limitation, we propose STAND, a systematic VFM-to-student distillation design tailored for FG-FSCIL. Specifically, STAND connects the teacher and student by equipping the student with a fixed copy of the teacher's classifier, aligning their outputs via projectors, and feeding both models composite images that combine different training images. On these composite images, relation distillation matches how the teacher and the student relate each image to other images, while difference distillation matches how this relation changes between two composite images. Finally, we freeze the student and classify new classes using length-calibrated prototypes, which restore the reduced similarity of few-shot prototypes. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that STAND achieves state-of-the-art performance across FG-FSCIL datasets at low computational cost, while generalizing across student backbones and broader datasets.

Unstable Action Boundaries in Lethal Drone Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents raise safety risks that are not captured by text-only refusal behavior, especially when tools can trigger high-impact actions. This concern is salient in military settings, where autonomous weapons raise the possibility of delegating force decisions to software agents. In this work, we study whether a drone agent's action boundary is stable under changes to the target descriptor. We evaluate recent and popular LLMs in a simulated perception-action pipeline over real-world drone imagery, where models can localize requested targets and invoke a simulated lethal tool. We find two concerning failure modes. First, some LLM agents invoke the lethal tool for ordinary generic descriptors such as \emph{people}, requiring no jailbreak at all. Second, an agent that initially appears conservative under protected descriptors can become unsafe under a simple semantic target shift.

Out of Sight, Not Out of Clues: Can Video LLMs Guess from Partial Evidence?

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

In real-world applications like egocentric recording, visual accessibility, and surveillance review, visual evidence for Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) is often only partially captured. A reliable model should match its response to the evidence: answering when sufficient, guessing from visible clues when partial, and abstaining when absent. Yet existing benchmarks enforce a binary answerable-or-unanswerable split, leaving partial evidence unmeasured and obscuring whether failures arise from missing evidence or misjudging sufficiency. We introduce ClueProbe, a diagnostic benchmark to (1) separate perceiving evidence from judging sufficiency, and (2) validate our diagnosis via targeted fine-tuning. ClueProbe comprises 1,721 human-annotated questions across 598 natural videos, labeled Answerable, Guessable, or Unanswerable, alongside controlled perturbations altering wording or visual input. Evaluating 10 open-source and proprietary Video LLMs reveals that failures stem from judgment rather than perception: models perceive the evidence yet lack graded sufficiency awareness, answering or refusing outright while rarely guessing from partial clues. Targeted fine-tuning shows that this reflects missing supervision rather than insufficient capacity: supervising the guess level lifts appropriate guessing from 1.6% to 40.4% without extra false refusals, whereas conventional refusal tuning rejects 92% of guessable questions. Data and code will be released at https://github.com/suat-llm/ClueProbe.

Crucible: Evaluating LLM Agents in Executable Chemistry Research Environments

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) perform well on chemistry knowledge benchmarks, which mainly assess question answering and isolated tasks. Chemical research, however, requires using specialized tools and interpreting their outputs to obtain chemically valid results. To address this gap, we introduce Crucible Bench to evaluate this ability across all four stages of the design-make-test-analyze (DMTA) cycle. The benchmark contains 1,078 tasks in 12 task families curated from 18 published datasets, covering molecular design, synthesis planning, experimental execution, closed-loop optimization, simulation, and analytical characterization. Agents access 75 domain-specific tools through the Model Context Protocol in seven isolated environments. Sandboxed graders verify the resulting synthesis routes, experimental protocols, and other outputs. Laboratory tasks use robotic simulators or previously measured experimental data. We evaluate 14 LLMs as agents and find that the best achieves an overall score of 0.545, with synthesis planning and closed-loop optimization emerging as the hardest domains. Overall performance does not predict optimization performance well: the model ranked twelfth overall achieves the highest score in closed-loop optimization. Error analysis also shows that agents often submit chemically invalid results despite running their tools successfully. Stronger models benefit more from tool access, but reliable use of chemical software remains a challenge that Crucible Bench can track as models improve.

Mitigating Overgeneralization in RND via Spectral Target Design

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Random Network Distillation (RND) is a scalable novelty signal for RL based on a fixed random target network and a trained predictor. However, it can overgeneralize: the predictor extrapolates the random target off the data manifold, causing novelty scores to collapse on unfamiliar inputs. This paper analyzes this failure through a kernel-theoretic lens. Under a GP target model and an NTK-regime predictor, we show that the RND novelty score is a cross-kernel residual variance: it is governed jointly by the target covariance kernel and the predictor NTK, rather than by either one alone. This identifies overgeneralization as spectral under-excitation. Motivated by this view, we formulate spectral target design as a principle for shaping RND novelty geometry. As a concrete instance, we replace the implicit random-network target with a bandwidth-controlled Random Fourier Feature (RFF) target, redistributing residual signal toward modes less attenuated by the predictor. Experiments on offline D4RL, online Atari, and Fetch manipulation benchmarks show improved novelty discrimination and downstream RL performance. The code for the RL implementation is available at https://github.com/tuuktuc86/RFF-RND.

Can image generation help visual reasoning? An empirical study

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Language models think in words; then multimodal models might think in images. We test whether a generated helper improves the answer to a visual question. A model describes the helper it needs and then answers using the original and generated images together. Across 13 vision-language model and generator pairs, generation lowers mean accuracy by 3.7 percentage points, with declines on nine of ten tasks. Two unified models, BAGEL and Janus-Pro-7B, each lose accuracy on eight tasks. On VSTAR, adding a generated image to the same planning hint lowers accuracy by 10.47 points. Repeated image edits yield different outcomes across tasks. ChartQAPro and MathVista stay below their no-generation baselines, while ERQA gains three points after ten edits.

TransHarness: From Benchmark Overfitting to Transferable Harness Evolution

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Harness evolution offers a path to improving coding agents without updating model weights. However, existing methods can overfit their evolution benchmarks, producing gains that fail to carry over to new tasks. To address this challenge, we introduce Transferable Harness Evolution (TransHarness), which evolves harness intervention policies from same-task success-failure contrasts. These policies couple execution-context triggers with diagnostic guidance, enabling task-dependent checks and corrections beyond the source executions. Cross-task replay and independent semantic selection retain policies that intervene selectively and ground corrections in current-task evidence rather than source-specific solutions. We further curate an independent evolution set for cross-distribution transfer and conduct a cross-benchmark evaluation covering multiple strong models and a range of lightweight and mature harnesses. Experiments show that, with only 300 source trajectories from outside the test distribution, TransHarness further improves strong agents across distinct target benchmarks, demonstrating transferable harness evolution beyond benchmark overfitting. Specifically, TransHarness achieves state-of-the-art scores of 76.4% on DeepSWE v1.1 and 95.5% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, surpassing the strongest baseline model, GPT-6 Astra, by 2.4 and 5.6 percentage points, respectively.

FIG-MAC: A Fine-grained Inspiration Graph Empowered Multi-Agent Collaboration for Automated Scientific Hypothesis Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Automated Scientific Hypothesis Generation (ASHG) leveraging large language models (LLMs) aims to harness their strengths in knowledge integration, logical reasoning, and text generation for efficiently producing innovative, verifiable scientific hypotheses. However, existing approaches face three critical limitations: (i) insufficient fine-grained knowledge representation, as unstructured context impairs LLM comprehension and reasoning; (ii) lack of knowledge evolution traceability, constraining the generation of innovative cross-domain conjectures; (iii) absence of human-inspired and role-specialized multi-agent collaboration, failing to leverage the efficient innovation mechanisms arising from cognitive diversity and division of labor among expert roles to complete the full ASHG-evaluation loop. To address these limitations, we propose the Fine-grained Inspiration Graph-empowered Multi-Agent Collaboration framework (FIG-MAC) including: (i) Fine-grained Inspiration Graph module (FIG), to obtain fine-grained structured knowledge representations, where nodes represent academic semantic units and edges encode heuristic relationships among them; (ii) ontology-guided hybrid reasoning module, which follows the FIG ontology to construct structured reasoning paths and uses vector search to enrich them with semantic evidence, to obtain the traceable scientific conjecture paths; (iii) role-specialized Multi-Agent framework, inspired by real scientific teamwork, enabling a closed-loop process of scientific conjecture generation, screening, and validation. Experimental results demonstrate that FIG-MAC significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on source diversity, provenance-adjusted novelty, and raw novelty.

Evaluating Ukrainian Grammatical Competence in Language Models: Probability Versus Prompting

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Organisations deploying large language models in Ukrainian-language information systems need to know whether a model produces normative Ukrainian. This paper establishes a protocol for measuring that reliably and applies it to 19 models. The instrument is a released set of 1,444 Ukrainian minimal pairs covering the vocative case, numeral-noun and adjective agreement, verb government and calques from russian, with subject-verb agreement as a control; a quarter of the set was checked against the answer key in two independent native-speaker ratings, which agreed at Cohen's $κ$ = 0.963. Every pair is scored twice: by comparing the probabilities a model assigns to the two sentences, and by asking the model directly, with the sentences presented in both orders so that answer-position effects are measured instead of being absorbed into the score. Three results follow. Order consistency (the share of pairs answered identically when the sentences are exchanged) falls below 0.05 for ten of the nineteen models, and what separates the usable models is instruction tuning, not scale. The vocative case is systematically inverted: 0.153 accuracy against 0.951 on the control, with models preferring the non-normative nominative form of address. Ukrainian adaptation substantially reduces this deficit, measured against the exact checkpoints two adapted models were trained from.

Sports Agent: A Self-Evolving System for Conversational Data Analytics

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Conversational sports analytics requires accurate, evidence-based responses with low latency. Changes in query distributions, data resources, and model behavior can degrade static agent configurations, motivating controlled evolution guided by runtime evidence. We present Sports Agent, a self-evolving framework integrating an Agentic SQL Model for structured-data reasoning, a harness for planning and tool orchestration, and a unified Feedback Engine connecting online execution with offline evolution. The engine assesses responses and execution trajectories, verifies factual claims, diagnoses failures, and generates evolution signals. Model evolution combines SQL-to-Question Scaling for coverage expansion with Graph-Guided Scaling for error-targeted synthesis. Generated questions undergo trajectory distillation and quality filtering for iterative supervised fine-tuning, followed by a separate Post-RL stage. Harness evolution translates recurrent failures into bounded prompt and skill updates, validated through independent audits, execution replay, and regression testing. Performance-tolerance checks guard against unacceptable degradation during simplification. The resulting Qwen3.6-35B-A3B-based model outperforms Qwen3.8-Max on sports question answering and analysis while achieving approximately three times the inference efficiency. Across 30 harness-evolution rounds, the aggregate performance score improves by 10.8 points. In a live sports analytics product, the evolved system improves factual correctness by 10.5 points and average response length, a proxy for answer richness, by 158.1%, while reducing average latency by 41.3% relative to the baseline.

The Linguistic Knowledge Benchmark: Do Language Models Know the Foundations of Early-Grade Literacy?

📅 2026-11-25 18:07
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Many languages of instruction in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) are poorly represented in the data used to train language models. Before these models can support early-grade literacy in such languages, we need to know whether they hold the linguistic knowledge of each language that literacy teaching relies on. To test this, we present the Linguistic Knowledge Benchmark and the reproducible, largely automated pipeline that turns curriculum documents, teacher guides and pupil books into a vetted question set organised on a fixed grid of nine linguistic domains and seven grade levels. The pipeline combines LLM-driven source survey, feature consolidation, item generation and quality control with an item-selection step inspired by item response theory and a final expert review. We instantiate it for Kiswahili (Kenya; 135 items) and English (United Kingdom; 137 items) and evaluate 121 and 119 models respectively. Frontier models exceed 90% accuracy in both languages, but accuracy falls steeply for smaller and cheaper models, and more so in Kiswahili. The two best models under 10 billion parameters reach 91.7% and 73.0% in English but only 51.9% and 40.7% in Kiswahili, and the mean accuracy of models deployable on mobile and tablet hardware is 47% in English against 24% in Kiswahili. The models most practical for low-resource classrooms are therefore the least capable in the languages they would serve. We release a 20-item sample per language, all pipeline prompts, open-source code, and a public leaderboard. This is the first rung of a three-level evaluation ladder for measuring and generating decodable text to support foundational literacy.

Practice Makes Fluent: Learning to Use Earlier Predictions in Looped Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

People become faster at a task through practice, but models that improve with experience do not necessarily learn to use less computation. We train looped models, whose intermediate predictions allow earlier exits, to perform tasks with fewer iterations through practice. This is difficult because shared-weight updates can degrade the final-loop prediction while improving early ones, and the exit gate must track early predictions as their quality changes. We propose FLUENT, which requires no additional task labels or rewards: it distills the initial model's final-loop prediction into early predictions, anchors the current final-loop prediction to the same frozen teacher, and trains the exit gate to balance disagreement with the current final-loop prediction against computation cost. FLUENT improves performance at shallower depth in new Crafter worlds, raises LIBERO success with fewer action-head iterations, and maintains language-model accuracy at lower selected depth. On a real robot, success rises from 62.3\% to 74.6\% while mean time over successful episodes falls from 69\,s to 31\,s. The project page is available at https://msseok96-cpu.github.io/FLUENT/.

$μ$-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-User Task Completion in Shared Interaction Voice Agents

📅 2026-11-25 18:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Voice agents in shared spaces must satisfy interdependent multi-user requests while upholding per-user authority, privacy, and task constraints. Existing evaluations offer limited coverage when participants react throughout a session. We introduce $μ$-Bench, a benchmark for joint task completion in closed-loop, full-duplex multi-user voice interaction. Simulated participants act from isolated profiles of goals, knowledge, and permissions, adapting to the agent's speech and tool outcomes. Across 20 domains, 100 scenarios vary group size, acoustics, and follow-up demands to form 800 cases (408 tool-augmented, 392 tool-free). Joint Task Success (JTS) requires all user goals, privacy boundaries, and terminal states to hold jointly, while Pure JTS further disallows unauthorized tool calls; Attribution, Threading, and Coordination (ATC) diagnostics localize failures. Across 13 voice agents, proprietary systems peak at 62.24\% tool-free and 65.44\% tool-augmented success, while open-weight models remain below 20\%. ATC diagnostics show that open-weight models lag primarily in multi-turn attribution and threading, and controlled contrasts reveal that larger groups consistently degrade joint task success across capable systems. By coupling reactive multi-user simulation with verifiable session-level outcomes, $μ$-Bench provides a rigorous testbed for shared voice agents.

Motion Beyond Pixels: Learning Future Visual Representations for Object-Centric Dynamics

📅 2026-11-25 16:24
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Many embodied world models predict future states in latent spaces optimized for RGB reconstruction, which do not explicitly prioritize object-level semantics and geometry relevant to motion generation. We present MotionRep, a visual representation world model that jointly generates future DINO representations and 3D object flow, coupling semantic and geometric information with object-centric dynamics. To make high-dimensional DINO features amenable to generative modeling, we introduce DINO-VAE, which combines temporal and channel compression with latent regularization to produce compact latents while preserving semantic and geometric information. Conditioned on an initial observation, object query points, and a language instruction, a unified diffusion transformer predicts future visual representations and 3D object flow through joint attention. MotionRep employs asymmetric modality-specific noise schedules to keep the visual stream ahead of 3D object flow during joint denoising, allowing progressively refined future visual representations to guide flow generation. Experiments on simulated and real-world manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that MotionRep outperforms pixel-based baselines in 3D object flow accuracy and generalization to unseen scenes, while requiring only three denoising steps for inference. These results support predictive visual representations as an effective future-state space for object-centric dynamics modeling.
