M$^{2}$AdaptVLA: Multimodal Memory-Augmented Vision-Language-Action Model with Predictive Test-Time Adaptation

📅 2026-11-25 15:32
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Effective robotic manipulation calls for multimodal memory and rapid adaptation to shifts in contact dynamics. However, many existing policies, which retain either visual or wrench history, may miss key cues from the other modality. Moreover, even with wrench memory, fixed policies can struggle when contact conditions shift at deployment. To this end, we introduce M$^{2}$AdaptVLA, a vision-language-action model that integrates visual and wrench memories with predictive test-time adaptation. For visual memory, the model uses test-time training (TTT) to encode observation history in fast weights. Each entry in the bounded TTT bank combines the corresponding frame's features with a readout from these weights, preserving cues that predate the oldest retained frame. For wrench memory, a context gate controls how much the action expert draws on wrench history as it jointly denoises future actions and wrenches. For predictive test-time adaptation, the auxiliary wrench prediction loss also provides online self-supervision. Using measured wrenches as targets, we update low-rank policy adapters by backpropagating only through the final denoising step. Real-world experiments show that the TTT bank outperforms visual memory baselines on progress tracking, scene recall, and demonstration following. On contact-sensitive tasks, the context gate yields higher success rates than direct injection or gated cross-attention. Predictive test-time adaptation improves robustness to shifts in contact dynamics. On eight tasks requiring visual and wrench memory, M$^{2}$AdaptVLA achieves a mean success rate of 82.71%, a 31.46% absolute gain over the strongest baseline. The project page is available at https://tlf-tlf.github.io/M2AdaptVLAPage/.

Decoder-as-a-Probe: Verbalizing and Steering Visual Representations in LVLMs

📅 2026-11-25 15:04
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

It is commonly accepted that high-level concepts are represented linearly in the representation space of large neural networks. For Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs), finding those interpretable directions in visual representations is important for interpretability. To find them, the classical approach is to train a linear classifier on the representations, but it needs a large amount of labels and only recovers what can be read from the representations, not what the model actually uses. In this paper we start from the following insight: The LLM Decoder of an LVLM is also a classifier whose classes are the possible next tokens. So we use its first-order Taylor expansion as a linear classifier. Hence, we introduce Decoder-as-a-Probe (DaP), which significantly outperforms classical probing and steering baselines especially when training data is sparse. (1) It verbalizes what an LVLM perceives in a given image. (2) It can surgically change the perception of the LVLM about specific attributes (e.g. change only the color, the clothes worn or the action done). Due to its simplicity and data efficiency, we believe DaP can serve as a plug-and-play tool for LVLM research, such as interpretability, hallucination mitigation or instruction tuning.

How Well Do Vision-Language Scene Graphs Serve as Memory for Embodied Question Answering?

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Embodied QA needs memory, but answer accuracy mixes retained evidence, reading errors and question priors. For a simulated multi-room scene-graph pipeline we define answerability: the fraction of questions a fixed rule-based reader answers correctly from the graph alone. The baseline recovers 24.1% of visible object types and its room labels flicker. Four targeted repairs double recall, raise answerability from 27.2% to 37.4%, and improve retrained QA from 71.4% to 75.9%, yet better object F1 can lower answerability. A question-type prior beats this reader on every predicted graph. In one real-video test, graph memory lowers accuracy. These exploratory findings favour scoring retained evidence alongside accuracy.

DiCALM: Converting Autoregressive Convolution-Attention Language Models into Discrete Diffusion Language Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Existing methods for adapting autoregressive (AR) language models to diffusion generation focus mainly on full-attention architectures. More efficient hybrid architectures remain underexplored. We introduce DiCALM, a recipe for converting the hybrid convolution-attention models of the LFM2 family (LFM2 and LFM2.5) from AR to masked-diffusion generation. DiCALM progressively extends LFM2's causal gated convolution so that it captures local context in both directions. It combines this extension with a masking warm-up and joint AR--diffusion training. In controlled experiments, DiCALM performs comparably to the corresponding fine-tuned LFM2.5-based AR models and delivers consistent speedups over them at both the 350M and 1.2B scales: over 1.4x on average and up to 1.79x.

Constraint-Aware Test-Time Scoring for LLM and VLM Candidate Selection

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

When an LLM or VLM produces several candidate answers, picking the best one usually relies on the model's own confidence, a learned judge model, or a domain check. Each has a known weakness: an answer can be fluent but insecure, mathematically plausible but physically infeasible, or rated highly by a judge while quietly breaking a task requirement. We propose a scoring framework that combines a small set of hard checks every answer must pass with a wider set of softer checks whose scores are blended into one number per candidate. This adaptive scoring framework consists of three components: a drafting model that produces the candidates, a configuring model that selects and weights the checks for each task, and scoring models that rate properties no grounded tool can compute. We apply our proposed method to four tasks: writing code that is both correct and secure (CWEval), generating robot manipulation plans (LIBERO with added safety variants), passing competitive-programming hidden tests (LiveCodeBench), and answering questions from retrieved documents (HotpotQA). An ablation study isolates the contribution of each component and finds that the largest gains come from combining hard checks with candidate comparison: on tasks where the first sampled answer is often broken or unsafe, this pairing lifts joint correctness-and-safety by up to +0.21 (retrieval-augmented QA) and code-generation safety by up to +0.28 (CWEval), with the size of the gain tracking how weak the first sample is, falling to near zero or slightly negative where it is already strong. The per-task layer that selects and weights the softer checks adds no measurable value over simpler alternatives. This builds a unified scoring framework, together with an ablation that identifies the grounded hard checks, not the soft-factor configuration, as the source of the gain.

Agentic LongLive: Asynchronous Agentic Memory for Long Video Generation

📅 2026-11-25 18:54
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Real-time autoregressive video generators stream minutes of video and accept new prompts on the fly, but struggle to keep people, objects, and places consistent over a long interactive story. Agent systems provide such memory for offline multi-shot generation, yet they read the whole script in advance, plan each shot before generating it, and regenerate failed shots, which a live stream does not allow. We present Agentic LongLive, an asynchronous agentic memory for long video generation. A memory agent on separate GPUs reads only the prompts revealed so far, never the generated video, and keeps a text state and evolving images for every person, object, and scene. From this entity memory it composes a global image and a per-shot memory image, which an auxiliary GPU prefills into the generator's attention sink before the next shot starts, and writes entity cards for the prompts that name them, so the video never waits for memory and video errors are not written back. A small language model, trained with reinforcement learning and an image reward calibrated using downstream video outcomes, decides what to remember before the next prompt is known. On LongStory-Online, a new benchmark of 25 stories (857 shots) revealed one shot at a time, Agentic LongLive improves persistent-state accuracy of LongLive-2.0, its base generator, by 26.3 points, protagonist identity by 44.9, and revisited-place consistency by 40.4, at about 1% extra generation time on H100. The full system streams faster than real time on one 8xB200 node with the generator in BF16 (34.3 FPS), keeps image quality stable over 14-minute stories, and improves identity on two MiniMax-H3 generators, one of which, LongLive-H3, also runs in real time with it. To our knowledge, it is the first real-time video generator with text and image memory kept by an LLM agent.

Residual Cross-Model Narrative Convergence under Shared Prompts: Auditing Prompt Controls and Narrative Measurement

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Stories created by LLMs based on the same prompts can be narratively similar to one another. This similarity may reflect constraints imposed by the prompts as well as the models' writing tendencies. The current research analyzes 51,240 stories created by five models in the StoryScope corpus based on 10,248 common prompts. It uses 213 narrative features in seven dimensions to compare cross-model distances between stories generated from the same prompts with distances between stories generated from different but semantically matched prompts. To assess the relationship between narrative features and prompt content, a semantic relevance mask was evaluated against 420 author-annotated prompt-feature judgments (F1 = .813; near-threshold accuracy = .621). The equally weighted mean Residual Convergence Index (RCI) was .409 without masking and .406 with masking. These findings indicate measurable cross-model narrative convergence relative to a semantically matched-prompt baseline, with little change following semantic relevance masking.

When the Proxy Is Wrong: Mismatch-Corrected Bandits for Adaptive Data Sampling

📅 2026-11-25 17:00
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Data allocation methods often use a small proxy model to select training data for a larger target model. However, this proxy can rank data differently from the target, leading to suboptimal training performance. To address this problem, we model the mismatch between the proxy and target models and incorporate it into the allocation decision. To do so, the problem is cast as a multi-fidelity bandit, where each arm is a data intervention. The proxy reward is observed every round, whereas the target reward is revealed only on rare audits of the same update. These paired observations identify arm-wise mismatches without assuming a shared offset or preservation of rankings. Mismatch-corrected bandits correct the proxy with paired residuals, audit only uncertain decisions, and pace the audit budget over training. From a theoretical viewpoint, we prove a regret decomposition and lower bounds under stationarity. These bounds show that proxy-only allocation can incur linear regret and that a minimum number of paired audits is necessary. Under abrupt changes, using the audit budget early can also incur linear regret. A sliding-window policy that spreads audits over training achieves regret of order $\sqrt{Γ_T T\log T}$ where $Γ_T$ denotes the number of change points. Experimentally, we test the approach in a closed-loop proof of concept on CivilComments-WILDS, where BERT-Small guides the training of BERT-Large. Mismatch correction improves worst-group accuracy over a target-only policy with the same target-model budget. Spreading audits over training improves worst-group accuracy by 6.3 to 10.5 percentage points at the same audit count.

Attacking Hard-Label Large Vision-Language Models with Model-Sensitive Adversarial Patch Designs

📅 2026-11-25 14:36
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large vision-language models (LVLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in handling multi-modal downstream tasks, gaining increasing popularity. However, recent studies show that LVLMs are susceptible to both intentional and inadvertent attacks. Existing attackers ideally optimize adversarial perturbations with backpropagated gradients from LVLMs, thus limiting their scalability in practical scenarios as real-world LVLM applications will not provide any LVLM's gradient or details. Motivated by this research gap and counter-practical phenomenon, we propose a novel hard-label attack method for LVLMs, named HardPatch, to generate visual adversarial patches by solely querying the model. Our method provides deeper insights into how to investigate the vulnerability of LVLMs in local visual regions and generate corresponding adversarial substitution under the practical yet challenging hard-label setting. Specifically, we first split each image into uniform patches and mask each of them to individually assess their sensitivity to the LVLM model. Then, according to the descending order of sensitive scores, we iteratively select the most vulnerable patch to initialize noise and estimate gradients with further additive random noises for optimization. In this manner, multiple patches are perturbed until the altered image satisfies the adversarial condition. Extensive LVLM models and datasets are evaluated to demonstrate the adversarial nature of HardPatch. Our empirical observations suggest that with appropriate patch substitution and optimization, HardPatch can craft effective adversarial images to attack hard-label LVLMs.

PeerReveal: Learning to Generalize in AI Peer Review Detection

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Can we distinguish fully AI-generated reviews from those using AI only to rewrite human judgement? Furthermore, can we do so reliably when reviews are generated from diverse AI models and workflows across scientific domains? To this end, we introduce PeerReveal, a new detector that grounds its decisions in learned relationships between a review's claims and reference claims drawn from AI-generated reviews of the same paper, complemented by review-level textual signals. We evaluate PeerReveal on PeerShift, a new, challenging test set of 5,430 reviews spanning multiple domains, including machine learning, life sciences, and social sciences, combining variation in disciplinary review conventions with diverse AI review-production mechanisms. With regular training, PeerReveal achieves 70.52% pooled Macro-F1 on this benchmark, improving over the strongest baseline by 11.48 points. We develop an effective and practical adversarial training framework that further improves the detector's generalization using a small local generator optimized to produce challenging reviews, raising pooled Macro-F1 from 70.52% to 82.40%. These results show that robust AI-review detection benefits from richer review-level evidence and adversarial training on more challenging generation processes.

DRBench Pro: A Knowledge-Centric Benchmark for Professional Deep Research Agents

📅 2026-11-25 18:25
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Deep research agents gather information from multiple sources and synthesize it into reports on complex, open-ended questions. Existing benchmarks assess report quality along several dimensions, but lack a dedicated knowledge-centric evaluation of which research directions a report covers and how deeply it explores them. We introduce DRBench Pro, a benchmark that makes structured knowledge coverage the primary evaluation target. Our automated pipeline builds local knowledge trees from Wikipedia and uses sampled subgraphs to generate research queries and evidence-grounded rubrics. Each rubric corresponds to a relation in the tree, preserving the structure of the knowledge being evaluated. Agents receive only the research query. This structure allows us to measure breadth across major research directions and depth along related knowledge chains, with their geometric mean giving overall coverage. The benchmark includes 144 cases across 24 domains and 44,102 rubrics. Evaluating 16 deep research agents reveals different strengths in breadth and depth, with the best-performing agent reaching 57.04% overall coverage. Rubric judgments agree closely with human annotations, and agent rankings remain stable across the tested metric configurations.

$μ$MPC: Microsecond-Scale Nonlinear and World Model Control from Edge to GPU via Koopman-Linearized Precompilation

📅 2026-11-25 15:56
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present $μ$MPC, a fast model predictive control (MPC) framework that reduces online computation using learned Koopman-linearized dynamics. By precomputing finite-horizon control-to-output maps, $μ$MPC eliminates lifted-state trajectories, yielding an action-only Koopman-Compiled QP (KC-QP) with $Hn_u$ decision variables, independent of lifted dimension. A fixed, precomputed primal operator enables efficient primal-dual iterations using only matrix-vector products and projections, accommodating changing goals and constraints without refactorization. This efficiency also enables control-aware dynamics learning through differentiable KC-QPs in the training loop and batched DAgger-style data aggregation. $μ$MPC achieves solve times as low as $23μ$s on a desktop CPU and 24 ms onboard a 168 MHz Crazyflie microcontroller. It scales to 4096D quadruped control in under 5 ms, visual planning with 2048D world models in 0.4 ms, and collision-aware planning for 200 quadrotors at over 30 Hz on the GPU. Across these benchmarks, $μ$MPC achieves up to $108\times$ faster constrained MPC and $1907\times$ faster visuomotor planning than competing methods.

DexEngine: Scaling Human Demonstrations into Physically Executable Whole-Robot Data

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A growing body of recent work addresses the retargeting of human hand-object demonstrations to robotic hands. To train whole-robot policies, however, the retargeted motions must be kinematically feasible and physically executable, and be produced efficiently at scale. We present DexEngine, a simulation pipeline that turns human manipulation data into train-ready, physically executable arm-hand trajectories. DexEngine first places each clip's hand-object motion where the arms can reach it, finds an arm motion that follows the human hands, and then corrects the motion in physics simulation with residual reinforcement learning. From nine human hand-object datasets, we generate about 5,200 clips for each of four dexterous hands, augmented into 65,000 trajectories (116 h) and 325,000 episodes (578 h) in total. Even with arms attached, DexEngine tracks objects more accurately than prior floating-hand methods and runs over 10x faster than RL-based ones. Policies trained on our data far outperform those trained on the same clips without physics correction or with the arms attached after a floating-hand correction, showing that fine-tuning data need physics correction. Mid-training on our data further improves them at equal fine-tuning budget, in simulation and on a real robot.

ProbFaS: Probabilistic Video Frame Selection for MLLMs via Multi-Fidelity Calibration

📅 2026-11-25 18:53
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Processing long videos with multimodal large language models (MLLMs) is prohibitively expensive, making informative frame selection essential for accurate video understanding. The default uniform sampling often misses task-relevant keyframes, while recent training-free selectors instead pick frames by how well each one matches the question under an image-text alignment (ITA) score. However, we show that ITA signals correspond poorly with the MLLM's true needs: their rankings correlate weakly with downstream accuracy and are even negatively correlated on over 40% of questions, whereas the MLLM's internal yes/no judgment yields a much stronger signal. We present ProbFaS, a training- and label-free probabilistic pipeline that fuses both signals for key frame selection. ProbFaS treats dense ITA scores as cheap, biased measurements and sparse MLLM judgments as accurate, costly ones, unifying them via co-kriging, a multi-fidelity Gaussian process, to calibrate relevance across all frames. A temporal determinantal point process then selects frames capturing relevance peaks while preserving temporal coverage. Across Video-MME, MLVU and LongVideoBench, ProbFaS is the most accurate among fourteen state-of-the-art training-free selectors and uniform sampling in 13 of 15 settings (8-128 frames), outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 3.1 percentage points. On MLVU and LongVideoBench, 32 ProbFaS frames match or surpass the top baseline at 128 frames and uniform sampling with up to 768 frames.

SysBio-SR: Benchmarking Symbolic Regression for Trajectory Fidelity and Mechanism Recovery in Systems Biology

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Symbolic regression seeks to discover interpretable governing equations for dynamical systems from data. In systems biology, however, accurately reproducing system trajectories does not necessarily imply recovery of the underlying reaction mechanisms. Existing benchmarks evaluate numerical accuracy, expression complexity, or equation-level similarity, but not agreement between recovered reactions and reference mechanisms in biological systems. We introduce SysBio-SR, a benchmark of 198 literature-based biological ODE systems with reaction annotations spanning mass action, Michaelis--Menten, Hill, and complex kinetics. Trajectory fidelity is evaluated across all systems under in-distribution reconstruction and out-of-distribution (OOD) extrapolation, while mechanism recovery is assessed on the subset of 161 systems. We evaluate trajectory fidelity across 11 symbolic-discovery methods and assess mechanism recovery for three reaction-based methods. Joint analysis across systems highlights how severely trajectory-based evaluation overestimates true mechanism recovery. Motivated by these findings, we develop KineSelect, a framework that searches candidate reaction topologies under biologically grounded kinetic laws to select optimal mechanisms that balance trajectory fidelity with structural parsimony. Compared with the strongest baseline, KineSelect improves aggregate OOD extrapolation \(R^2\) by 19.2\% and overall reaction F1 score by 14.3\%.

ConTra: A Training Framework for Effective Contextual Retrieval via Prefix Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Data management systems organize large text collections for tasks such as finding relevant studies and extracting contract clauses. Systems make long documents searchable by splitting them into independently indexed chunks. Chunking can separate text from its identifying details. The indexing challenge is to keep each chunk independently searchable while preserving the context needed to find and rank it. Existing methods either encode that context in chunk vectors or attach it as a text prefix. Context encoded only in vectors is unavailable to text rerankers. Prompted prefixes serve both vector search and reranking, but are costly to generate at indexing time and can underperform contextual encoders. We propose ConTra (Context That Travels), a reinforcement learning framework for training lightweight prefix generators. Its reward combines feedback from frozen embedding and reranking models with factual verification and length control. ConTra generates one prefix per chunk during index construction and stores the augmented text and its embedding for reuse across queries. To evaluate contextual retrieval across workloads and under interference, we introduce RealContextBench, a benchmark with five application collections and three controlled conditions that isolate the effect of distracting text. On ConTEB and RealContextBench, ConTra-2B outperforms the prompted 9B baseline using Anthropic's method and the PPLX-4B contextual encoder, with a margin of up to 3.15 points over their best overall scores, while indexing short documents 7.74 times and 2.15 times as fast as those baselines, respectively. Post-trained only on ConTEB, it leads the standalone methods on RealContextBench after reranking and is less sensitive than the prompted 2B baseline to random and topically similar distractors. Code, datasets, and trained models are available at https://github.com/SStan1/ConTra.

Rethinking Sequential Locate-Then-Edit: Optimality and Stability

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The Locate-Then-Edit framework enables efficient knowledge updates in large language models without requiring full retraining. Recent research has increasingly focused on sequential editing, where knowledge arrives continuously and the model is updated incrementally. However, existing sequential methods commonly suffer from catastrophic forgetting and model collapse after only a few hundred edits. In contrast, batch editing - which jointly optimizes all edits and yields the optimal solution under a specified editing objective - can support tens of thousands of edits with high edit efficacy while preserving the model's general capabilities, resulting in a substantial performance gap between the two editing paradigms. In this paper, we demonstrate that this gap can be closed via a batch-equivalent sequential editing approach. Moreover, we investigate the underlying mechanism of model degradation in existing sequential methods by analyzing the convergence behavior of weight perturbations as edits accumulate. Our analysis reveals that current methods typically exhibit linear or logarithmic growth in perturbation, whereas the proposed batch-equivalent method achieves time-invariant growth, thereby theoretically guaranteeing long-term stability and preserving the integrity of pretrained knowledge throughout massive-scale sequential editing.

ShowNow: Fine-Grained Speculative Streaming for Responsive World Models

📅 2026-11-25 16:52
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Interactive world models must generate video continuously while responding promptly to user actions. Real-time throughput alone leaves input latency unresolved: coarse generation blocks delay action updates, and generation after input adds work to the response path. We introduce ShowNow, a fine-grained speculative streaming framework that uses computation available during playback to prepare action-conditioned futures before input arrives. Single-latent commitment enables frequent decisions, while configurable preparation covers one to eight actions. We construct a matched multi-future dataset to train alternative continuations from a common state; shared-history tree execution and fused commitment make their preparation efficient. On a single H100, our 5B model sustains 480p generation at 16 fps with eight prepared futures. In ViZDoom, the complete ShowNow deployment reduces median input-to-display latency from 620 to 208 ms relative to the native-block SolarWM-FT deployment. Matched-policy experiments reduce restart empty ticks from 1.56% to 0.09% (94%) and improve on-time responses to action changes. These results establish complementary roles for fine-grained commitment and advance preparation, with measured trade-offs in visual fidelity and long-horizon consistency. ShowNow turns playback-time computation into responsive visual interaction. Project: https://github.com/showlab/ShowNow.

Reading Fine-Tuning Diffs Through Lenses: Model Diffing for Backdoor Detection

📅 2026-11-25 17:28
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We introduce a common scoring system based on token readouts to evaluate weight, activation and output-based model diffing methods for backdoor detection in fine-tuning processes. We also improve existing weight and activation based methods by incorporating the $J$-lens and its refinement, the $J{+}{+}$-lens. We test our setup in experiments where we compare a backdoored model with the base model from which it was fine-tuned. Our results show that incorporating the $J{+}{+}$-lens improves the weight-based method in all our settings, while the $J$-lens improves its ranks within single layers. The strongest single method is $ΔW J{+}{+}$, the weight difference decoded through the $J{+}{+}$-lens of the base model, which ranks a token naming the backdoor among its first ten tokens in 11 of our 12 settings. Moreover, we show that output and activation based methods such as ADL and Diff Mining can easily break if the backdoored model is penalised during fine-tuning for giving the backdoor's words probability on clean inputs, whereas the weight-based methods are not affected. Conversely, permuting the hidden units or training only the layers that read the residual stream hides the backdoor from the weight-based methods but not from ADL and Diff Mining. No single method resists both attacks.

Letting Models Manage Their Own Context: Where We Stand

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agent harnesses today manage context on the model's behalf: when the window fills, the harness triggers a compaction that summarizes the history. We study the alternative, \emph{model-driven} context management, in which the model itself decides when and what to compact through a context API. We gave models control over their own context in \nooa, NVIDIA's open source agent harness, and compared the two strategies on document research and machine learning engineering across context budgets from 8K to 512K tokens. Models that use the context API effectively reach higher accuracy, often with fewer input tokens, especially at tight budgets: \textbf{GPT 6.1 nearly triples its score at a 32K budget} on OfficeQA Pro v2 (21\%~$\rightarrow$~56\%), and \textbf{Kimi gains with up to 41\% fewer input tokens}. Models differ widely, however, in how often they use the API, how aggressively they compact, and when they start compacting, and prompt changes shift that timing. Finally, we show that \textbf{inference engines can make model-driven edits cheaper}: by reusing the KV cache of the unchanged suffix after an edit, \textbf{we cut prefill compute by up to 15\% per request with no measurable change in accuracy}. Together, these results point to a new co-design frontier: models trained to manage their own context, served by inference engines that support KV cache editing.

Noise threshold for learning with quantum memory

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A quantum memory can reduce exponentially the number of experiments needed to learn a physical process, but real memories are noisy. Here we show that, for Pauli memory noise with eigenvalues $λ_X,λ_Y,λ_Z$, the advantage is governed by $λ_X^2+λ_Y^2+λ_Z^2$. We prove that no protocol with an entangled probe gains an exponential advantage when this sum is at most one, reduce the general case to the additivity of a Rényi-2 coherent information, and find coded memories that retain a growing advantage within 1% of this threshold. On a superconducting processor, nine simultaneously operated Bell pairs show an advantage that grows or shrinks exponentially with system size, reversing where a parameter-free model predicts. A three-qubit repetition code turns a shrinking advantage into a growing one with all processor and readout errors included, and under dephasing its memory performs better than Bell sampling in any memory frame on a perfect processor.

DFly2: Turbocharging Long-Context Speculative Decoding by Sharing the Target's Sparsity

📅 2026-11-25 17:37
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

To avoid the quadratic cost of dense attention over long contexts, models such as DeepSeek-V4 and Hy4 have turned natively sparse, and speculative decoding, which matters most where decoding is slowest, increasingly runs on them. Their drafters, however, still keep a context cache of their own, over the whole prompt or a sliding window. Beyond memory, such a cache costs prefill time, prefix-cache hits and decode concurrency. Drafters that read the target's cache avoid one of their own, but settle only which cache, not which positions, and still read everything or a recent window. Neither rule is made for the query at hand, and computing a selection per query in the drafter would cost more as the context grows. Yet a natively sparse target already picks positions for each query, over a cache it keeps anyway. Both of the drafter's choices are therefore already made, and paid for, by the target: its cache and its selection. We measure how far one selection carries: made a step early, it still covers close to nine tenths of what each later token selects on DS-V4.1-Flash. We present DFly2, a block-parallel drafter that shares the target's sparsity. Each draft layer reads the KV its paired target layer cached (KV sharing), at the positions that layer's indexer selected, a fixed number anywhere in the context (Index sharing). DFly2 thus runs no scoring pass and keeps no cache of its own. On DS-V4.1-Flash, GLM-5.3-Flash and Hy4~flash, whose sparse attention and architecture differ, it accepts more tokens per step than DFlash and DSpark trained on the same recipe, over general, long-context and agentic benchmarks. Its margin over the stronger baseline widens from 3.9%-10.5% on general workloads to 10.0%-31.5% on long-context and agentic ones. At high concurrency on DS-V4.1-Flash, at matched per-user decode speed, its throughput is 62% and 81% higher on two 64K sets.

One Token, Many Lessons: Sliding-Window Attention within Gated Delta Networks

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Linear attention compresses historical context into a fixed-size recurrent state, reducing the computational and memory costs of long-sequence modeling. From the perspective of test-time regression (TTR), the state matrix can be viewed as an online-learned regression model, where keys and values serve as inputs and targets respectively, and queries are used to read from the model. From this perspective, the effectiveness of state learning depends not only on the update rule but also on how the key-value learning pairs used for state updates are constructed. While existing work has largely focused on how the state is updated and organized, we investigate how the learning pairs used for state updates are constructed. To this end, we propose Sliding-Window Gated DeltaNet (SWGDN), which replaces short convolutions in Delta-Rule linear attention with sliding-window attention to perform content-dependent local aggregation of projected queries, keys, and values. Our experiments show that SWGDN makes the key-value pairs used in consecutive updates more similar in both GDN2 and KDA. In SWGDN, carrying information from the same token across updates can help the recurrent state reinforce a previously learned association. The local mixing strategy can be incorporated into any Delta-Rule-based linear attention architecture without changing its state update equations or chunkwise parallel algorithms. We apply this strategy to two Delta-Rule variants, GDN2 and KDA, and experimental results demonstrate that SWGDN outperforms the original Delta-Rule baseline across multiple benchmarks.

When Rerankers Work Together: Better and Faster Reranking without Joint Training

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Standalone reranker quality does not determine its value in a multistage RAG pipeline. We systematically study heterogeneous rerankers composed without joint training, spanning lexical, single-vector, late-interaction, and joint query-context scoring. Two lightweight semantic coarse scorers, Cache-Pointwise and Cache-Shapley, learn from passage relevance labels and share cacheable representations and linear-time singleton inference. Under matched candidates and budgets, we compare cascades with stage-removal references to measure evidence retention, repair and breakage, reader accuracy, and online pruning cost. On NoLiMa 32K with Gemma, inserting these scorers before CrossEncoder or Provence improves answer accuracy by 4.02-9.43 percentage points while accelerating online pruning by 1.57-1.88x relative to the corresponding reranker alone. Cache-Pointwise composed with ColBERTv2 through reciprocal rank fusion improves accuracy by 4.60 points at 1.36x speedup over direct ColBERTv2. The stage-removal comparisons reveal an asymmetry: a cascade can outperform its final reranker alone while underperforming its coarse selector alone. Two readers, a second multi-hop dataset, and three-stage extensions show that the gains depend on the reader, reranker pairing, and stage configuration. These findings demonstrate a practical route to better and faster reranking without joint training and motivate studying stage order, candidate budgets, and rank fusion as determinants of composition quality.

Formalization, Transparency, and Collusion in Open-Source Game Theory

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Cooperation among AI agents will undermine alignment protocols when it takes the form of collusion. Under these protocols, agents often have some information about one another, and how much they have may determine whether they collude. Open-source game theory studies the extreme case, in which each program reads its opponent's source code and acts based on what it can prove about it. We develop formal foundations for this theory: we specify a concrete proof system, resolve an open problem concerning DupocBot and CupodBot through a transposition argument, and mechanize the framework in Lean 4; to our knowledge, this is the first mechanization of resource-bounded open-source game theory. We then extend the framework to the more realistic setting of partial transparency, where each program receives only partial information about its opponent. In Prisoner's Dilemma experiments, where mutual cooperation models collusion, greater transparency increases both the cooperation sustainable in equilibrium and the cooperation selected by replicator dynamics. This suggests that controlling how much agents can know about one another matters for alignment protocols.

Recalling the Past to Answer the Present: Context-Aware Multi-Evidence Reasoning for Streaming Video Question Answering

📅 2026-11-25 15:24
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in video understanding, yet existing video question answering (VQA) benchmarks mainly evaluate models in offline settings. Real-world streaming scenarios instead require models to answer context-dependent queries by retrieving and reasoning over evidence distributed across the video history. We introduce StreamRecall, a benchmark for context-aware Multi evidence reasoning in streaming video QA. StreamRecall requires models to retrieve, associate, and reason over temporally scattered evidence to answer queries at the appropriate moment. It contains 4,983 questions over 462 videos, with context-aligned query times and fine-grained temporal evidence annotations. We further propose the Evidence Utilization Cascade to diagnose model reasoning through evidence localization, grounding, chain completion, and answer derivation. To enable efficient long-term streaming reasoning, we present EviStream, a training-free causal visual memory mechanism that preserves reasoning-critical evidence under a fixed KV budget. Experiments reveal substantial gaps between current VLMs and realistic streaming requirements, establishing StreamRecall as a foundation for developing evidence-aware streaming video intelligence.

Psychophysics of Duration Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Studies of time perception in language models focus mainly on text-based settings. Yet multimodal models are increasingly used for tasks that require perceiving the passage of time, making the limits of their temporal perception important to understand. We measure the limits of duration perception from audio against a human baseline to address this gap. Drawing from psychophysics, we operationalise duration perception as a melody recognition task, testing when a melody becomes unrecognisable as it is slowed down. Of 21 tested models, most cannot recognise well-known melodies without labelled in-context examples. With in-context examples, seven recognise melodies reliably, most peaking within the median human recognition band. Generalisation is limited: (i) recognition peaks shift with in-context example speed, (ii) melody titles induce prediction bias, and (iii) providing examples with scientific pitch notation does not prevent accuracy declining at long note durations for most models. In humans, the recognition window is a property of the perceiver; in current models, it is largely a property of the prompt.

CA-RAG: Constraint-Aligned Retrieval for Structurally Faithful Long-Form Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) typically optimises evidence selection for semantic relevance, whereas compliance-sensitive long-form generation also requires evidence supporting explicit structural obligations. Semantically relevant retrieval may therefore omit information needed for mandatory downstream clauses. We propose Constraint-Aligned RAG (CA-RAG), a retrieval-conditioning framework that augments section-specific queries with ontology-derived structural constraints and policy-grounded knowledge-graph relations before evidence selection. Retrieval-only evaluation with manually annotated evidence shows consistent improvements across retrieval budgets. At $k=6$, CA-RAG increases Precision@$k$ from $0.5268$ to $0.5692$, pooled Recall@$k$ from $0.5402$ to $0.5950$, and F1@$k$ from $0.5389$ to $0.5672$ over standard RAG. Generation experiments further show improvements in compliance and reliability in several model--domain settings, while generation-only constraints achieve higher clause coverage but lower overall compliance and reliability. These results indicate that structurally aligned retrieval can improve evidence sufficiency in compliance-sensitive long-form generation.

PowerFix: Physics-Guided Iterative Repair for LLM-Assisted Power Converter Design

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly being explored for automated engineering design. However, repairing power-converter designs that fail engineering validation remains challenging: the system must identify physical causes, locate responsible design variables, and resolve violations without compromising other constraints. We introduce PowerFix, a physics-guided iterative repair framework that converts validation failures into structured diagnoses and executable patches while preserving topology and design requirements. PowerDiagGraph links failed checks to possible physical causes and constrains repair routes. An LLM prioritizes these routes, while a deterministic compiler translates selected repairs into authorized field-level edits. Candidates are validated on isolated design copies and committed only after all required engineering checks pass; failed validation can guide replanning. On our constructed 96-task flyback repair benchmark, comprising injected single faults, coupled faults, and held-out natural failures from our design generator, PowerFix achieves 78.7% verified repair success rate (VRSR), compared with 30.1% for the strongest of three LLM baselines. Three additional replanning rounds increase VRSR to 88.4%. These offline results provide pilot-stage evidence within a simulation-based design workflow, with hardware prototype validation and transfer to a second converter topology as the next steps toward deployment.

How Much Do Coding Agents Over-Edit, and Why? An Empirical Study of Unneeded Edits in Repository-Level Agent Patches

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agent patches are larger than developers', which is widely read as over-editing. But patch size does not say whether the extra edits are needed, what they are, or where they come from. We introduce Delta Residue Attribution (DRA), which replays agent trajectories, traces every block (unit) of the final patch to the step that wrote it, reverts each unit, and reruns the task tests to decide whether the unit is needed. We apply DRA to 7,660 resolved GitHub Copilot CLI trajectories from 18 frontier models on SWE-bench Verified (6,728) and DeepSWE (932), and grade developer patches using the same method. Agent patches do change more lines than developers'; on SWE-bench Verified the majority of extra edits add tests (61%) and documentation (13%) while on DeepSWE, most extra edits are code (58%). On both benchmarks, agents' code is on average at least as necessary as developers' (mean $NP_{code}$ 0.887 vs. 0.864 on SWE-bench Verified, where 11 of 18 models leave less unneeded code than developers, and 0.797 vs. 0.785 on DeepSWE). On average, 11% of an agent patch's code units on SWE-bench Verified are still unneeded, and 89% of those change behavior. Debug steps write almost none of the unneeded code. Code necessity differs substantially by model; it is unrelated to resolve rate on SWE-bench Verified and negatively correlated with it on DeepSWE (Spearman -0.59, p = 0.01). On SWE-bench Verified, pruning with DRA using only the tests the agent can see (its own tests and the repository's) removes the majority of unneeded code but also some needed code (67% recall, 46% precision), and 89% of pruned patches still resolve the task; asking the agent to audit its own diff is more precise but less effective (36% recall, 66% precision). We recommend that benchmarks report code necessity next to resolve rate and that over-editing be addressed through model training and harness-level pruning.

Do Machine Learning Ensembles Beat Published Quotas? Target Parameterization, Censored Baselines, and Conformal Intervals Under Admissions Reform

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Public admission capacity caps published prior to matriculation are widely used by ministries and institutional analysts as default intake projections in centralized postsecondary clearinghouses. Using an audited 2022 to 2026 Vietnamese undergraduate panel (26,895 program-year records across 309 universities) linked to strictly lagged t-1 occupational vacancy signals, this paper evaluates machine learning, multilevel, and censored econometric estimators against published quotas across 5,385 continuing 2024 programs (five-fold university-grouped cross-validation) and 3,348 matched (2,179 grain-consistent) 2025 programs strictly out of time. Because 62.8% of 2024 programs enroll their exact published quota, unanchored log-growth ensembles degrade accuracy, while the worst 5% generate half of total quota error via unfilled "phantom capacity." Controlled target ablations, including a single-factor test under widened non-binding clipping, show that parameterizing the log fill ratio ln(D_it / S_it) instead of log growth reduces 2024 MAE by 21.5% (14.86 to 11.67), aligns ML with Tobit, Cragg, REML HLM, and capped L1 baselines, and lifts combined tuition and institutional-tier TreeSHAP attribution from 2.5% to 45.8%. Hurdle stacking attains the lowest full-census 2024 MAE (H-Hard 11.29; H-Soft 11.32 vs. 12.35, p = 0.022, clearing 10% not 5% FDR; fold-overlap p = 0.134), whereas continuous QA-Stack outperforms H-Soft on mixed-reporting campuses, Tobit (median) ties H-Soft across both 90% MCS sets, and interior-only Cragg-ML lowers RMSE to 28.1. Under Vietnam's 2025 unified-clearing reform, exact filling falls to 10.4%, H-Soft and capped L1 tie quotas (p = 0.979), and 90% cluster-weighted conformal coverage drops from 89.3% to 74.6%. Across 257 reporting campuses, reconciled 2026 national totals (627,114 to 629,256) lie squarely within 604,533 to 662,044.

How Much Effort Does an AI Code Reviewer Need? Evidence from Claude 5.5 Models on Plausible-but-Wrong Patches

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.

EgoUseBench: Benchmarking Computer-Use Agents under Evolving First-Person Contexts

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI wearables, such as smart glasses, extend the context available to agents beyond digital environments to users' physical surroundings. With these devices, computer tasks can originate from users' ongoing activities rather than solely from explicit instructions. Through first-person observations, agents can identify objects and activities relevant to user requests and ground digital tasks in the surrounding context. We call this interaction paradigm Ego Use. However, first-person observations are limited by the wearer's viewpoint and may provide incomplete or ambiguous evidence of ongoing activities. As new observations arrive, agents may need to revise their understanding of the physical context and user intent, and reassess whether prior digital actions remain valid. Whether current agents can reliably handle these changes remains insufficiently evaluated. We introduce EgoUseBench, a benchmark for evaluating Ego Use across three interaction settings with different levels of first-person context. Computer-Use relies on explicit instructions, Offline Ego Use provides prerecorded first-person context before execution, and Online Ego Use provides continuous observations as user intent evolves. Based on over 74 hours of real-world first-person recordings, we construct 693 tasks across the three interaction settings. To capture task evolution, we track first-person observations, user requirements, and website state separately. We further group tasks by how information changes during execution, resulting in three types: Graft (revised user requirements), Refresh (new first-person observations), and Clarify (agent-initiated information seeking). We evaluate advanced models and find that average success rates in Online Ego Use are 26.3% lower than in Computer-Use. This result reveals the difficulty of keeping task execution aligned with evolving first-person context and user intent.

FlashProve-Next: Verifiable Inference for Production LLM APIs

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Clients of closed-weight LLM APIs cannot verify whether a provider ran the advertised model, precision, hidden prompt, and reasoning budget. Existing approaches either trust hardware or third parties, or re-prove every layer of an integer surrogate at high cost while diverging from native floating-point serving. FlashProve-Next produces verifiable, zero-knowledge-masked proofs for individual LLM API responses without modifying native serving (bf16/fp8/mxfp4, random sampling, FlashAttention, CUDA graphs, batching, prefix caching) and without disclosing weights, hidden prompts, hidden reasoning, or activations (dense profiles fix coarse geometry; cross-architecture hiding is deferred). A non-depleting Reed-Solomon weight commitment model_id opens inside proofs via hidden column openings, while local per-operator rounding contracts remove integer surrogates and the refusal-bit leak. Cost-weighted spot-checking supports non-interactive public verification (NI-315, $\varepsilon \le 2^{-40}$, $Q \le 2^{64}$) and external-seed C_pre mode (ExtSeed-153, $\varepsilon \le 2^{-20}$, $Q_{\rm samp} \le 2^{30}$). Twenty-eight Lean 4 modules (696 declarations, zero sorry, standard axioms only) mechanize round-by-round soundness, bucketed sampling, $\mathbb{F}_{p^3}$ constrained-mask binding, causal masking, six IR gadgets, and executable FPN2 verifiers. Five-model Blackwell calibration gives zero false rejections across 1.85M GEMM/MoE blocks and 1.96M non-GEMM rows. Our zero-Phase-2 folded backend (folded ZK simulator open) proves Qwen3-0.6B ExtSeed-153 (1.47B cells, 8 MiB) in 450.2 s GPU (10.76 s CPU) at unique-decoding $Q=128$, passes a 14/14 cheating matrix, and scales to 36-layer Qwen3-4B (dev $4+2$) and live vLLM Qwen3-30B-A3B-FP8 Relation 6 (1.49 s prove, 23.4 ms Rust verify, public weight tiles). Rust fpn-verify checks 8 MiB FPN2 Relation 2 proofs in 10.1 ms on CPU.

From Shared Patterns to Individual Evidence: Two-Stage Reasoning for Emerging Entities in Temporal Knowledge Graphs

📅 2026-11-25 17:01
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs) continually introduce emerging entities whose interaction histories evolve from zero-shot debuts into sparse, bursty few-shot trajectories. However, current paradigms are fragmented: pattern-transfer methods lack catalog-wide event memory, whereas online memory models suffer from endpoint-role asymmetry that degrades unseen-answer ranking. Furthermore, sparse early interactions exhibit both long-range dispersion and local redundancy under skewed distribution. To address these challenges, we propose INSPIRE (Integrating Shared Patterns and Individual Evidence for Reasoning), a two-stage forecasting framework over a known catalog. In Stage I, INSPIRE learns an availability-aware shared-pattern predictor that fuses event-updated memory across the full candidate catalog while applying coordinated query- and catalog-side masking. Subsequently, Stage II freezes this predictor and optimizes a lightweight individual-evidence adapter (EA) combining textual semantic priors with correlation-weighted long-range event statistics under support- and role-balanced co-answer supervision with prior-odds debiasing and soft residual alignment. In addition, we decompose evaluation by endpoint roles and track longitudinal emerging-answer cohorts via query-micro and answer-entity macro metrics. Across four benchmarks based on the ICEWS and GDELT event datasets, INSPIRE improves Emerging MRR by 16.73%-88.83% over the strongest baselines. Relative to paired Stage I predictors, EA further increases Emerging MRR by 8.28%-32.63%, Answer-new MRR by 7.81%-41.24%, and cohort macro MRR by 9.64%-39.94%, gains attributed to semantic priors and candidate-level score competition suppressing mismatched established competitors without zero-support prior shortcuts. The official code is publicly available at https://github.com/KcAcoZhang/INSPIRE.

What, Who, and When: Structured Harm Assessment for Mental Health Safety via Short Reasoning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The growing use of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support raises important safety concerns. Existing guard models primarily identify categories of harmful content, often overlooking the context needed to determine an appropriate response. We introduce a clinically grounded taxonomy that characterizes harm along three dimensions: what the harm is, who is involved, and when it occurs. It further separates harm characterization from intervention decisions, thus enabling safety policies to be specified outside the model weights through explicit, clinician-curated instructions that can be updated and audited. We validate the taxonomy with expert clinicians and validate a panel of LLM judges to scale annotation. Relabeling public benchmarks in this taxonomy shows that they rarely contain harm directed at the user, which accounts for nearly half of the harm in real conversations. We then train MindGuard2, a 35B-A3B reasoning-based guard model using supervised fine-tuning on short reasoning traces followed by reinforcement learning with a reasoning-length penalty, to minimize inference overhead while preserving the benefits of reasoning. On two public benchmarks and real-world conversations, MindGuard2 achieves balanced accuracy comparable to the substantially larger GLM-5.3 at approximately 10x lower median latency, advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier. Furthermore, it achieves low false-positive rates at high recall, helping balance the need to detect unsafe situations against the risk of unnecessary interventions that may disrupt support and undermine user trust. We release a safety guardrail model with SOTA performance trained on synthetic data, together with synthetic conversations and annotations of public benchmarks in our taxonomy.

Cairn: Object-Centric 3D Trajectory Priors for Steering Frozen VLAs

📅 2026-11-25 18:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language-action models (VLAs) are capable low-level controllers, but they tie where objects go to the layouts they were trained on: when objects are moved or obstacles block the way, they carry them to the wrong place or into collisions. Fine-tuning on new layouts needs robot data for each one and erodes skills the model already has. We introduce Cairn, which keeps the VLA frozen and steers it at test time along learned 3D trajectories of the object in its gripper. A small prior reads point clouds of the scene and the held object, along with its pose when grasped and the instruction, and samples SE(3) trajectories up to its release that lead it around obstacles and turn it to fit through gaps. The prior and the VLA form the two levels of a hierarchical planner in which feedback runs both ways: the VLA's intended motion chooses and reshapes the trajectories, and the chosen one guides the VLA's denoising. Its predicted actions, mapped to the held object's poses through the grasp, are pulled toward the next waypoint, while the VLA's weights stay untouched. Across 1,941 held-out LIBERO scenes with displaced objects and obstacles, Cairn raises the success rate of a frozen π0.5 from 24.5% to 95.1%, above that of a π0.5 fine-tuned on our training layouts, without hurting standard LIBERO. On the third-party LIBERO-PRO benchmark, it raises success from 27.3% to 38.0%, and from 54.4% to 81.1% on the episodes it steers. On a real Franka, with a prior trained purely in simulation, it guides the frozen DROID π0.5 around an obstacle and to a placement at the limit of the arm's reach, where it otherwise fails. All code, models and data will be made public.

LUDIUS: A Benchmark for Embodied Social Intelligence in Everyday Human-Robot Interactions

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Social intelligence in a robot requires interpreting a situation from the perspectives of the people involved and using that understanding to decide when and how to act. We introduce LUDIUS, a benchmark for evaluating how vision-language models exercise this judgment through a robot's verbal responses and actions, organized around 15 social intelligence skills drawn from social psychology, sociology, and cognitive science. LUDIUS comprises six editions, each a collection of episodes spanning one simulated week. Three editions each cover all 15 skills in a different household. The other three focus on interactions involving multiple people, safety, and context-specific norms, respectively. We evaluate frontier models and a smaller model finetuned on social-interaction data we generated, using a common evaluation harness. The harness supplies each model with the robot's camera views and residents' reactions, retrieves relevant interaction history, and converts the model's tool calls into robot verbal responses and actions. Models tend to perform worse when they must reason about what a resident saw or heard earlier, reconcile competing interests, or fulfill a request within safety constraints and household rules. Beyond the home setting, LUDIUS's design pipeline offers a way to construct social intelligence benchmarks by translating social intelligence skills into context-specific judgments and observable robot behavior.

Evidence-Aligned Benchmarking and Learning for Medical Video Language Models

📅 2026-11-25 14:35
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A medical video language model should answer from the visual evidence its question asks about. We find three places where this breaks down and address each with an approach we call MedEvidU. First, benchmarks reward answers that ignore the video: on one dataset, the position of a clip within its recording predicts the surgical step in 37% of questions, and a constant answer beats every trained model on a standard safety-assessment metric. We build MedVidBench-v2, 34 tasks from 18 surgical and clinical video datasets, in which prompts and metrics are audited with answers given without video, and which tests unseen hospitals and windows cut blindly from whole recordings. Second, common video encoders merge consecutive frames into shared tokens, so a question about one frame is answered from a blend of two. Query-slot inputs give each queried frame its own tokens at almost no extra cost. Third, a fine-tuned model has memorized its training questions, which leaves little to learn from. Label-matched question resampling (LQR) adds unseen questions about the same recordings while keeping the answer distribution fixed, in a final supervised epoch and in reinforcement learning. Together, these raise the grounding IoU of a 4B model from 30.4 to 44.4, above a supervised 27B model, and instrument grounding at an unseen hospital from 33.9 to 72.5. On blindly cut windows, event-presence accuracy improves by 5.5 points and localization F1 by 8.1 points. Project page: https://gaozhongpai.github.io/MedEvidU/

LLM-coupled interpretable tool wear monitoring with multi-evidence reasoning in CNC machining

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Interpretable tool wear assessment in CNC machining is challenging. Existing diagnostic models have high accuracy but provide limited guidance for tool replacement. This study proposes a two layer framework combining deep learning wear classification and LLM assisted reasoning. The first layer uses PCA Loading feature selection and optimized GRU to recognize three wear states, achieving 98.38 percent macro F1 score on the PHM2010 dataset. The second layer uses LLMs to generate natural language reports and replacement suggestions from classification results and feature trends.
