Tracking Any Point with Agentic Visual Reasoning

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A general-purpose Vision-Language Model (VLM) has surpassed expert vision models in many domains such as object detection and video recognition. However, point correspondence is known to be challenging for VLMs since it goes beyond object-level recognition: it requires pixel-level localization of the same physical surface point across observations, even when appearance changes or the point disappears and later reappears. In this paper, we introduce AgenTAP, a VLM-based agent that directly predicts point coordinates and visibility and actively reasons over videos. Starting from its own estimate, the agent autonomously chooses what to inspect and uses the resulting evidence to verify point identity and refine its predictions. With GPT-6 Astra, it surpasses expert vision models in both point localization and visibility prediction, without correspondence-specific fine-tuning. We further use AgenTAP to re-identify physical points after disappearance and reconnect fragmented tracks, constructing EgoTAP-4D, a dataset of 3D point trajectories in long real-world egocentric videos. On sampled reappearing points, its predictions approach human agreement, and adding EgoTAP-4D to D4RT training improves average 3D tracking performance.

Read Before You Use: Improving Visual Memory Utilization in Vision-Language Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal memory enables long-horizon vision-language agents to preserve and retrieve past experiences. While prior research has focused on memory construction and retrieval, effectively using the provided memory remains a challenge for vision-language models (VLMs). We find that VLMs often fail to answer downstream questions even when sufficient multimodal evidence is provided. To investigate why VLMs struggle, we replace memory images with reference captions and observe substantial performance gains, suggesting difficulties in reading visual evidence directly. However, model-generated captions may omit critical information, limiting this approach in practice. These limitations motivate a closer examination of memory utilization, revealing failures in both evidence reading and downstream question answering. Building on this distinction, we propose \textbf{MemRU}, a two-stage training framework that improves evidence reading through supervised fine-tuning (Read SFT) and downstream evidence utilization through Group Relative Policy Optimization (Use GRPO). Experimental results demonstrate that our 8B model generalizes across three unseen multimodal memory systems, outperforming Qwen3-VL-32B and approaching GPT-5-mini.

Ensuring Deployment-Time Safety of Neural Network Controlled Systems via Localized Certificate Repair

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Barrier certificates provide formal safety guarantees for neural network controlled systems by ensuring that system trajectories avoid unsafe regions over time. However, after deployment, previously unseen obstacles or constraint changes may introduce new unsafe regions, invalidating the original certificate and potentially leading to unsafe behavior. We address this setting with adaptive piecewise barrier certificates, which preserve the original certificate and policy in unchanged regions while introducing localized updates around newly emerged unsafe regions. The central question is how to perform such updates efficiently during system execution without retraining from scratch. Our key idea is to cast deployment-time adaptation as a localized certificate repair problem and reduce it to an optimization task that leverages the learned structure of previous barrier certificates, enabling fast updates without global retraining. We further design a staged repair framework that progressively transitions from localized repair to joint policy-certificate updates and, when necessary, localized retraining. Empirical results on four benchmarks with five new obstacles show that our method succeeds in all cases and completes within tens of seconds on average, whereas retraining from scratch fails in most cases. On instances where retraining succeeds, our method is 17.3$\times$ faster on average. It incurs only 16.2% performance degradation relative to the original policy.

QPAT: A Universal Quantum Machine Learning Framework for Quantum-Assisted Training and Classical Inference

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Deep learning models often struggle with sharp local optima and overfitting in limited-data regimes, and exhibit limited capacity for capturing high-order, complex probability distributions. Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models, by operating in exponentially large Hilbert spaces, can efficiently represent such distributions and provide favorable inductive biases for learning, offering a promising pathway to overcome these limitations. However, their practical deployment in the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era is severely hindered by gradient coupling, barren plateaus, data-loading bottlenecks, and high inference latency. To bridge classical AI and QML, we propose QPAT (QCBM-Prior-Assisted Training), a universal framework that employs a Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM) as a learnable generative prior to provide a task-aligned structured inductive bias during classical model training. The framework can be seamlessly integrated into any classical backbone, including LSTMs, CNNs, and Transformers. We introduce a decoupled fitness-based scoring mechanism and a stochastic "Drop-Prior" mechanism to implicitly distill quantum structural knowledge into the classical weights during training. Evaluated extensively on six benchmarks spanning time-series forecasting, computer vision, and natural language processing, our framework consistently outperforms purely classical backbones in both predictive performance and stability across random seeds; ablation studies further show that these gains are not reproduced by unstructured random priors or a classically learned generative prior. Remarkably, our approach enables completely classical inference, bypassing quantum latency while preserving quantum-enhanced accuracy, paving a highly practical path for QML deployment.

Pitfalls of Dependency-Ready Decoding for Masked Diffusion Language Models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Masked diffusion models (MDMs) have shown strong performance on structured reasoning tasks such as Sudoku, Trip Planning, and Countdown. MDMs introduce a freedom in the order in which tokens may be unmasked, and to exercise this freedom for good accuracy, early decoding strategies relied on signals of confidence or the downstream impact of decoding a token. However, simple examples seem to illustrate that resolving a token's dependencies before unmasking it should be important. Therefore, we examine whether this principle, which we call dependency-ready decoding, would contribute to better accuracy, from a practical and a theoretical perspective. We examine a recent decoder that implements dependency-readiness through attention, and generally find that when it performs better than confidence-based or future utility baselines, its decoding order collapses to left-to-right decoding. For theoretical analysis, we establish a definition of dependency without resorting to attention, measured by an information-theoretic score of how much revealing a single masked token (or a set) would shift the model's prediction for another. We prove that when the margin is large relative to this score, no such revelation can change the top prediction, so in this regime Maximum-Margin decoding already commits dependency-ready tokens. These findings leave only a narrow scope for future dependency-ready decoders: their potential benefit is limited to positions with margins smaller than the information-theoretic sensitivity, and reliance on attention should be questioned.

Kolpona: An Agentic Framework with Gradient-Free Neuron Programming for RTL Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Open-weight large language models (LLMs) for register-transfer level (RTL) design are usually improved by fine-tuning on curated Verilog corpora, which needs large sets of verified designs and GPU training. We present Kolpona, an agentic framework that improves such models without gradient training. Its first component is neuron programming, a new form of weight-space programming (WSP) that writes single neurons in closed form into the weights to remove failure habits found on practice tasks, and keeps each edit only when testbench results on fresh tasks favour it. At inference time, tool-using agents propose designs, and a label-free selection stage delivers one design per task from the specification, the candidates and their simulations alone. On Qwen3.5-4B, edits to 16 neurons raise the VerilogEval v2 pass@1 of the model alone from 31.7% to 55.4%, and the full framework reaches 77.6%. On Qwen3.8-27B, Kolpona lifts pass@1 from 73.1% to 94.9% on VerilogEval v2 and from 66.0% to 92.0% on RTLLM v2.1 on a single 32 GB GPU, above every published system in our comparison. The RTL edits also carry over to Python and raise the pass@1 of the 4B model alone from 84.8% to 91.5% on HumanEval+ and from 67.7% to 77.5% on MBPP+.

REE: A Scientific Research Entrance Exam for AI Agents

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Autonomous research agents are emerging as a promising approach to scientific problem-solving across disciplines, owing to their growing ability to improve a solution through repeated experiments. Evaluating this ability requires benchmarks that pose open-ended research problems and measure improvements over established scientific methods. We introduce REE (Research Entrance Exam), a benchmark that tests AI agents on scientific research. It consists of 100 open-ended research problems spanning five broad scientific areas and 23 domains. To ensure high quality, every problem is drawn from active research in its field and individually reviewed by domain experts. Each problem is posed as a task of iterative improvement: an agent has up to 12 hours to improve a working starter solution, and REE scores the result on a common scale against an established expert solution evaluated under comparable conditions. By this measure, two of the four frontier agents we evaluate, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol, outperform the expert solutions on 86% and 81% of problems, across all five scientific areas. Their runs also show how they do so: both agents surpass the expert solutions within the first two hours on average, and their best solutions mostly tailor or combine established methods of the field. REE thus provides a unified framework for evaluating autonomous scientific research, capturing both improvements over established methods and the research processes behind them.

The Socratic PAUSE: safeguarding scientific judgement at AI speed

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generative AI can turn a tentative scientific question into hypotheses, methods, code and interpretations within minutes. Yet generating a contribution does not establish that it deserves scientific reliance: researchers must still examine its evidence, assumptions and implications, and remain accountable for the decision to rely on it. Using Einstein's sketch of scientific discovery as a lens, we argue that AI has accelerated discovery far more than justification. In human-AI research partnerships, confidence can therefore outgrow evidence through three mechanisms: recursive reinforcement, loss of provenance and miscalibrated reliance on AI-generated contributions. Verification debt accumulates when such contributions are carried into later claims or decisions without adequate scrutiny. Accordingly, we propose the Socratic Pause as a deliberate checkpoint at consequential decision gates, where an AI contribution remains provisional while a researcher examines its basis. The PAUSE protocol structures this examination through five steps: Pin down the claim, Ask and challenge, Uncover assumptions and alternatives, Scrutinise the evidence and Exercise judgement. It concludes with a recorded decision to accept, revise, reject or defer the contribution. We show how AI research tools can support this practice without displacing human judgement, and outline a framework for evaluating whether it improves verification, decision-making and independent understanding. The aim is to preserve the freedom to explore with AI while ensuring that those responsible for the work can say what was examined, what remains uncertain and why the next step is justified.

Transferring Architecture as an Optimization Prior

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Initialization determines the optimization "fate" of a model, going far beyond just setting the scale of its weights. It establishes a fundamental initial geometry over the input data, dictating certain aspects such as which examples are considered close and which representations gradient descent will naturally refine first. In this paper, we view the initial geometry of a network as an optimization prior and ask whether a network can change its optimization prior by referencing a completely different architecture. To investigate this question, we use an initialization procedure where a target network is aligned to the representational geometry of a randomly initialized guide network before any downstream training occurs. Crucially, alignment to the guide network cannot transfer learned features, never receives gradients, and does not use labels. We demonstrate that this cross-architecture transfer of "fate" benefits the target network in two key domains: (1) instilling the optimization benefits of depth into shallow networks by aligning them to deeper networks, and (2) transferring representational granularity by using fine-resolution networks to shape the geometry of networks with coarser representations. In both cases, the alignment results in improved downstream performance of the target network. Ultimately, this demonstrates that a model's architectural destiny can be decoupled from its physical structure and explicitly modified at initialization.

SlicePlanner: Logic-Guided Agentic AI for Online QoS-Aware Network Slicing in Open RAN

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Adaptive network slicing in Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN) demands reconfiguration decisions that respond to live traffic conditions while remaining safe with respect to Service Level Agreement (SLA) constraints, a combination that purely data-driven controllers cannot reliably guarantee without continuous retraining. We present SlicePlanner, a logic-guided closed-loop framework that treats each RAN reconfiguration as a controlled hypothesis: proposed from live and historical KeyPerformance Indicator (KPI) evidence, filtered through explicit SLA constraints and mined RAN control rules, and committed only when post-action measurements confirm improvement. SlicePlanner comprises two cooperating agents: SliceSense, which monitors live KPIs, retrieves context-conditioned evidence, and ranks candidate RAN actions through a surrogate-guided admissibility filter; and SliceGuard, which validates post-action KPI responses, rolls back harmful configurations, and refreshes rules under distribution drift. Validated over the air using OpenAirInterface, FlexRIC, and commercial Sierra Wireless 5G devices, SlicePlanner reduces SLA latency violations by up to 86% over static slicing and 50% over Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL)-based slicing, while maintaining comparable cell throughput and auditable decisions without online retraining.

Seeing the Right Context: Grounded On-Policy Distillation for Remote Sensing

📅 2026-11-25 16:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Remote sensing scene understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to interpret queried targets in relation to nearby objects and surrounding structures, which serve as spatial references or define the scope of an answer. However, standard answers and bounding boxes often leave these contextual relationships implicit. Thus, we construct GeoContext-5K to make them explicit in 5,394 training pairs for visual question answering (VQA), with spatial cues, visual facts, and supporting regions. GeOPD uses these annotations for on-policy distillation, guiding a frozen teacher with region marks on the full image and a visual fact while preserving surrounding spatial references. It prioritizes teacher corrections that agree with the shift in the student's next token distribution when evidence is restored. A complementary objective weights the contrast between the student's scores on the two views by how much masking lowers the teacher's log probability for the same sampled token. Experiments on five remote sensing VQA benchmarks show that GeOPD outperforms the evaluated open-source MLLMs and matched post-training baselines, improving over the 4B and 9B base models by 5.81 and 2.97 percentage points on average. The dataset and code will be publicly released at https://github.com/YeeCharm/GeOPD.

The Platonic Representation Hypothesis in Jacobian-Space: Language and Vision Models Agree Approximately Up to a Rotation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) posits that AI models converge toward a shared representation of the world, even when trained on different data modalities. Previous work has found evidence for the PRH in the activations of vision and language models. In this work, we ask whether vision and language models also converge in how they act on these representations: precisely, in how these internal representations affect the models' output. At a chosen layer of a transformer, the Jacobian lens gives every word in the vocabulary (or class in an image classifier) a vector in the hidden state: the vector that most increases the model's tendency to generate that word, at that position or later, or to predict that class. Collectively, these vectors make up what is referred to as the model's J-space. We find evidence of the PRH in J-space across vision and language models, and furthermore, we find that J-spaces mostly converge up to a rotation. That is, a single rotation, fitted on a subset of words, approximately maps one model's J-space onto another's, and so applies to words and classes it was not fitted on. A word's vector, rotated from a text-only language model's J-space into the visual patch tokens of a vision-language model, changes the object the vision-language model says is in the image (and only in the image region where it is added). Rotated into an image generator's text conditioning, a word's vector changes the object in the generated image. Rotated into an ImageNet classifier (trained without text), it raises that object's class score above almost all others, and vice versa. This suggests that models converge not only in how they represent the world, but also in how they act on it.

Context Scaling: A New Frontier of AI Scaling

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Many failures of deployed AI arise not from weak inference but from a mismatch between the state a task depends on and the context available when the system acts. Relevant user state, repository revisions, tool traces, sensor readings, or environment changes may be unavailable, distorted, or poorly selected. We call the effort to reduce this bottleneck context scaling. We frame a model as $P_θ(d\mid c)$ within a system governed by three budgets: training $B_θ$, decision-time $B_d$, and context-infrastructure $B_c$. Pretraining scaling invests in $θ$; decision-time scaling searches, refines, or verifies decisions under supplied context; context scaling improves the system's capacity to acquire, represent, deliver, evaluate, and persist deployment state for model use. The three programs interact, but only $B_c$ targets what the system can make available before a decision. This view makes the model-infrastructure pair the unit of analysis. We define effective-context value as a task- and intervention-dependent attribution, separate deployment state from model-visible context, and identify availability, fidelity, selection, and utilization failures. We then organize current mechanisms through a five-operation context lifecycle and propose controlled budget interventions and utility-cost curves for evaluation. The resulting agenda centers on grounded reporting, interaction-data and full-cost infrastructure, and safety and governance for persistent, adaptive systems.

FORK: Co-Evolving Safe Disclosure and Decision Skills Against Attackers Who Read Every Refusal

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

When a guardrail rejects an LLM agent's action, its message reaches any attacker observing the trajectory: a fixed refusal leaves benign users unable to recover, whereas an explanation can tell the attacker what to try next. We study this as safe disclosure: explaining rejections so that authorized tasks recover without increasing adaptive attack success. Safe disclosure depends on both the decision and the message: what the guardrail blocks and what it reveals to the agent and the attacker. Because hand-written rules alone cannot anticipate how adaptive attackers probe decisions or use messages, we introduce FORK, which co-evolves a guardrail's natural-language decision and disclosure skills with an attacker that reads every public message. Each exploited message and permissive decision gives FORK evidence for a gradient-free revision of its natural-language skill library. We evaluate FORK on four benchmarks. Averaged over three agents on FORK-World, FORK cuts attack success rate (ASR) under adaptive attack from 78.4% to 14.1% and keeps utility above 85%, outperforming baselines on both ASR and utility. Notably, on MT-AgentRisk, whose environments and tasks are unseen in training, FORK lowers ASR from 70.6% to 9.1% with 93.1% benign utility.

HoMe: Toward Practical Harness Optimization of Memory Systems for AI Assistants

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The behavior of memory systems for long-running AI assistants is governed by a harness, which consists of the code, prompts, and configurations for memory construction, maintenance, retrieval, and answer generation. However, optimizing the harness of a memory system remains largely manual, and unrestricted editing alone does not ensure structural improvements. In this paper, we introduce HoMe, an evaluation-driven framework that iteratively evaluates candidate harnesses and derives each new candidate from any previously evaluated harness by editing any part of it. HoMe combines session-level memory versioning with execution traces to identify shared failure mechanisms across cases and localize them to the responsible sessions and harness components. It encourages early structural exploration through candidate gating and reduces evaluation cost through smoke tests, adaptive sampling, and the reuse of constructed memory. We validate HoMe with the open-source memory system ReMe on two large-scale long-horizon memory benchmarks, LongMemEval-S and BEAM. With six formal candidate evaluations per benchmark, after a full initial evaluation, HoMe discovers harnesses that improve end-task performance over the originally strong harness while consuming far fewer query-time tokens. The code is available at https://github.com/agentscope-ai/ReMe.

Carrying Feature Ablations Across Sparse Autoencoder Seeds

📅 2026-11-25 16:20
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A vision encoder turns an image into one vector per patch, refined layer by layer. A sparse autoencoder (SAE) writes each vector of one layer as a sum of learned directions, most with a coefficient of zero. Researchers call each direction a feature and edit the encoder by removing one feature. An SAE trained from another random seed learns different features. Earlier work matches the two SAEs' features one to one by how closely their directions agree. Matched features can point alike yet respond to different image parts, so removing them may change the encoder differently. We remove a feature and, separately, its match, then compare the changes in the encoder's output on the same held out images. When the second change nearly reproduces the first, we say the removal carries across seeds. A setting fixes a DINOv2 or SigLIP layer and its SAE feature count. At our tolerance, the removal carries for 12% to 70% of matched pairs across three settings. The share is 0% in two controls: an SAE whose directions stay random and, post hoc, matches shuffled among trained features. Post hoc, predicting which removals carry is more accurate with layer contributions than with directions. Some incorrect predictions remain. One persists on independent images. So pairs can be screened at the layer. Measuring the output detects errors this screen misses.

AlignPre: Skill-Aligned Post-Training for Latent-Free Generative Precipitation Nowcasting

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Precipitation nowcasting requires accurate short-term predictions under rapidly evolving spatiotemporal dynamics. Generative diffusion models can produce detailed and competitive radar forecasts by modeling conditional distributions. However, most existing approaches rely on separately trained components, such as deterministic branches or latent compression modules. More fundamentally, distribution matching alone fails to explicitly optimize operational forecast skill. We propose AlignPre, a streamlined pixel-space nowcasting framework that bridges this gap via skill-aligned post-training (SAPT). AlignPre employs an end-to-end flow-matching forecaster with a hierarchical spatiotemporal architecture for distribution matching and direct radar-field synthesis, without latent compression. During post-training, SAPT exploits the analytic structure of forecast verification scores to construct a differentiable objective. By backpropagating gradients of this objective from radar fields to the generator, SAPT turns forecast verification into direct training feedback. Across three real-world benchmarks spanning different forecast horizons, AlignPre consistently surpasses state-of-the-art deterministic and generative baselines in forecast skill, demonstrating that aligning generative modeling with domain-specific verification provides an effective paradigm for meteorological forecasting.

A Hierarchy of Risk Certificates for Representation-Based Prediction

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Representation-based prediction underlies many machine-learning systems. Yet a fitted predictor's failure to meet an accuracy requirement does not establish that its representation-decoder class cannot meet it. We study optimal population mean squared error (MSE) over Lipschitz decoders acting on a fixed representation. For blocks of $b\geq2$ independent and identically distributed (iid) observations, we minimize total squared error (normalized by $b-1$) under pairwise Lipschitz constraints. We prove that expected block scores form nondecreasing lower bounds on optimal class risk and converge to that risk on compact metric feature spaces, although no fixed block size provides a uniform relative guarantee. Verified dual lower bounds and iid calibration observations independent of model development, with bounded targets and predictions, yield simultaneous lower confidence bounds on optimal class risk and upper confidence bounds on fitted-predictor risk. These bounds can establish that every admissible decoder exceeds a prescribed MSE threshold or that an admissible fitted predictor meets it. Controlled experiments illustrate population-risk certification; fixed PTB-XL and Bike Sharing experiments provide deterministic empirical-risk conclusions. The results support principled assessment and selection of representation-decoder configurations. Efficient certification at scale and extensions to dependent observations remain open problems.

SOLO: Towards Sample-Faithful and Detectable LLM Watermarking under Repeated Sampling via Content-Anchored Single-Owner Contexts

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Providers watermark the outputs of large language models, while applications sample many responses per prompt for self-consistency, best-of-n selection, and uncertainty estimation. We show that deployed configurations of distortion-free watermarks, which deduplicate keyed contexts only within a response, couple the responses of a request: answer templates make all responses query the watermark with the same context before the answer, so collisions across responses, usually assumed rare, become the norm. Key pools reduce or avoid the coupling at the cost of multiple testing, and the exact remedy of a context history shared across the request leaves many positions unwatermarked. We propose SOLO, which keeps a request-scoped context history but anchors its keyed contexts on content selected by min-hash, so that siblings rarely compete for a context and random edits stay local. Its responses are jointly distributed as independent unwatermarked responses, each detectable with a single key. On five language models from three families and three benchmarks, SOLO preserves self-consistency and uncertainty estimation and, at a 1% false positive rate, detects more responses than four-token key pools on most tasks, also after randomly editing 10% of the tokens, and more than the context history on most clean tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/ZhongliangGuo/solo-watermark.

KnitNet: Learning to Search at Inference Time for Exact RNA Inverse Design

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

RNA inverse design seeks nucleotide sequences that fold into specified target structures. Existing methods rely on learned generators that can miss exact structural matches, especially beyond their training distributions, or on costly search over a vast, multimodal sequence space. Search is further complicated by globally coupled nucleotide interactions, as correcting one pairing error can create new errors beyond a fixed repair scope. To address this, we introduce KnitNet, an inference-time scaling framework that learns from verifier feedback to refine sequences for exact fold-back matching. Starting from candidates generated for a target secondary structure, KnitNet uses mismatched folds to infer nucleotide dependencies and adapt its joint repair strategy, while an online surrogate learns to predict which edits are promising. This makes search progressively more informed and compute-efficient. Crucially, KnitNet generalizes across RNA structural regimes by changing only the optimization signal to match the verifier required by each setting. Across five secondary-structure benchmarks, KnitNet increases exact solve rates by an average of 21 percentage points over continued sampling with 70% less time-to-solve. On OpenKnot57 specifically, KnitNet is the first method to achieve exact computational matches for all complex, pseudoknotted structures. Beyond 2D, KnitNet improves 3D structural self-consistency over per-target RL by 7.8% while being 39.3% faster, demonstrating generalization and effective inference-time scaling across RNA design regimes.

Did the Model Forecast It, or Already Know It? Evaluating Large Language Models for Novel Drug-Target Prioritisation with a Temporal Knowledge-Graph O...

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI systems increasingly help decide which drug targets enter costly clinical trials. We recast each target-disease hypothesis as a dated event, the pair's first clinical trial. This lets us test whether a language model can forecast such events after its training cutoff, rather than recall events before it. We rebuild a dated biomedical knowledge graph as it stood at the end of each year from 2020 up to its newest version. We then ask six open language models, with and without this graph, which targets will enter a first trial for a disease the next year. Without retrieval, the models rank about as well as trial history, a count of each target's past trials in the same therapeutic area, also after their training cutoffs. Evidence dated before the decision adds at most a small gain, before or after the models' training cutoffs. The newest graph raises five of six models clearly above that baseline, but its paths usually include the answer: the pair's own trial, dated after the decision. Evaluations of target prioritisation should date both features and labels, with evidence from before the decision and outcomes after it.

Navigent: A Geometry-Grounded Agentic Framework for Vision Language Navigation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) with general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offers a flexible alternative to task-specific navigation policies, while reliable long-horizon execution remains challenging. Agents must track navigation progress, detect discrepancies between commanded and actual motion, and recognize when movement has passed relevant landmarks or entrances. We present Navigent, an agentic VLN framework built around a frozen MLLM and a Geometry-Grounded Execution Critic (GEC). GEC performs two complementary checks: action-to-motion consistency, which evaluates measured displacement against commanded movement, and motion-to-landmark consistency, which tracks agent-selected visual anchors and entrance candidates in world coordinates. Detected discrepancies interrupt the remaining action sequence and trigger reobservation, local adjustment, or node-based recovery. Historical visual memory supports progress tracking across extended routes. An offline self-improvement procedure further analyzes successful and failed training trajectories to refine navigation prompts and GEC logic while keeping model weights fixed. On the R2R-CE validation-unseen split (1,839 episodes) and RxR-CE validation-unseen split(3,669 episodes), Navigent achieves 84.0%/86.0% SR and 72.9%/69.1% SPL, respectively, demonstrating the value of geometry-grounded execution verification for reliable agentic navigation. Project Page: https://marslab-navigent.github.io/navigent.github.io/ Code: https://github.com/MarsLab-Navigent/Navigent

SceneDesigner3D: Agentic Evolutionary Search for Principled Rearrangement of 3D Indoor Scenes

📅 2026-11-25 17:41
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Scene rearrangement is an essential task for 3D content creation applications, requiring generation of diverse, aesthetic and functional scene rearrangements. We present SceneDesigner3D, an agentic evolutionary search method for 3D indoor scene rearrangement that systematically explores the design space of 3D scenes and generates multiple solutions according to common interior design principles. Given an input scene and a text prompt, SceneDesigner3D represents the rearrangement as an editable program and evolves a population of candidate layouts. Starting from distinct design ideas, a VLM agent mutates the programs by refining promising candidates, exploring new rearrangement strategies or redesigning from scratch, while verifying the layouts for physical validity. Proposals are scored by a VLM judge for adherence to the input prompt and satisfaction of design principles such as balance, zoning and emphasis, as well as numerically computed principles such as circulation and clutter. An archive of top-performing, structurally varied candidates further increases diversity of rearrangements. Experiments on the SAGE-10k and SceneSmith datasets show that SceneDesigner3D improves prompt adherence and computed design principle scores over baselines by up to 5.1% and 15.2%, respectively. Code will be made publicly available.

Guidance as Variance Matching

📅 2026-11-25 17:53
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Guidance repels the prediction of a diffusion model away from a weaker reference prediction. It substantially improves sample quality, but its mechanism remains poorly understood. We first find that, at the scales used in practice, guidance does not improve the prediction of the posterior mean (the model's training target); it makes the prediction worse, while samples improve. Second, posterior-mean predictions have less variance than the data, and we find that guidance restores it. We introduce variance-matched guidance: a closed-form rule that picks the guidance scale by matching the variance of the guided predictions to that of the data. Without tuning, it recovers the hand-picked scales of eleven widely used models across image, video, audio, motion, and world generation; to our knowledge, no previous explanation of guidance predicts them. Applied per noise level, it yields a schedule without tuned hyperparameters that users prefer to hand-crafted ones on SD 3.5 Medium. Third, we offer an interpretation of the mechanism behind guidance: each model makes a random error in predicting the posterior mean, and guidance rescales the difference of these errors into a sample with the posterior's covariance, which improves sampling. This view also suggests two new hypotheses that our experiments support: guidance can work with negative scales, and guiding a model with its own checkpoint needs a larger scale than guiding it with an independently trained model.

PoVi: Agentic Poetry-to-Video Generation with Knowledge Grounding and Figurative Understanding

📅 2026-11-25 16:50
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Poetry-to-video generation requires more than translating text into visual scenes. Poetic language is highly compressed, often depends on historical and cultural knowledge, and frequently conveys meaning through metaphor, imagery, and other non-literal expressions. We introduce PoVi, a benchmark and agentic framework for grounded poetry-to-video generation, evaluating semantic grounding, discourse coherence, and audiovisual realization. PoVi reconstructs poem semantics into an evidence-bounded representation and progressively transforms them into narration, interpretation, character assets, and shot-level visual plans, followed by narration-aligned video generation. Across three planning backbones and four video generators, PoVi consistently improves over single-call generation, with the largest gains on implicit background knowledge and metaphorical relations. PoVi provides a testbed for studying knowledge-grounded, long-horizon multimodal generation beyond direct text-to-video synthesis.

Know the Design, Pick the Attack: Sentence-Keyed Watermarks for LLMs

📅 2026-11-25 14:56
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A watermark lets the provider of a language model recognise the text its model wrote, which matters more as generated text becomes hard to tell from human writing: provenance underpins academic integrity, the fight against misinformation and new regulation. Providers have started to watermark the text of their models, and to explain how their watermarks work. Explaining a watermark, however, also tells an attacker how to break it, and there are two ways to do so: reword the text to erase the mark, or learn the mark and copy it onto text the model never wrote. Each attack has been studied on its own. We study an attacker that reads the published design to choose between them: it erases the watermarks that are hard to learn and forges the ones that survive rewording. Against it, every existing watermark we compare fails. We propose KeyedLex, which reads its secret key from the grammar of the previous sentence, which rewording changes little, and whose detector does not need to recover the key exactly. It outperforms every watermark we compare on both models we test, losing about half of its signal where the best alternative loses two thirds, at no extra cost in text quality over the best-known watermarks.

RankCast: Phase-Aware Rank Balancing in MoE Serving

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Modern large language model (LLM) inference increasingly adopts disaggregated architectures that separate Prefill (P) and Decode (D) phases. Meanwhile, Expert Parallelism (EP) has been widely adopted in large Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model serving. However, load imbalance still slows down MoE inference because each layer must wait for the busiest EP rank. The main source of latency differs between prefill and decode. Prefill is compute-bound, and its MoE layers' latency is determined by the maximum token count of the busiest rank. At the same time, under moderate batch sizes, Decode is memory-bound, so the latency is dominated by the maximum active-expert count across ranks. To solve both problems under a unified framework, we present RankCast, a system that reduces rank-level maximum token counts during prefill and active-expert counts during decode. For every token, RankCast preserves the predicted Top-p experts and chooses the remaining k-p from its Top-L candidates (L > k). In prefill, RankCast yields a price per rank that steers flexible assignments to less loaded ranks. In decode, RankCast closes experts on the busiest ranks while preserving the constraints for each token. Both paths are implemented and tested in the state-of-the-art inference frameworks SGLang and DeepEP. We overlap RankCast's kernels with model execution and reuse the existing DeepEP communication channel for metadata transmission. On Qwen3-235B-A22B with EP=8 and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B with EP=16, RankCast removes over 75% of the busiest rank's excess prefill load, improves prefill input throughput by up to 19%, and reduces decode time per output token by up to 10%. The quality cost is below one percentage point.

RadOncGym: An Agent-Based Framework for Radiotherapy Dose-Mimicking Optimization

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI dose-prediction models generate patient-specific 3D dose distributions from patient anatomy, providing an important intermediate representation for automated radiotherapy planning. However, an AI-predicted dose must undergo downstream optimization to produce a physically constrained treatment plan, a process that requires balancing competing planning objectives. We present RadOncGym, an agent-based framework that enables an LLM agent to iteratively tune 12 planning-objective weights for dose-mimicking optimization. To reduce iterative evaluation cost, RadOncGym incorporates a surrogate verifier that provides rapid feedback while allowing the agent to decide when to request a full planning solve within a fixed solve budget. We evaluate RadOncGym on 60 OpenKBP head-and-neck patients, each paired with 10 predictions from OpenKBP-Opt dose-prediction sets, yielding 600 patient-prediction contexts per experimental configuration. Under RadOncGym's 16-turn setting with a three-solve budget, a GPT-5.6 Sol agent reduces mean clinical-criterion violation by 0.6131 Gy and improves DVH and dose scores by 0.5986 and 0.2204 Gy relative to leaving all 12 weights fixed at 1, outperforming all evaluated non-agent weight-tuning methods under the same solve budget. It remains competitive with the 16-solve variants of these methods while requiring 75.0-77.6% less normalized runtime. RadOncGym demonstrates the potential of LLM agents as optimization planners in downstream radiotherapy plan optimization and provides a public testbed for their systematic evaluation. Code and configurations are available at https://github.com/miaot-usc/RadOncGym.

Joint Estimation of Abilities and Item Parameters for Large Language Models Using Probability and Binary Responses: A Fractional-Response IRT Framewor...

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model (LLM) evaluations provide two response types: probabilities assigned to correct answers and binary indicators of correctness. Restricting evaluation to one type narrows the range of available models or discards probability information. We propose a method to integrate both types through a common two-parameter logistic item response model and fractional-response quasi-maximum likelihood estimation. The framework includes binary-only models in common-scale evaluation without discarding available probabilityinformation; broader measurement coverage does not by itself ensure greater precision or measurement equivalence. Simulations evaluate statistical efficiency when both response types have the same expected value. Moreover, adding probability respondents improves item calibration more efficiently than adding binary respondents. However, adding noisier binary responses to an already precise probability sample can increase estimation error under equal observation weights. An application to Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) combines probability response data from the Open LLM Leaderboard with binary response data from Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM) and reveals model-family response patterns, differences in item characteristics, and systematic ability shifts between evaluation protocols. The proposed framework extends common-scale IRT evaluation to models with different response formats while highlighting the roles of observation variability and evaluation-protocol comparability in statistical efficiency and interpretation.

DemoFoundry: Toward Scalable Photorealistic Robot Data with Coding Agents and Generative Rendering

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Scaling robot learning requires demonstrations that combine realistic visual observations with reliable action supervision. Simulation-based synthesis faces a substantial visual gap relative to real environments, while video-only generation struggles to maintain physically consistent interactions and provide aligned action labels. We present DemoFoundry, an automated framework that couples coding agents with generative rendering to synthesize photorealistic and physically grounded robot demonstrations. Coding agents both construct aligned real--proxy video pairs from existing robot episodes to train a video renderer and write executable interaction programs to generate new demonstrations. Given a scene reference and task instruction, an agent reconstructs task-relevant geometry and writes a control program without requiring target-task human demonstrations. Program execution in a physics simulator produces proxy videos with aligned robot states and actions. The video renderer translates these proxy videos into scene-consistent, photorealistic observations, reducing the need for detailed appearance modeling in each scene. Automated task-execution checks select successful trajectories for rendering and policy learning. Experiments show improved visual quality and action--video consistency. Policies trained on the generated demonstrations outperform raw-simulation training and approach the performance of training on an equal number of real demonstrations.

Should Service Requirements Be Scalarized or Constrained When Minimizing Costs? A Case Study on HVAC Control

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Automated decision systems, from learned policies to model-based optimizers, often minimize costs such as energy or resource use while meeting a service requirement, for example the quality of service (QoS) specified in a service level agreement (SLA), a comfort band, or a safety margin. The requirement can be scalarized with the costs into a single objective, with a fixed or an adaptively tuned (Lagrangian) weight, or it can be imposed as a constraint. We study this choice in the Smart Buildings Challenge, in which a single policy controls the heating, ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) of previously unseen buildings and is evaluated by a weighted sum of comfort, energy cost and carbon emissions. With all other components fixed, we compare the two treatments in model-based optimization, represented by model predictive control (MPC) with the requirement as a hard or a soft (l1-penalized) constraint, and in learned policies, represented by proximal policy optimization (PPO) with a fixed or a Lagrangian penalty in the reward or a projection layer on the action. In MPC, the soft constraint reproduces the hard one once its weight exceeds the requirement's shadow price, which the challenge's comfort weight exceeds approximately fourfold; below this weight, comfort is sacrificed, and under a metric that values comfort below its shadow price the soft constraint outperforms the hard one by up to 28%. A weight tuned on the training buildings is 3.9% worse than the hard constraint on 48 unseen buildings, because the shadow price varies across buildings. In PPO, the projected policy satisfies the requirement from the outset and leads early in training; with longer training, the Lagrangian policy overtakes it while the projected policy deteriorates, and the fixed penalty does not learn to satisfy the requirement.

Make the Adversary Spend More: Stateful Deception as a Cost-Exchange Primitive for AI-Era Cyber Defense

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) are transforming the economics of intrusion by reducing the attacker's cost to scan and adapt, while tempting defenders into unbounded compute. We argue that cyber deception should be judged by the cost exchange it imposes, not by realism or detection, and that the primitive making that exchange favorable is adversary-specific persistent state. Rather than a honeypot that resets or merges sessions, each recognized adversary re-enters the world it believes it has compromised, kept coherent with its own past actions and isolated from every other adversary, while the language model acts only as a constrained renderer of explicit state, never as its store. We formalize the trade-off as a Deception Exchange Rate (DER), relating mission-weighted adversary effort burned to defender resources spent, and instantiate it in an SSH honeypot pairing Cowrie with a local LLM. In this paper, we focus on the defender-side evaluation of that exchange, while specifying, but not yet measuring, per-adversary state and the adversary side of the DER. Across five open-weight models, deterministic-layer and prompt optimizations cut per-session defender cost by around 63% against a naive all-LLM decoy.

Whose Side Are You On? A Framework and Benchmark for Assessing LLM Political Stances through Politicians Social Media Posts

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) increasingly participate in political discussions, making it important to understand which positions they support or oppose. We propose a framework for assessing LLMs' political stances through simulated social media interactions. The framework uses politicians' posts and multiple prompting methods to identify consistently expressed stances. Using this framework, we construct WorldPOLL, comprising 4,600 political posts from X/Twitter collected from approximately 200 politicians representing ruling and opposition blocs across 23 countries. Our quantitative analysis shows how support and opposition vary across models, countries, and political directions, while our qualitative analysis identifies the political views and policies models support or oppose. Together, these analyses reveal shared positions across models and differences across national contexts. We also examine whether models express consistent stances across prompting methods, investigate the reasons for disagreement, and assess the effects of model size and instruction tuning in stance inconsistency. WorldPOLL offers a practical way to measure LLM political tendencies, identify the views behind them, and assess their consistency in real-world political discussions.

Calibration of Neuromusculoskeletal Models Using Differentiable Physics: Recommendations for Improving Model Accuracy

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Differentiable physics has shown potential to usher in a new paradigm in human neuromusculoskeletal (NMS) model calibration. Early results from pioneering works have demonstrated that auto-differentiable NMS models can greatly reduce NMS model calibration time while achieving comparable accuracy compared to traditional calibration methods. The efficient gradient-based optimizers have enabled calibration of increasingly sophisticated human models with a large number of parameters, which seek to narrow the gap between simulation and reality. Several modules have been evaluated separately in different studies, but have never been directly compared within the same study. In this work, these modules are compared on the same differentiable upper limb model with a 3-DoF shoulder, a 2-DoF elbow, and 11 superficial and 5 deep muscles in the calibration to find an optimal pipeline for improving model accuracy. The considered modules include muscle synergy, learnable bone parameters, optimizable geometry of wrap objects, and constraint torque of the differentiable arm model. To evaluate the effect and interplay between the modules on calibration accuracy, one or more modules were used in each of 16 calibration configurations, which were compared in terms of the error between two joint torques calculated by the forward and inverse dynamics with the calibrated model on 9 distinctive testing movements across 4 subjects. Results showed that torque loss was reduced by 23.6% on average using muscle synergy extrapolation to estimate deep muscle activities, and by 10.9% on average through bone dynamic parameter optimization, which are both recommended to be incorporated in the calibration.

Agentic-Level Adversarial Purification: Revisiting Diffusion Purifiers on Native-Resolution Agentic Inputs

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language agents act on native-resolution renders of multi-element pages, where one perturbed photo or a few altered text strokes can redirect the decision. Any content provider can craft such a perturbation on the open model behind the agent, so a plug-in purifier is the natural first line of defense. Yet diffusion purifiers were designed and evaluated at the fixed resolution of the diffusion model on classifier or natural-image benchmarks, and defense evaluations for agents test JPEG, blur and noise but no diffusion purifier. We present the first study of adversarial purification at the agentic level, for an agent built on Qwen3-VL-8B: purifiers run at native resolution on multi-element inputs, face grey-box attackers who know the agent's model but not the defense, and are compared at matched clean cost. We show that treating agentic inputs as classifier inputs misleads both evaluation and design. Resizing the input to the resolution of the diffusion model, as existing purifiers do, raises the TextVQA clean cost of the Divide-and-Conquer purifier (DC) from 4.0 to 35.7 points. On web-shop pages, DC keeps 85.0% grey-box robust accuracy against web-hardened perturbations, where cheap input transformations at matched clean cost keep at most 40.0%, and this robustness comes from the global noise level: input-estimated masks and noise levels add none. These findings hold on a disjoint held-out set and on a second VLM and yield three requirements for defenses, we release the protocol.

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into telecommunications for customer service, network analytics, and operational support. Spectral Optimal Transport for Dynamics-Aware Sign Language Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Sign language generation (SLG) translates spoken-language text into pose sequences that convey its meaning. Recently, flow-based models with optimal transport (OT) have become a strong SLG baseline by handling the one-to-many nature of text-to-sign. While classifier-free guidance (CFG) could be applied to further strengthen the semantic alignment theoretically, its training involves conditional and unconditional training, which may contradict the pose-space OT, thus reducing the generation quality. Under the condition dropout, one noise sample can be paired with target motions exhibiting incompatible motion dynamics, interfering with the semantic guidance when the text condition is present. To address this issue, we propose Spectral Optimal Transport (SOT), which augments the pose-space cost with a spectral feature distance, reweighting the coupling toward the temporal-frequency structure of the target motions, thereby improving the performance when using CFG. The quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

FineDex: Bridging Fine-Grained Human-Object Interaction Generation to Dexterous Humanoid Manipulation

📅 2026-11-25 18:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Dexterity is, at its core, finger-level control, yet existing dexterous manipulation and human-object interaction (HOI) generation methods rarely let one specify how an interaction is performed: five-fingered hands are often reduced to grippers, or finger usage is copied from individual human demonstrations. We present FineDex, a framework that makes finger-level intent both expressible and executable. FineDex learns coordinated human body, hand, and object motions that follow fine-grained instructions on hand selection, individual fingers, and contacts with articulated objects. To bridge generation and execution, FineDex leverages generated interactions as actionable priors for an agent that discovers physically feasible contact strategies through practice in a physics-based digital twin. By adapting at test time without per-instance retraining, FineDex generalizes across fine-grained instructions and object configurations. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art HOI generation quality and fine-grained controllability, substantial improvements in dexterous manipulation success, and successful deployment on a real dexterous humanoid robot.

LiDAR Intensity Reconstruction with Limited Real Data: An Architecture Benchmark under Fixed-Epoch Training

📅 2026-11-25 17:10
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Physics-derived simulation can supplement scarce real-world LiDAR intensity measurements, but its effectiveness depends on the learning architecture and the amount of real training data available. We benchmark nine neural architectures for LiDAR intensity reconstruction from depth, semantic labels, incidence angle, and material reflectance, using real SemanticKITTI and synthetic VoxelScape range-image data. All models follow a fixed 20-epoch protocol; this controls the number of training passes, not the computational cost across architectures. We report mean absolute error (MAE), mean squared error (MSE), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), structural similarity (SSIM), inference timing, and spectral-energy agreement. We also evaluate four selected architectures with fixed-size training mixtures containing 0\%, 1\%, 5\%, 10\%, 20\%, 50\%, or 100\% real frames. With real-only training, UNet-V2 achieves the best reported reconstruction metrics (MAE 0.234, MSE 0.140, PSNR 8.64 dB, and SSIM 0.647), followed by a denoising diffusion model (DDPM). In several mixtures containing 1--10\% real data, DDPM achieves lower error and higher image-quality metrics than the evaluated deterministic alternatives, whereas UNet-V2 leads with 100\% real data. Spectral analysis additionally reveals architecture-dependent discrepancies across frequency bands. These findings characterize reconstruction trade-offs under the stated data mixtures and fixed-epoch protocol, without establishing compute-matched superiority or isolating the diffusion objective from backbone differences.

Sequential Testing on Complex and High Dimensional Data Streams Leveraging Unsupervised Model Errors

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We propose two general, distribution-free test statistics for binary Sequential Hypothesis Testing (SHT) and Sequential Change Point Detection (SCPD) respectively, in streaming environments where the data (e.g., network traffic metadata) are too complicated for the exact probabilistic modeling required by conventional likelihood ratio-driven tests. Our framework also addresses the challenge of information asymmetry, where data is available only from the null/pre-change regime and not from the alternative/post-change one such as zero-day attacks or emerging disease related regimes. The proposed statistics monitor the errors of a predictive or reconstructive machine learning model trained solely on null/pre-change data, relying on imperfect generalization (the model's performance will degrade when deployed on data from the alternative/post-change class). Under mild assumptions on the model's errors, we establish rigorous analytical guarantees: strict non-asymptotic error control for SHT, and explicit bounds on the Average Run Length (ARL) and the Expected Detection Delay (EDD) for SCPD. Extensive evaluations in target tracking, chaotic dynamical systems, and real-world network security datasets, demonstrate that our framework consistently satisfies prescribed constraints where existing algorithms succumb to inflated missed detections and/or false alarms. Finally, because our methods operate on scalar errors without requiring online retraining or kernel evaluations, they are highly scalable.

Spectrum Adaptation of AI-Native Autoencoder Communications with Transfer Learning

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

End-to-end physical-layer learning replaces separate modulation, coding, and detection operations with an autoencoder (AE) that consists of a jointly trained encoder-decoder pair at the transmitter and the receiver. This paper studies AI-native communications with AEs and quantifies the sensitivity to training-test mismatch in channel models. Models trained only on an AWGN channel achieve low bit error rates (BERs) under matched conditions but degrade severely on Rayleigh fading with practical front-end impairments. To reduce the time and energy required to adapt to new channel conditions, we propose a low-latency transfer-learning procedure that starts from an AWGN-pretrained system and fine-tunes only a small subset of transmitter and receiver layers while freezing the remainder. We further formulate layer selection as a budget-constrained optimization problem that identifies the most informative parameters to update during adaptation. This preserves the learned signal geometry and the deep decision structure while adapting lightweight front ends to fading- and impairment-induced mixing. For AE communications, we show that partial updates remove the mismatch-induced error floor and approach the BER of fully retrained models. We provide a layer-wise study and quantify resource-performance trade-offs, showing that modest adaptation substantially reduces BER while cutting training time, multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations, energy consumption, and the fraction of updated parameters for resource-efficient implementation.

Agentic Waterfall: An Intent-Driven Information Discovery Framework for Reclaiming User Control in the Attention Economy

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We formalize Agentic Waterfall Feed, a new problem of personalized information discovery in which users actively direct recommendations and the system adapts to evolving needs inferred from both explicit requests and browsing behavior. We address this problem with the Agentic Waterfall framework, whose language-model controller maintains persistent user state and coordinates content retrieval, ranking, and state updates. Verified experience improves materials prediction with large language models

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Materials discovery is an iterative loop: experiments and calculations verify predictions, and each outcome can inform the next. Yet most predictors are specialized single-task models fixed after training. Large language models have recently emerged as predictors across heterogeneous tasks but generally underperform specialized models, and retrieval-augmented variants discard their own outcomes. Benchmarks also score queries in isolation, leaving improvement from outcomes untested. Here we construct MatMemBench, a sequential benchmark of 22 materials tasks that reveals outcomes only after prediction and tests online adaptation, memory retention and cross-task transfer. We present MatRecall, a framework that lets large language models store verified predictions, with evidence, outcome and error, as episodes and recall them without retraining. MatRecall improved median performance by 62% over a memory-free model, exceeded specialized models on 11 tasks, remained useful after feedback stopped and transferred between related tasks. RoboARC: Physical Puzzles for Robotic Manipulation under Spatial Constraints

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Many hard manipulation tasks come down to a spatial constraint that only a few motions satisfy, such as passing a part through a gap barely wider than itself or separating two interlocked parts. These motions depend on the exact geometry of each scene, so demonstrations and skills recorded elsewhere rarely transfer. We therefore ask: when manipulation hinges on spatial constraints, can robots reason out the right motions? We introduce RoboARC, a benchmark of 90 tasks in six constraint types over rigid and soft materials, on three robots in MuJoCo. Every task is a frozen scene with a physics-based grader and a verified reference solution that replays bit-identically. Each task comes in a Privileged regime, with the full simulator state and resets, and a Standard regime with real-sensor inputs (camera images and proprioception) and a single attempt without resets. We evaluate seven coding agents and four VLA policies, none trained on these tasks. The strongest agent, GPT-6 Astra in Codex, solves 86% of the tasks in the Privileged regime and 43% in the Standard regime, and the four VLA policies, run zero-shot, solve none of their 18 one-arm puzzles. On 15 puzzles that GPT-6 Astra solved in the Privileged regime and failed in the Standard regime, object poses add 4 points and part geometry 13, but both together add 36, from 24% to 60%. Only 7% of the coding agents' failed runs pass more than half of a task, and 53% of the failed runs in the Standard regime are stuck on a single step. Frontier coding agents already reason out the right motions for many of these tasks once they know where the parts are and what shape they have. From their own sensors, in a single attempt, the strongest solves fewer than half of the tasks. The website can be found at https://roboarc.embodied-first-exam.ai

Probing Visual Idiosyncrasies in Text-to-Image Models

📅 2026-11-25 18:20
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Text-to-Image (T2I) models turn short text descriptions into images, and the leading models now produce images of comparable visual quality. Whether their outputs still carry model-specific characteristics matters for attribution, for model comparison beyond aggregate quality, and for evaluations that assume anonymity. We ask whether images from frontier T2I models can still be classified by source, and which visual properties make this possible. Here we show that a standard ResNet-50 identifies the source of an image among HiDream-O1-Dev, FLUX.2-dev, HunyuanImage-3.0, and Qwen-Image-2512, the four highest-ranked open-source models on the Text-to-Image Arena, with 100.00% accuracy on held-out prompts. Human participants reach only 62.61% on the pairwise version of this task, so the distinguishing cues are not visually obvious. They are also not tied to a particular classifier and survive severe image corruptions and out-of-distribution prompts. We trace them to visual idiosyncrasies at three levels. Low-level texture statistics alone let a lightweight multi-layer perceptron match ResNet-50. Object layout, depth, and albedo estimated from the images each carry a source signal. Image captions reveal model-specific preferences in style and background that the prompts never specify. A one-sentence hint about these cues raises human accuracy by 9.03 percentage points. These idiosyncrasies make source classification a tool for measuring similarity between models and between real and generated images, and they caution against assuming anonymity in human preference evaluation of T2I models.
