One Representation to Rule Them All -- Open-Vocabulary Change Detection from Text or Visual Examples

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Open-vocabulary change detection (OVCD) aims to localise user-specified changes without retraining for predefined categories. Existing approaches typically rely on textual descriptions and combine separate models for region proposal, semantic recognition, and temporal comparison. This limits how target concepts can be expressed and makes detection dependent on several intermediate predictions. We introduce OVTSID3, a training-free framework that unifies region grouping, concept identification, and temporal comparison in the representation space of DINOv3. OVTSID3 supports two complementary modes of concept specification: zero-shot textual queries or one-shot visual prompting through a masked reference pair. OVTSID3 outperforms all prior training-free OVCD methods on two building and two natural-disaster benchmarks, raising the average F1 by 8.9 points. OVTSID3 also supports semantic change detection and achieves state-of-the-art on SECOND. These results demonstrate the flexibility of a unified representation for text- or example-guided change detection without task-specific model training.

Connecting AI Agents to Live Discrete Element Simulations via the Model Context Protocol

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

This paper describes a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server through which a general-purpose AI agent operates a discrete element engine, commercial or open-source, while the simulation runs. The agent submits a long simulation to the engine and, while it computes, reads the running model, changes it, or stops it. The human modeler stays at the engine's console and graphical interface throughout, and the agent sees what the modeler does there. The tools are derived from what the agent needs to do at the engine and are held to four principles: correctness, coverage, orthogonality and simplicity. Two documentation tools let the agent search the engine's manual and browse it level by level. Five execution tools let the agent run code in the engine, submit long simulations in the background, check on them, read the running model, and stop them. A bridge inside the engine serves these tools in a commercial DEM engine, PFC, and an open-source one, YADE. In a case study, a general-purpose agent calibrated a drained triaxial model of dense Toyoura sand against laboratory data in both engines, while the modeler worked at the same engine; the agent noticed the modeler's actions at the console and acted on them. The same calibration on YADE was then given to seven language models, three sessions each and without human intervention. Twenty of the twenty-one sessions reached the targets, and nineteen of the parameter sets lie in one small region of the admissible ranges. The server and the bridge are released as two open-source packages, itasca-mcp, which also serves FLAC, 3DEC, MPoint and MassFlow, and yade-mcp.

RAP: Radial-Angular Random Projection for Hyperbolic Representations

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Random projection is a basic compression primitive for Euclidean representation learning, with the Johnson--Lindenstrauss lemma giving a target dimension $k=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ independent of the ambient dimension. As hyperbolic representations gain increasing attention in modern Transformers, vision-language models, and large language models, the same need for oblivious dimensionality reduction arises, but no analogous primitive yet exists for hyperbolic representations. We study this gap. Two natural attempts fail in dual ways: a tangent-space scheme gives a clean local guarantee but is chart-bound and pays a curvature penalty, while a fully hyperbolic projection in the hyperboloid model is geometrically native but mixes radial and angular error before feeding them into the conditioning of $\operatorname{arcosh}$. We propose radial-angular random projection (RAP), which preserves the radial coordinate exactly and applies a Euclidean random projection only to the angular direction, lifting the result back to the target hyperboloid. RAP is hyperbolic-to-hyperbolic by construction, costs one Gaussian matrix-vector product, and reduces hyperbolic compression to a normalized angular JL question on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$. We prove a finite-set RAP guarantee with explicit $\sinh(\sqrt{c}\,r_x)\sinh(\sqrt{c}\,r_y)$ conditioning, complemented by a local tangent-space JL theorem and an arcosh-conditioning lemma for the fully hyperbolic comparator. Across an origin-centered broad-radius point cloud, a balanced tree, a clustered Gaussian, and the WordNet noun hierarchy, RAP attains the lowest mean relative distortion in every $(n,k)$ cell, reducing distortion over the strongest hyperbolic baseline by approximately $48\%$--$49\%$ on broad-radius data. RAP also scales to million-point datasets and improves retrieval on vision--language representations over hyperbolic projection baselines.

Incorporating the Knowledge Distillation to Improve the EfficientNet Transfer Learning Capability

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Due to the Deep Learning requirement for a large training dataset, Transfer Learning has become a central method in the field of Computer Vision, which heavily used Deep Learning. Since the adoption of transfer learning in the field, the performance of the models in computer vision is significantly upgraded. The common transfer learning practice in computer vision research is to use state-of-the-art architectures in the ImageNet dataset as the backbone network. The best performing architecture in ImageNet is currently held by EfficientNet, which is a set of architectures that were progressively grown from a baseline architecture. By observing the EfficientNet development approach, it is natural to hypothesize that the smaller EfficientNet architecture is capable to contain the knowledge of the larger architecture. This hypothesis comes from the fact that the larger EfficientNet architecture was grown from the smaller architecture. Therefore, in this study, we experimented if it is beneficial to transfer the knowledge from the larger architecture to the smaller architecture of the EfficientNet. To achieve this goal, we proposed a transfer learning method that uses Knowledge Distillation as the knowledge transfer mechanism from the larger to smaller architecture. We found that the proposed method is able to upgrade the performance of each of the EfficientNet architecture. Several smaller architectures are even able to outperform the larger architecture that was trained using the standard transfer learning method.

SecID: An LLM-Guided Framework for Threat-Aware Secondary Asset Identification with Quantitative RTL Analysis

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The growing complexity of modern hardware designs is making security assurance increasingly difficult. One of the foundational steps in pre-silicon security verification is identifying security assets. While primary assets are often evident from design functionality, the internal signals that can facilitate their compromise are much less apparent. Complex data- and control-flow dependencies create many such internal security-critical locations, whose exploitation depends on the threat-specific attack mechanism---these are the secondary assets. To precisely identify these, we propose SecID, a multi-agent framework that combines deterministic RTL analysis with bounded dynamic evaluation and LLM reasoning to identify threat-specific secondary assets. Each asset decision is supported by functional influence, threat features, and explicit attack mechanisms. We demonstrate SecID on seven open-source SoC and IP designs, including openMSP430, NEORV32, and CV32E40P. The experimental results are corroborated through comparison with prior work, fault simulation, and formal analysis, and further evaluated through pipeline- and final-stage ablation, threshold-sensitivity studies, and final-stage repeatability analysis. Together, these evaluations show that SecID can identify highly threat-relevant secondary assets with respect to a given primary asset, providing a scalable path toward hardware-security verification.

Dynamics of Collective Diversity in Human-AI Co-Creation for Creative Tasks

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generative AI systems are increasingly deployed at scale to support human users in creative tasks across a variety of domains. However, as more users adopt the same AI agent, the resulting artifacts may become increasingly homogeneous, leading to a collapse in collective diversity. Prior work has documented this risk, but lacks a systematic framework for understanding how collective diversity changes as AI adoption scales across co-creation settings. In this work, we conduct a large-scale crowdsourcing study to understand and model the dynamics of collective diversity, quality, and effort in human-AI co-creation across two creative domains: Creative Hypothesis Generation and Creative Story Writing. We find that current AI agents used in co-creation can improve artifact quality and reduce effort, but at the cost of declining collective diversity as adoption increases. We further show that more fine-grained human-AI interaction, along with more diverse agent policies, can mitigate this collapse. Finally, we fit predictive models that quantify how collective diversity scales with adoption across co-creation settings, providing insights for designing AI agents that aim to preserve collective diversity.

Bigger Model or Bigger Table? How Much Backbone a Chorus N-gram Module Replaces

📅 2026-11-25 17:10
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We ask how much of a transformer's backbone the Chorus N-gram module replaces. Chorus adds hashed tables of token bigrams and trigrams to its input and attention. We train models of 16M to 349M parameters on 1.34 billion tokens of Polish printed before 1918 and find the size of a plain model with the same held-out loss. A separate learning-rate search for each kind of model cuts Chorus's gain at 47M by about a third. With tables about as large as the backbone, Chorus then matches a plain model 1.34 times its size at 16M and 1.16 to 1.20 times from 107M to 349M. With these tables, each added parameter replaces at most 0.31 backbone parameters. Chorus trains slower than a plain model of its size and faster than one of the same loss. On a fact quiz the two differ about as much as two seeds of one model. The results rest on one corpus, one 8,000-token vocabulary and a fifth of an epoch.

Focus Only on What's Important: Training-Free Focus Maps for Frozen Vision Transformers in Long-Video Object Segmentation

📅 2026-11-25 18:30
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision transformers are strong but costly image encoders for video object segmentation. Training-free token merging cuts compute uniformly across the frame, including on tracked objects. We give a frozen DINOv3 ViT inside a trained Cutie head a training-free focus map: full resolution where the segmenter expects its objects and coarser merging elsewhere. Protecting only the previous frame's mask works on short clips but fails on long videos, where fast motion pushes targets outside the margin and into background merging, locking in loss. Our policy instead takes the union of velocity-shifted and block-matched masks, runs a camera-compensated surprise check, delegates uncertain regions to the compared merger, and falls back to it when all objects are lost. On long videos (LVOS V2 valid, pre-specified protocol, three ViT-S seeds), it beats ALGM 0.98 by +2.76 J&F (95% object CI [+1.22,+4.44]; video-cluster [+0.99,+4.52]) at 16% fewer counted encoder FLOPs (0.385 vs. 0.459), sitting 1.0 J&F point below the dense-encoder baseline; at ViT-B the gain over ALGM 0.95 is +3.89 [+2.03,+5.87]. On DAVIS 2017 val it stays within -0.19 J&F of the dense encoder at ViT-S (-0.12 at ViT-B). Controls qualify the result: protecting only the previous mask under ALGM matches or exceeds accuracy at higher FLOPs (0.486), so expectation and surprise mainly reduce counted encoder compute. Practically, lower counted encoder compute did not reduce startup-inclusive mean Cutie step time on a 12-video LVOS sample (3%-8% lower in steady state; an equally engineered ALGM is faster at ViT-S and ViT-B). Section 5.2 separately examines a Cutie permanent-memory schedule and its latency and memory trade-offs. Code: https://github.com/boletoms/focused-cv

AutoformBench: A Lean Formalization Benchmark for Advanced Mathematics

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI systems are increasingly capable of producing machine-checked Lean proofs of advanced mathematical results. Real mathematical formalization, however, is not isolated theorem proving: it requires building the definitions and intermediate lemmas that connect a result to a formal library. Existing evaluations often provide isolated theorem statements in settings where much of this supporting theory is already available in Mathlib. We introduce AutoformBench, a benchmark of 341 Lean 4 formalization tasks drawn from 24 advanced mathematical sources. Lean-experienced mathematicians formalized and audited each target statement, leaving the corresponding Lean proof to the evaluated agent. Each problem is embedded in a broader mathematical development: agents receive the informal proof but must translate it into Lean while constructing the missing definitions, intermediate lemmas, and supporting infrastructure. We compare the performance of frontier models on these problems. We find a largely shared difficulty frontier: success depends less on mathematical domain than on models' ability to construct missing formal infrastructure. Data and code at https://github.com/facebookresearch/autoformbench

Neural Rendering Compiler: Generative Video Models as Programmable Renderers

📅 2026-11-25 16:44
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generative video models are becoming powerful renderers, yet lack the predictable control interfaces of conventional systems such as HTML/CSS engines or Blender. Their capabilities emerge implicitly from training, making it difficult to determine what they can reliably execute. We present Neural Rendering Compiler (NeurCo), which automatically probes frozen video models to construct model-specific capability manuals describing which semantic requirements they can reliably satisfy. NeurCo represents user intent in a model-agnostic intermediate representation of objects, constraints, events, and cameras, then compiles it into model-specific execution strategies. Reliable requirements remain unchanged, while unreliable ones are selectively rewritten or compiled into alternative execution strategies. We introduce VComBench, a benchmark of 380 compositional, multi-requirement tasks spanning text-to-video, image-to-video, and reference-to-video generation. NeurCo improves requirement satisfaction from 46.8% to 52.3% on Wan and from 66.5% to 69.3% on Minimax H3, and achieves further gains when combined with prompt enhancers, including on Seedance 2.5. Our analysis reveals model-specific differences in control capabilities and trade-offs between controllability and photorealism, highlighting capability-guided compilation as a promising approach to programmable generative video rendering.

Interpretable and Accurate Hybrid Decision Trees with Selective Case-Based Splits

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Decision trees are among the most well-known interpretable models in Machine Learning. Over the years, a wide variety of strategies have been proposed to define split conditions within the greedy learning framework, aiming to guide instance routing and enhance predictive performance. These strategies range from axis-parallel and oblique splits based on feature values to proximity-based splits using exemplar instances and more complex hybrid approaches. However, no single method has emerged as universally superior. In this work, we present two key contributions to the construction of greedy decision trees. First, we propose a hybrid tree architecture that integrates both feature-based and case-based splits, allowing for heterogeneous splits throughout the tree. Second, we introduce a novel type of split that leverages a selective case-based approach: when a split is needed, our method searches for the most informative subset of features to define the split, effectively balancing the specificity of case-based methods with the simplicity of feature-based ones. Experiments on a diverse set of tabular datasets show that our method outperforms standard decision trees and achieves performance on par with ensemble methods, while producing shallow trees that retain or even improve predictive accuracy.

Counting Beyond Frames: Codec Motion Tells Frozen Diffusion VLMs How Many

📅 2026-11-25 16:30
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Counting how many times something happens is among the simplest questions about a video and among the hardest for video-language models, autoregressive and diffusion alike. The difficulty lies between the frames: a model samples only a few of them, while each repetition unfolds as movement in between. Video codecs already record this movement densely as block displacements, and codec-based video models use it to shape their visual input. We are the first to show that this motion provides direct evidence for the count: a candidate count is a testable motion hypothesis, and unwinding the cycles it implies measures how strongly the observed directions support it. We present CoDiCount, a unified training-free framework that turns this test into a count potential and applies it while a frozen diffusion vision-language model (dVLM) denoises its answer. At every denoising step, structured count messages map each option letter, or each grammar-valid edit of an open integer, to the count it expresses and reweight the model's own probabilities within that set. Consistent gains over the official decoders on MotionBench, TOMATO, and SVCBench demonstrate the broad generalization of CoDiCount across diffusion models, datasets, and diverse counting tasks. With CoDiCount, a 7B dVLM outperforms GPT-5.1 and Gemini-2.5-Pro on MotionBench, surpasses GPT-4o and much larger open models on TOMATO, and matches GPT-5.4 on SVCBench.

ProxyGenBench: Benchmarking Proxy-Guided Video Generation

📅 2026-11-25 14:31
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recent video generators produce realistic videos, yet controlling how scenes unfold remains challenging. Structural proxies, such as animated 3D blockouts, guide generation by specifying scene layout, motion, camera behavior, and event order. However, these coarse inputs leave details unspecified and may contain artifacts that should not be reproduced. Successful proxy-guided generation must therefore Preserve intended structure, Complete missing content and correct proxy artifacts, and Bind text, image, and audio conditions to the right entities and events. We introduce ProxyGenBench to study these interacting requirements, with 312 evaluation cases from 60 scenes across three scene tasks and controlled variations in proxy fidelity, text detail, and multimodal inputs. We evaluate five video generators using verified, case-specific checklists. Experiments show that proxies improve spatiotemporal control, but greater proxy detail can coincide with poorer completion, while strong structural adherence does not ensure correct binding.

ProxyGenBench: Benchmarking Proxy-Guided Video Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Hierarchical Semantic Learning for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with Missing Modalities

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal sentiment analysis (MSA) in real-world systems must handle unpredictable modality missingness caused by sensor failures, privacy masking, and unstable transmission. Existing methods either learn reliability-aware representations or reconstruct missing inputs, often using a single flattened encoder output. This overlooks deep encoders' semantic hierarchy and intra- and inter-modal interactions, causing semantic averaging and ill-posed mappings. We propose Hierarchical Semantic Learning (HSL), a framework that exploits semantic hierarchy for robust MSA with missing modalities. Hierarchical Semantic Decoupling exposes low-, mid-, and high-level feature streams from each modality encoder. Cross-Modal Cross-Level Semantic Reconstruction performs ladder-style recovery, using high-level semantics from observed modalities as global anchors to progressively reconstruct mid-level structures and low-level details of missing modalities. To stabilize cross-level reconstruction, Hierarchical Cascade Error Supervisor applies prototypical contrastive regularization to observed and reconstructed features, enforcing level-specific semantic consistency and limiting error propagation. Finally, Hierarchical Interactive Fusion captures dense interactions across modalities and semantic levels to produce robust predictions under diverse missingness patterns. Experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, IEMOCAP, and CH-SIMS show that HSL outperforms baselines in most missing-modality settings.

PURA: Video Token Pruning via Unified Spatiotemporal Redundancy Assessment

📅 2026-11-25 15:08
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Video large language models incur substantial inference costs from long visual token sequences. Existing video token compression methods mitigate these costs by exploiting spatial redundancy within frames and temporal redundancy across frames. However, many approaches impose predefined processing orders or fixed allocation ratios for spatial and temporal compression, limiting their ability to adapt to the varying balance of spatiotemporal redundancy across videos. To address this limitation, we propose PURA, a training-free method that prunes visual tokens through unified spatiotemporal redundancy assessment. For each token, PURA constructs spatial and temporal reference candidates and selects the reference with the lowest representation error across the two dimensions. The representation error is combined with token importance to determine each token's removal cost. To avoid the time overhead of removing the lowest-cost token one by one, we introduce Coordinated Pruning, which iteratively groups mutually compatible low-cost removals into batches for simultaneous execution and updates the pruning state after each batch until the target budget is reached. Extensive experiments on three video large language models and five video understanding benchmarks demonstrate that PURA achieves a favorable trade-off between task performance and inference efficiency.

PURA: Video Token Pruning via Unified Spatiotemporal Redundancy Assessment

📅 2026-11-26 14:33

WebVibe: Multimodal Interactive Website Generation via Reverse Synthesis and Visual Self-Distillation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Although multimodal large language models translate single screenshots into static HTML, generating interactive functional websites from design specifications still struggles with multi-page routing and stateful widget transitions. Examining existing training pipelines reveals two bottlenecks: (1) in forward data synthesis (X -> Y), writing the specification X before code execution leaves unimplemented or altered requirements in X as supervision noise; and (2) in reinforcement learning, scalar rewards discard the rich state transitions observed during replay. Based on these findings, we introduce WebVibe and Interactive-SDPO to train InteractiveCoder. WebVibe reverses synthesis into Y -> X by verifying cross-page and in-page interactions in the browser before deriving multimodal inputs from replay-confirmed states to build the 31,542-site WebVibe-32K corpus with reusable execution contracts. Interactive-SDPO then conditions a weight-sharing self-teacher on the replay trace and a 2x2 state-transition montage to provide token-level supervision at zero inference-time overhead. On the public Vision2Web L2, one-shot InteractiveCoder-9B nearly 3x its base model's functional score (20.2 to 55.2 FS) and matches a 1T-parameter coding agent in a single pass with 111x fewer total parameters and 78x fewer tokens. On our WebVibeBench, InteractiveCoder-4B and 9B further outperform all evaluated baselines by over 23 points.

Score-based Black-box Attack via Adversarial Condition Guided Diffusion Model

📅 2026-11-25 15:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generative models enable unrestricted adversarial attacks that aim to fool classifiers while preserving the natural appearance and semantics of the original image. However, existing diffusion-based black-box attacks largely rely on transferability from surrogates, making their effectiveness sensitive to differences between surrogate and victim. Improving transferability can also distort semantic content and degrade image quality. Moreover, retaining the original prompt to preserve fidelity leaves the diffusion model's conditional prior underused for adversarial guidance. We address these limitations by adapting score-based black-box attacks to reverse diffusion, using the victim's output probabilities to guide latent updates without accessing its gradients. We further introduce adaptive-target guidance, which uses the victim's current runner-up class as an adversarial condition to steer denoising towards the decision boundary. Because such guidance can introduce unwanted semantic changes, we also propose target-search guidance, which searches for a small perturbation of the original condition embedding using a score-based attack. This achieves comparable attack effectiveness with better image quality than runner-up guidance. Experiments on the ImageNet-Compatible dataset show that our framework outperforms transfer-based baselines in both attack effectiveness and image quality.

Attack Policy Selection can Mislead Monitor Evaluators

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI monitors, classifiers that flag dangerous behaviour by AI agents, are often evaluated against transcripts generated by attack policies acting as proxies for dangerous behaviour in deployment. We evaluate an ex-production OpenAI monitor as a case study and find that measured false negative rate is highly sensitive to both the method used to generate attack policies and the level of optimisation applied within that method. This rate ranged from 0.5\% to 20.8\% across optimisation levels of a single method (reinforcement learning), and from 6.4\% to 23.0\% across methods at their best observed optimisation level (optimised prompting vs.\ transcript editing). Every attack policy was developed against proxy monitors only: all reported results show attacks transferring to the held-out GPT-5.4 target monitor. Our results suggest that monitor evasion evaluations can easily mislead: an evaluation using only basic attack methods may give false confidence that a monitor is strong. The sensitivity to attack-policy choice raises a problem: interpreting any particular result as evidence about deployment performance requires justification. The transcripts produced by attack policies are proxies for the dangerous behaviour a monitor must flag in deployment. These proxies can introduce visible artefacts, such as reasoning about a ``side task'' absent from the instructions shown to the monitor, that affect monitor judgements. Given the sensitivity above, the choice of proxy can change an evaluation's conclusion. Reading these results as evidence about deployment therefore requires a separate legitimacy argument for why the proxies are informative, including any justification of conservativeness. Making convincing arguments remains an open problem.

Can Its Own Misses Teach an MLLM to Find Small Brain Metastases?

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are becoming general readers of medical images, yet they remain weak at locating small abnormalities. Brain metastases expose this gap: at 384 px a 0.1 mL lesion covers less than a quarter of one visual token, and at one false positive per scan frontier MLLMs find fewer than 30% of lesions of 0.1-1 mL. Fine-tuning on one dataset helps but transfers poorly, and improving it further is slow trial and error steered by a single score. We introduce LANTERN (Lesion-finding AgeNt Through Error-driven Recursive learNing), a framework in which an MLLM improves recursively from its own misses. LANTERN recasts model development as an error-driven loop: a failure census attributes every missed lesion to one of four causes, an LLM mentor revises one of four levels (evidence acquisition, visual representation, learning policy, decision aggregation) per round, and a rule frozen before the first round decides what is kept, without test data and with every tool call audited. Over 15 unattended rounds, LANTERN raises a fine-tuned 4B MLLM from finding 75% to 94% of lesions at one false positive per scan on BraTS-MET, and 91% of those of 0.1-1 mL, compared with at most 51% and 29% for five frontier MLLMs read under the starting protocol; the gains hold on an external cohort (89%) and on unseen meningiomas (77%). These results show that an MLLM's own misses, attributed to their causes, can drive its reliable recursive self-improvement.

AliceAI-Foundation-80B-A3B-Base

📅 2026-11-25 15:14
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present AliceAI-Foundation-80B-A3B-Base, a mixture-of-experts language model with 80 billion total parameters and approximately 3 billion active parameters per token, trained from scratch at Yandex and released under the Apache 2.0 license. The model combines Kimi Delta Attention with full attention, uses Attention Residuals, and is trained with Muon. Training comprises general pretraining on 17.5 trillion tokens, two context-extension stages, and a dedicated stage for reasoning and agentic interaction. We describe the data collection and augmentation pipelines, evaluation protocols, architectural ablations, and scaling-law experiments. We identify the limitations of held-out training loss as a proxy for downstream performance and introduce an auxiliary loss on mixture-of-experts outputs to stabilize training at the predicted learning rate. Domain-specific data selection and augmentation improve factual knowledge, mathematics, and programming skills, while agent trajectories introduced during pretraining improve performance on agentic benchmarks after supervised fine-tuning. In zero-shot reasoning evaluation, the model achieves leading results among open base models of comparable size, with 88.7% pass@8 on IMO-AnswerBench and 82.9% pass@8 on LiveCodeBench v5-v6. To evaluate Russian-language factual knowledge, we introduce and release two benchmarks with their evaluation protocols: WikiWebFacts, which tests short factual questions drawn from encyclopedic sources and search queries, and HardMultiQA, which tests more demanding factual questions inspired by user interactions. Our model scores 86.5% on WikiWebFacts and 67.9% on HardMultiQA, compared with 83.2% and 65.4% for DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base (284B total and 13B active parameters) and 72.8% and 54.5% for Nemotron-3-Super-120B-A12B-Base (120B total and 12B active parameters), outperforming both larger models on these benchmarks.

EPPC-CARE: Staged Hierarchical Annotation of Communication Behaviors in Secure Patient-Provider Messages

📅 2026-11-25 15:21
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Objectives: Patient portal messages provide invaluable information about communication dynamics between patients and healthcare providers, but extracting it manually is labor-intensive. We developed and evaluated EPPC-CARE, an automated framework for identifying communication behaviors together with the supporting text for each classification. Materials and Methods: From 867 messages, we obtained 5,516 reference standard annotations. Each annotation is a triplet of communication-related text (span), a broader communication category (Code), and a specific subcategory (Sub-code). We evaluated EPPC-CARE's identified triplets across six open-weight large language models (LLMs) against supervised fine-tuning and communication-based models such as EPPC-OASIS. We also compared EPPC-CARE with human expert annotations on 100 messages. Results: EPPC-CARE improved performance over EPPC-OASIS across all six models. EPPC-CARE's Triplet F1 improved by 2.90 percentage points on average, with a best score of 64.4%. On the human expert evaluation subset, EPPC-CARE and the human expert showed comparable agreement with the reference standard, with differences favoring EPPC-CARE. Discussion: These findings suggest that identifying triplet components (span, code, subcode) sequentially can improve performance across model families while preserving the supporting text behind each identification. EPPC-CARE links the communication code and subcode with the source text (span), supporting human verification of automated annotations in clinical informatics. EPPC-CARE may therefore make larger-scale studies of patient-provider communication more feasible and provide a foundation for human-in-the-loop annotation workflows. Conclusion: EPPC-CARE offers a more reliable and reviewable approach for automated characterization of communication behaviors in secure patient-provider messaging.

Honest but Wrong: Formalizing Retraction in the Ethics of Speech Acts

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A speaker who asserts honestly may later discover that its assertion was false. Such erroneous truths are not lies, yet they raise the question of whether, and on which moral grounds, the speaker must retract them. Using the FIPA-ACL performative disconfirm, we extend a belief-based framework that evaluates speech acts under deontologism and consequentialism to the case of retraction. We prove that the honesty duty of deontologism, which bears on utterances when they are made, never requires retracting an erroneous truth, and add a persistent one, which forbids letting the addressee believe what the speaker no longer believes. On a legal-advice case implemented in Answer Set Programming, we show that deontologism and consequentialism both require the retraction, but that consequentialism no longer does once it comes too late to prevent harm.

Show Your Work: Can Small Open Models Read SEC Filings with Evidence a Machine Can Check?

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A fluent answer from a language model is not a verified one. We ask whether small open models that run on a single consumer GPU can extract numbers from SEC filings with evidence a machine can check: the page and a verbatim quote containing the number. We introduce Filing-Evidence, a benchmark of 1,000 questions over 90 recent 10-K and 10-Q filings of 45 US companies, with answer keys taken from the filers' inline XBRL tags. We evaluate ten instruct models from ten developers (7-21B parameters) on one RTX 4090 in three setups: plain, check-and-retry, and the model's own reasoning mode. Accuracy with checkable evidence ranges from 17.1% to 82.7% in the plain setup, and the best result is 84.5% (Qwen3.5-9B). An automatic checker rejects many wrong answers, but between 16 and 167 answers per 1,000 give a wrong value for an answerable question and still pass every check. In the plain setup, 57% of these are scale or unit errors and 30% take another period or column of the right line. Reasoning significantly reduces such errors for 4 of 5 models that offer it. Retrying after a failed check raises accuracy by up to 3.9 points but never reduces them. Questions, model outputs, code and protocol are public.

InterGenEHR$^+$: An Interactive Framework for Natural Language-Conditioned Generation of Longitudinal Patient Trajectories

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Objective: To develop and validate InterGenEHR$^+$, an interactive framework for natural language-conditioned generation of synthetic tabular electronic health records (EHRs) and longitudinal trajectories for scenario-based evaluation of machine learning models. Methods: An optional large language model (LLM) parser converts clinical requests into structured constraints. Conditional variational autoencoders and diffusion models generate single-time-point records, while diffusion models generate longitudinal trajectories. We evaluate parsing, distributional fidelity, imputation, clinical-constraint adherence, mortality prediction, and clinical plausibility using a multi-institutional cohort of 874 patients receiving venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO). Results: Gemma 3 4B with few-shot prompting achieves the highest parsing performance with a low spurious slot rate. IGE-TabDDPM and IGE-PMVAE perform well in single-time-point generation; IGE-CSDI, IGE-FGTI, and IGE-MTSCI lead on different longitudinal fidelity metrics. Across five random seeds and three classifiers, synthetic cohorts retain predictive utility for mortality prediction on real test data. Clinician ratings support trajectory plausibility and alignment with requested scenarios. Conclusion: InterGenEHR$^+$ supports clinician-specified generation of EHR trajectories with statistical fidelity and downstream predictive utility in the evaluated VV-ECMO cohort. Extension to other clinical settings requires further validation. Significance: The framework supports scenario-based evaluation of machine learning models in data-sparse critical care settings.

Proverbs As A Window On Collective Prescriptions

📅 2026-11-25 18:12
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Proverbs, as repositories of traditional wisdom, have informed human reasoning for millennia. Yet the collective prescriptions they encode have remained largely unquantified. We introduce a computational framework to analyze nearly 50,000 English-language proverbs. Using large language models, validated against human judgments, we map the topical landscape of proverbs, assess their prescriptive power, and quantify their recommendations on key dimensions of human judgment and behavior. We find that most proverbs are prescriptive, offering guidance in domains under individual control, such as preparation and prudent action, whereas proverbs about uncontrollable circumstances, such as fate and mortality, tend to describe rather than prescribe. When prescriptive proverbs take a consistent stance, they favor caution over risk, long-term over short-term outcomes, honesty over deception, and kindness over malice, and show a moderate tendency toward suspicion over trust. On dimensions involving genuine trade-offs, especially autonomy versus compliance, proverbs provide contradicting advice rather than a single recommendation. This analysis provides a quantitative map of the collective prescriptions in a culture's proverbial wisdom, revealing a core of prudent, honest, and forward-looking guidance alongside varied advice where circumstances dictate the best course of action.

From Models to Services: Systematizing Security Assurance for Model-as-a-Service

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models are mainly delivered as cloud services, in which the assets of different principals are coupled within a single service pipeline, while the related security research is scattered across several fields with differing threat models. This paper systematizes this research from a service perspective. We decompose Model-as-a-Service (MaaS) into a five-layer stack and five trust boundaries, define six adversary classes and six security goals, use them to code 120 studies, and evaluate 50 defenses under a unified set of criteria. The results show that 44.2% of the studies concentrate on behavioral integrity, while service integrity, availability and cost, and accountability together account for only 13.3%. Defenses are evaluated on production services at less than half the rate of attacks, and none of the cryptographic or hardware mechanisms that protect against the provider can be adopted unilaterally by customers. Finally, we propose a research agenda by comparison with current standards and regulations.

Reproducer Synthesis for AI-Reported Bugs in the Linux Kernel

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agentic review systems are increasingly flagging suspected bugs in Linux kernel patches as textual reports. A maintainer who receives such reports needs further inspection to determine whether the report is true. The inspection requires understanding whether the bug can be triggered and, if necessary, manually constructing one. As a consequence, despite the excessive textual explanation, many reports go unanswered. In this paper, we argue that a runnable proof-of-concept (PoC) is the key information in such AI-generated kernel review reports. It is, however, challenging to automatically synthesize a valid and runnable PoC. We present ReproCue, a framework that generates PoCs for AI-generated Linux kernel reviews. ReproCue replays the historical kernel bugs and learns one reproduction finite-state-machine (FSM) per subsystem from successful runs. An FSM records the steps a reproduction goes through, together with the requirements observed at each step. Given a new review, ReproCue follows the FSM of that subsystem, adjusting the PoC and the environment as execution shows what is missing. In 100 recent AI-generated reviews, ReproCue writes PoCs for 49 of them, compared with 10 for the current agentic reproducer and 9 for the state-of-the-art directed fuzzing. Excitingly, with our generated PoCs, Linux kernel maintainers confirmed 69.4% of them, much higher than 9.3% from Sashiko where only textual explanations are included, making AI reviews more actionable in practice.

Anti-AI in the Wild: From Toxic Discourse to Action

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Online discussion of generative AI has become increasingly contentious, and communities devoted to supporting, opposing, or debating AI have emerged on social media. Some of these communities take anti-AI actions, such as deploying crawler tarpits and data-poisoning services to interfere with data collection and model training. Yet, the scope of these actions and their relationship to online discourse remain poorly understood. In this paper, we present the first measurement study of online conflict over generative AI, from toxic discourse to anti-AI actions. We collect 931,640 posts from 25 Reddit communities, label them with a large language model detector validated against human labels, identify 3,312 anti-AI actions, and trace the output of three deployed anti-AI tools. Our analysis reveals that 30.0% of posts in communities devoted to this conflict are toxic, compared to 4.4% in general AI communities. Anti-AI actions in these communities mainly target AI crawling or model training data, such as image cloaking and data poisoning. We further examine the implications of these anti-AI actions for AI training, by analyzing the text and code generated from deployed tools. Notably, 66.5% of the code samples generated by a data-poisoning service contain fabricated facts, misused APIs, or broken logic, yet 93.6% passes a public code-curation pipeline, close to the rate for real source code (95.0%). For crawler tarpit output, adding a period to the end of each paragraph increases the curation survival rate from 16.4% to 91.9%. We also find that crawler tarpit and data-poisoning output has already entered Common Crawl, a widely used public web archive, and that crawler tarpit output has also entered FineWeb, a released pretraining dataset. We hope our study can facilitate a better understanding of online conflict over generative AI and its implications for content moderation and AI training.

Asset4D-Bench: Benchmarking Coding Agents for Editable 3D and 4D Reconstruction

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A cart can follow its reference trajectory while its cargo floats above the deck. Asset4D-Bench tests whether coding agents recover objects, motion and interactions from visual references as editable Blender scenes. Its 114 conditions span image editing, video reconstruction with matched full or partial starter assets, and independently selected from-scratch tasks. We independently reopen projects and measure them against native reference states. The 228 archived scores include original delivery outcomes and versioned measures of scene quality. On 24 from-scratch tasks, each model earns full dynamics credit on 14. Of these, three GPT-6.1-Sol and eight GPT-6-Astra reconstructions earn zero relation points. Under the fixed scoring weights, relations contribute 5.15 points to the 6.50-point Sol-minus-Astra difference in mean recovered fidelity. Differences in execution and recovery policy across archived runs preclude a causal estimate of starter-asset effects.

Sage-Med: Scaling Medical Multimodal Intelligence

📅 2026-11-25 15:39
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Establishing effective scaling laws for medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) remains a persistent and fundamental challenge. Our systematic evaluation shows that existing medical MLLMs tend to underperform frontier general-purpose MLLMs across a range of medical tasks, while scaling model size primarily improves text capabilities rather than multimodal perception, and enabling reasoning modes fails to close this visual gap. To investigate how medical multimodal intelligence scales in practice, we introduce Sage-Med-Preview, a 35B-parameter medical multimodal model with 3B activated parameters, built by combining stage-specific curation over 322.7 million candidate records with a progressive training curriculum spanning medical pretraining, staged supervised fine-tuning, and adaptive reinforcement learning. Across this curriculum, progressive supervised refinement and adaptive reinforcement learning yield consistent stage-wise gains, substantially advancing both specialized visual understanding and medical language capabilities. Evaluated against 77 competing models across 238 clinical tasks spanning 61 public and 13 private multi-center benchmarks in more than 40 modalities, Sage-Med-Preview achieves an average score of 58.26% on the 31 public multimodal benchmarks. It outperforms the state-of-the-art open-source medical model Hulu-Med-235B-A22B at 50.99% and the general scientific model Intern-S2-Preview at 52.24%, and approaches the advanced proprietary model GPT-6.2-Sol across multiple tasks such as comprehensive multimodal understanding, report generation, and visual grounding. Although substantial challenges remain in complex medical reasoning and multi-step clinical workflows, this report provides empirical evidence, actionable data and training recipes, and a rigorous benchmark toward scalable clinical multimodal intelligence.

Will AI Replace Juniors? How Task Complexity Reduces Automation Risk

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

How does task complexity shape firms' willingness to substitute entry-level labour with artificial intelligence (AI) tools? We study this question using a pre-registered survey experiment with managers from 1,435 German firms in the information economy and manufacturing industry. Managers are randomly assigned to consider a long lasting need for additional capacity involving either well-structured and standardised tasks, or more complex tasks that require professional judgement, contextual knowledge and independent problem-solving. Relative to standardised tasks, complex tasks reduce willingness to use AI rather than hire an entry-level employee by 0.54 points on a five-point scale and increase the importance attached to human oversight by 0.15 points. Further, pre-treatment responses indicate that managers expect the availability of AI applications to reduce hiring of both entry-level employees and experienced workers, but significantly more strongly for entry-level workers.

MUSE: Multi-Task Uncertainty-Aware Self-Supervised Encoder for mmWave ISAC

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) allows a wireless system to use the same waveform for communication and environment sensing. At mmWave frequency bands, ISAC enables diverse human sensing tasks, such as localization, gesture recognition, and pose estimation, with high precision. However, each task requires training a dedicated model, typically from scratch on a task-specific dataset, with no mechanism to flag unreliable predictions. This hurts the scalability, transferability, and reliability of mmWave ISAC models. In this work, we propose MUSE (Multi-task Uncertainty-aware Self-supervised Encoder), a unified framework for mmWave human sensing. MUSE leverages a self-supervised transformer model with per-dataset tokenization and is trained via masked autoencoding across five datasets. During fine-tuning, MUSE uses evidential classification heads that enable uncertainty quantification. MUSE replaces N separate encoders with a single shared transformer encoder, an N-times backbone parameter reduction, while achieving a 9.7 percentage points improvement in classification performance compared to the task-specific model, showing that learning jointly across tasks improves per-task generalization. Across four classification tasks, MUSE produces reliable uncertainty estimates that enable selective prediction, improving accuracy by abstaining on the least confident 20% of predictions. We further improve the reliability of the model's confidence estimates using temperature scaling and conformal prediction. Finally, MUSE's uncertainty signal remains reliable even under cross-user deployment, assigning higher uncertainty to wrong predictions than to correct ones 78% of the time, and recovering 9.2 percentage points of accuracy through selective prediction, with no additional retraining on the unseen user.

Effect of class proportion shifts on classifier curvature

📅 2026-11-25 16:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Changing the class mix can alter mean loss without changing the classifier. To distinguish this reweighting from changes in the model itself, we studied ten-class CIFAR-10 classification in separate branches for two target classes, measuring the trace of the loss Hessian with respect to the output-layer parameters on fixed images of each target class. Across ten new initialisations, after 60 updates to the output layer with frozen features, the trace changed by -0.152% on average in branches where the target class share rose from 10% to 19%, and by +0.071% in branches where it fell to 1%, relative to each branch's initial trace. These changes do not isolate the effect of class share from that of the assigned training images. We developed a forecast based on initial class gradients after examining the first experiment's results and then tested it prospectively without modification. For direct increases from 10% to 19%, rather than increases after a deficit, its mean absolute error was 0.022 percentage points on the initial-trace scale, compared with 0.152 for a zero-change forecast and 0.104 for equally weighted gradients. Using the assigned class proportions improved the forecast of how much the trace would change.

Real2SimLab: A Unified Platform for Real-to-Simulation Transfer

📅 2026-11-25 18:23
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Real-to-simulation transfer, which builds simulations from real-world observations, has long been studied in research and industry. Recently, coding agents have become capable of generating simulation programs from video recordings, and several benchmarks have been proposed to evaluate them. However, each individual benchmark defines its own inputs, deliverables, and scoring pipeline. This leads to $N \times M$ integrations when evaluating $N$ methods on $M$ benchmarks. To this end, we present Real2SimLab, a unified platform for real-to-simulation transfer. It evaluates a method by running its written program and scoring the output simulation with the original scorer of each benchmark. Moreover, Real2SimLab provides a pipeline to create training data and reward annotations for real-to-simulation methods. Our pipeline carefully avoids data contamination by filtering out the video recordings that exist in benchmarks. For each video recording, a program is first produced, and then our pipeline computes its reward with the same code that Real2SimLab uses for evaluation. So far, it supports 47 coding agents and 5 non-agent methods on 6 benchmarks. Both the platform and Awesome-Real2Sim, our research map of nearly 300 real-to-simulation papers, are open source, and we invite the Real2SimHub community to build them together.

AI Buckingham: Agentic Discovery of Dimensionless Scaling Laws Across Physical Regimes

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Classical dimensional analysis defines admissible dimensionless representations but does not determine which combinations capture the dominant physical dependence, whether it changes across physical regimes, or when dependent-variable rescaling is required. We present AI Buckingham, an agentic framework for autonomous dimensionless discovery from natural-language descriptions of physical problems and observational data. It couples reasoning and planning by a large language model (LLM) with predefined symbolic and numerical tools. Literature-based knowledge retrieval supports dimensional checking, gradient-based clustering identifies physical regimes, and mutual information provides an objective, reparameterization-invariant criterion for identifying dominant dimensionless groups. Explicit numerical criteria determine the group count and assess simplification. Across ten physical systems spanning fluid dynamics, magnetohydrodynamics, thermal convection and additive manufacturing, AI Buckingham recovers established similarity representations and resolves regime-dependent scalings. For the longstanding challenge of predicting turbulent drag on rough surfaces, it identifies informative reparameterizations of surface statistics that improve equivalent sand-grain roughness estimates for engineering-oriented drag modelling.

Brand Recall and Category Accuracy in Language Models: An Audit of 1,000 B2B SaaS Brands

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A language model can name a brand without classifying it correctly. This study measures these outcomes separately for 1,000 B2B SaaS brands across 333 product categories. On Gemma 4 12B, with no external retrieval, category prompts elicit 510 brands (51.0%). A separate five-candidate category probe ranks the reference label first for 787 brands, a distractor first for 194, and yields little separation for 19. Among the 981 separated rankings, the error rate is 19.8%. It falls to 3.5% for the 547 brands with a winner-to-runner-up log-probability margin above 0.6. These are within-cohort measurements, not calibrated confidence estimates. Exploratory regressions associate technical coverage with recall and an owned-properties composite with category accuracy, but the composites use different adjustment sets and do not establish causal effects. Across four Gemma generations, measured recall is 39.8%, 44.3%, 51.1% and 51.0%. A separate representation-based sentiment measure has little linear association with customer ratings (r=0.03, n=921). The findings support reporting recall and category accuracy separately. Failure to elicit a brand does not establish its absence from model parameters or training data.

LiveVAE: Causal Window Attention Makes Efficient Streaming Video VAE

📅 2026-11-25 18:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

While video diffusion models have grown faster and increasingly streamable, the video VAE remains a primary latency bottleneck across interactive generation and editing pipelines. Existing VAEs and their acceleration methods rely mainly on heavy convolutions or full frame attention, and most focus only on decoding, leaving encoding costly for streaming conditional generation and editing. This raises a natural question: Do video VAE encoding and decoding actually require such global spatiotemporal modeling? By analyzing the effective receptive fields of pretrained video VAEs, we find that both encoding and decoding a patch rely almost entirely on its spatial and temporal neighbors. To exploit this strong locality, we introduce LiveVAE, a streaming encoder and decoder built on causal window attention, which cuts per frame compute while keeping the temporal KV cache bounded for streaming. Decoupled encoder/decoder distillation followed by joint finetuning preserves the pretrained latent space, making LiveVAE a drop-in replacement for the original VAE. On MiniMax-H3, LiveVAE achieves 5.2-6.2x faster encoding and 3.0-3.3x faster decoding than the official VAE with higher PSNR. Paired with our dedicated inference infrastructure, LiveVAE pushes the speedup to 13.4x with almost no quality loss. Code and models will be released.

PersonaFactory: Stylistic User Prompt Augmentation with Synthetic Persona Bank

📅 2026-11-25 17:42
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models increasingly replace real people in the AI development pipeline, as they are used to generate training dialogues at scale and to drive alignment and evaluation. However, out-of-the-box models produce monolingual and stylistically flat texts as synthetic user prompts, failing to replicate the real user-AI communication mechanics. We present PersonaFactory, an end-to-end pipeline that generates detailed user personas and uses them to augment existing user prompts from their point of view, producing a bank of over 740000 structured synthetic personas detailing their background and communication behaviors, and a corpus of roughly 1.24 million persona-styled synthetic conversations spanning 85 languages. We evaluate the effect of personal features on style adoption and intent preservation, using an LLM-judge and heuristic metrics to assess augmentation fidelity together with the downstream impact on assistant persona control through training data. Results show that our structured, attribute-based style representation enables per-axis control and better intent preservation at comparable style fidelity than free-text persona descriptions used in existing methods. A human expert evaluation found the augmented conversations plausible, with favorable ratings for intent preservation, style naturalness, and multilingual authenticity. Finally, PersonaFactory conversations achieve comparable performance to original training data on capability benchmarks, are preferred on real user prompts at equal reply length, and adapt their reply style more closely to users without explicit steering.
