Tokenisation is not in APX

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

In this paper, we prove that, unless P=NP, no polynomial-time algorithm can find a tokeniser whose objective is within any constant factor of the optimum, meaning that tokenisation is not in the APX complexity class. We prove this for direct and bottom-up tokenisation, under both a compression or unigram log-likelihood objectives, and for binary or larger alphabets. We further show that, even over unary alphabets, direct tokenisation is APX-hard, and thus admits no polynomial-time approximation scheme.

Improving and Expanding Video Editing Capabilities Without Videos

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Image editing models can follow a wide range of instructions, but video editing models still lag behind, limited in part by the difficulty of collecting paired source and edited videos. Is it possible to transfer advanced image editing capabilities to video editors without new paired videos? We introduce Image-to-Video Transfer (I2VT), which improves pretrained video editors and expands them to new editing tasks using an image editor. It enables efficient post-training with no architectural changes and no new videos, using only individual images. Our key idea is to let the video editor role-play as an image editor: it edits an image as a one-frame video, with the image editor guiding the video editor's predictions at each denoising step. Although the video editor sees only single images during training, at test time it applies the improved and expanded capabilities to whole video clips while preserving temporal consistency. To push the boundaries of video editing, we introduce OpenVE++, an extension of OpenVE-Bench that adds four new editing tasks useful for video production, including action changes, expression changes, body movements, and scene relighting. Our experiments across three different video editors show that I2VT improves existing editing capabilities and expands them to new tasks, and our models achieve state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on both OpenVE-Bench and OpenVE++.

Assessing the Physical Interpretability of AlphaEarth Foundation Embeddings Through Multi-Sensor Satellite Time-Series Benchmarking for Agricultural M...

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Accurate and dynamic maps of agricultural LULC are important for agricultural water resource management, particularly in areas where water scarcity is prevalent, such as Morocco. Here, we evaluate the utility of AlphaEarth Foundation Model (AEF) embeddings for agricultural LULC and irrigation-type mapping in Morocco. AEF embeddings were compared to single-date and time-series remote-sensing features using three machine-learning classifiers: XGBoost, CatBoost, and TabPFN. We also evaluated whether AEF embeddings can: (a) provide an efficient and transferable alternative to conventional direct use of Earth observation products and (b) reconstruct biophysical information. Results showed that multi-temporal feature time series achieved the highest performance, with accuracies of 0.93 for agricultural LULC and 0.87 for irrigation-type mapping when using TabPFN. However, AEF embeddings yielded comparable results, achieving accuracies of 0.91 for agricultural LULC and 0.85 for irrigation-type mapping, while reducing the need for extensive data processing and manual feature engineering typically required for satellite products. Spatial LULC mapping using TabPFN achieved comparable overall accuracies (0.85-0.86) across the three feature configurations, but revealed substantial differences in mapped tree cover, ranging from 14.57% for surface reflectance to 29.79% for AFE. Physical probing revealed that AEF embeddings strongly encode spectral indices (A30-SWIR: rho=0.58-0.67), (A38-NDMI: rho=-0.72), vegetation (A49-NDVI: rho = 0.72-0.73), and SAR structure (A25-VH: rho = 0.71). Future research should focus on enhancing the interpretability of embedding-based approaches by incorporating physical knowledge to develop more transparent and trustworthy AI systems for agricultural applications.

Hijacking 3D Generation: Targeted Adversarial Attacks against Image-to-3D Models via Prefix Trajectory Alignment

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Online services that deploy Image-to-3D models enable users to generate high-quality 3D assets from a single image. However, the generated 3D geometry can violate content policies and cause broader security risks. To prevent such content, these services typically use policy-restricted inspectors to examine uploaded images before generation. Since the inspector examines only the visible content of the input, a visually benign image can still induce the generation of policy-restricted 3D geometry. However, turning this vulnerability into an effective attack is non-trivial in the black-box setting: global backpropagation through the long DiT generation trajectory is difficult to fit, while different image preprocessing pipelines across services can substantially reduce transferability. In this paper, we present PTA3D, a targeted adversarial attack against Image-to-3D models that addresses these challenges through Prefix Trajectory Alignment (PTA) and Preprocessing Aware Optimization (PAO). PTA progressively aligns the prefix DiT outputs of the adversarial trajectory with those of the target trajectory, where the core geometric structure is formed, allowing the final geometry to be controlled without optimizing through the complete generation trajectory. PAO exposes adversarial optimization to diverse image transformations rather than a single preprocessing pipeline, reducing sensitivity to the mismatch between surrogate and target services. We establish a prefix-to-output bound and a preprocessing-aware transfer bound to theoretically characterize the effectiveness of the two designs. Experiments on three open-source Image-to-3D models and three online services show that PTA3D achieves 41.5%-88.5% ASR on online services and 90.8%-94.6% ASR in white box settings, substantially outperforming existing attacks.

ChemoMAE: Self-Supervised Spectral Representation Learning Reveals Chemical Heterogeneity in Historical Wood

📅 2026-11-25 15:45
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

In historical timber buildings, multiple environmental factors act over centuries on wood whose chemical composition varies across anatomical regions, producing complex patterns of degradation. Understanding how timber members degrade is important for the effective conservation and restoration of historical buildings, yet the mechanisms underlying these patterns remain unclear. To investigate these mechanisms, we used self-supervised learning to establish a common basis for comparing specimens from timber members that differ in wood species and service history. We examined transverse sections of 49 wood specimens from several historical buildings in Japan using near-infrared hyperspectral imaging. ChemoMAE, a self-supervised Transformer, learns pixelwise spectral representations through masked denoising that exploits multicollinearity among spectral variables. Clustering these representations yields spatial maps without predefined chemical-state categories. Because anatomy and degradation can produce spatially continuous regions, we formulated Local Label Agreement (LLA) to quantify agreement among labels assigned to neighboring pixels. Using spectra alone, ChemoMAE yielded higher mean LLA than baseline methods on validation specimens. We then trained ChemoMAE anew and fitted cluster centers across all specimens. Spatial structures consistent with wood anatomy and the expected effects of surface exposure emerged in the resulting maps. By cross-referencing regional spectra and spatial distributions across specimens in light of wood anatomy, service histories, and prior studies, we proposed models of internal and surface degradation as mechanistic hypotheses. This exploratory AI for Science framework gives researchers a basis for linking latent patterns learned through self-supervision with domain knowledge when investigating how material states form.

Quantum Machine Learning for Signature Forgery Detection

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Offline handwritten signatures remain a primary behavioural biometric in legal and financial documents. Detecting skilled forgeries from a static raster image is hard because pen pressure, timing and stroke order are missing. This work studies a family of quantum machine learning pipelines for offline signature forgery detection on a public Kaggle corpus of images split into two classes. We pair a ZZ feature map quantum kernel with three classical learners: K-Nearest Neighbours, Support Vector Machine and Random Forest. We then propose a deeper hybrid that uses EfficientNetB0 as a frozen feature extractor, reduces the resulting 1280 dimensional representation to a qubit register sized subspace through a quantum compatible principal component projection and decides class membership with a nearest neighbour rule. On a single held out test fold the proposed hybrid reaches 92.62 percent accuracy. On stratified five fold cross validation the same pipeline yields a mean accuracy of 91.26 percent with a standard deviation of 1.13 percent. The pipeline keeps a quantum embedding step that is portable to NISQ hardware. We report a controlled ablation across six quantum variants, confusion matrices, a t SNE projection and per class precision, recall and F1 score.

FoundSurface: Feedforward Ray-Based 3D Scene Surface Reconstruction from Unposed Images

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Accurate 3D surface reconstruction from unposed images remains a fundamental challenge in computer vision. Recent feed-forward pointmap methods eliminate the need for camera poses but inherently produce discrete, sparse representations. In this paper, we present a generalizable pipeline that directly recovers continuous, high-fidelity 3D surfaces from unposed images by seamlessly integrating pointmap- and ray-based representations. Our approach first extracts explicit geometric priors using a pre-trained pointmap model. A query-view-centric selection module then identifies geometrically relevant reference views via a robust surfel voting mechanism. Next, a cross-attention regressor bridges target query rays with reference features to estimate a continuous coarse surface. Lastly, a raylet-based module aggregates local 3D features to carve out high-frequency details and analytical normals. Trained as a single foundation model on 9 diverse datasets, our method demonstrates exceptional zero-shot generalization on 6 unseen datasets, clearly surpassing state-of-the-art baselines in geometric accuracy and detail preservation.

WorldExpand: Building Coherent 3D Worlds via Sparse Joint Panoramic Expansion

📅 2026-11-25 17:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Constructing complete 3D worlds from a single panorama is challenging because large portions of the scene remain unobserved. Existing panoramic-video methods expand spatial coverage through dense sequential generation, but neighboring frames contain substantial redundancy, long trajectories accumulate errors, and independently generated directions may be inconsistent. We present WorldExpand, which formulates single-panorama world expansion as the generation of sparse, complementary, and high-resolution panoramic observations. This representation concentrates the generation budget on informative scene regions and preserves the fine-grained details required for 3D construction. WorldExpand addresses two key challenges: where to observe and how to synthesize. Our Coverage-Guided View Selection identifies informative target locations using scene-adaptive quadrants and warp-based coverage scores, while our Joint Panoramic Diffusion uses Gaussian-splatting-based geometric warps to jointly synthesize four pose-grounded and cross-view-consistent panoramas. A diagnostic-guided global--local attention schedule retains global attention only in sensitive layers, reducing the training and inference costs of high-resolution joint generation. The resulting posed panoramas can be directly fed into an existing reconstruction pipeline to build an explicit 3D representation. Experiments show that WorldExpand recovers content behind occluders and around distant corners, improves both novel-view synthesis and downstream 3D reconstruction metrics across various datasets, and generalizes robustly to diverse in-the-wild scenes. WorldExpand runs significantly faster than the state-of-the-art panoramic-video method while producing higher-resolution observations with stronger cross-view consistency.

Where Does the Asymmetry Enter? Staging a Fairness Audit Across a Fused Multimodal Moderation Pipeline

📅 2026-11-25 14:50
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Almost every short-video upload now passes through a classifier that fuses video, speech, and on-screen text into one decision about whether it may be seen. It moderates every language community on the platform at once, and fairness across them is judged, if at all, from one accuracy score. That score is compatible with error falling almost entirely on one language group. An audit that goes looking reads the model's decisions, the last stage of a long pipeline and the one whose test has the fewest items. We ask where in a fused pipeline a subgroup asymmetry can be detected at all: in what the classifier may read, in what its branches encode, or in what it decides. We answer this with a post-hoc audit of a published TikTok classifier, holding all three stages to one significance standard using only its logged decisions, labels, and pre-fusion features, with source language as the subgroup axis. The asymmetry is significant at the stage deciding what the model may read, and again in what the text branch encodes. At the earlier stage, a language-blind cap admits less of a Vietnamese clip than an English one, for reasons of segmentation, not verbosity. The asymmetry is undetectable at the decision, where the smallest subgroup leaves twelve items to test. An audit reading only decisions looks where its evidence is thinnest; the same test staged upstream recovers a disparity the decision-level view returns as noise.

A Hard Background Pairing Transfers across Classifiers and Images

📅 2026-11-25 16:24
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

An image classifier names the object in an image, and its accuracy is the share of images named correctly. A classifier that names a car on a road may miss the same car pasted onto clouds. Composite benchmarks measure such failures by pasting objects cut from photographs onto backgrounds from other photographs. A pairing puts every object label on a different background label, such as truck on sea. The pairing on which some classifiers do worst, their hardest pairing, makes a strict test, but may be hard only for them or only on the photographs used to choose it. We show that the hardest pairing of some classifiers stays hard for others and on new photographs. We split 40 ImageNet classifiers into halves balanced by reported ImageNet accuracy. One half picks its hardest pairing of 8 object and 8 background labels from Common Objects in Context (COCO). The other half, held out of the choice, compares that pairing with random pairings of the same objects and backgrounds. For the 14 held-out classifiers with little net accuracy change after pasting objects back onto their own backgrounds, mean accuracy on composites from new photographs falls from 50.3% over random pairings to 43.7% on the hardest pairing, 6.6 percentage points lower. A benchmark using these composites can compare this pool's held-out classifiers on the fixed and random pairings.

Generation as Reverse Encoding

📅 2026-11-25 17:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Pretrained visual representations accelerate generative learning by abstracting away appearance and spatial detail. Yet the same abstraction makes the return to pixels far harder, shifting much of the burden onto a larger decoder. We ask why the representation-to-pixel path should be learned independently when the encoder already provides a learned pixel-to-semantics path (the semantic encoding path). We introduce RevFlow, based on the principle of generation as reverse encoding. RevFlow uses intermediate representations of a frozen encoder as successive generative targets, first generating deep semantic representations, then shallow representations that retain appearance and spatial detail, before decoding to pixels. To learn these transitions without training a separate generator for each stage, we develop HyC-DiT, a unified generative model with a standard DiT backbone shared across stages and stage-specific condition injection. We further show that native matching in semantic representation space enables few-step generation. RevFlow accelerates both learning and sampling. On ImageNet $256 \times 256$, RevFlow reaches 1.96 FID at 690M and 1.74 FID at 1.55B after only 5 training epochs. It also achieves 30.25 dB reconstruction PSNR with an 89M decoder. After a short native-matching adaptation phase, RevFlow-XL achieves 2.85 FID using a single sampling step in each stage. Our code repository is at https://github.com/ChengShiest/RevFlow

An Information-Theoretic Analysis of Information Hiding in Semantic Communication

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Semantic communication shifts the transmission goal from the accurate recovery of syntactic symbols to the reliable conveyance of semantic content, yet a systematic information-theoretic characterization of information hiding at the semantic layer is still missing. Building on the semantic information theory of Niu-Zhang and the classical information hiding theory of Moulin-O'Sullivan, this paper establishes an information-theoretic framework for information hiding under semantic communication. The classical hiding model is extended to a two-layer structure (semantic host, syntactic host, syntactic composite signal, syntactic received, semantic received) bridged by synonymous mappings f_s, f_x and the inverse mapping f_y^{-1}, subject to four distortion constraints: syntactic transparency, semantic fidelity, syntactic perturbation, and semantic destruction. The capacity is cast as a Stackelberg game with the hider moving first and the payoff J(Q,A) = I(U~; Y~ | K) - I(U~; S~ | K), yielding the single-letter maximin C^s = max_Q min_A J(Q,A), whose achievability and converse follow from random coding and joint typicality decoding. Relative to the classical capacity, comparison payoffs built from the up/down semantic mutual information give the endpoint sandwich C^s_end(0,1) <= C^s <= C^s_end(1,0) with the classical value C in the same interval, while closing semantic tolerance yields C^s_no-tol <= C_cl under a fair comparison. These results clarify that semantic hiding is not unconditionally superior to classical hiding. Binary, ternary, and Gaussian examples confirm the degeneration under one-to-one mappings and the capacity loss under coarsening.

Correlation-Aware Evidential Fusion for Trusted Multi-View Classification

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Trusted multi-view classification aims to make accurate and reliable predictions by integrating information from multiple views. Existing methods typically estimate class evidence and uncertainty for each view and combine the resulting opinions through aggregation rules. However, they generally treat different views as independent information sources, overlooking the correlations among them. Highly correlated views tend to produce similar evidence, and treating them as independent sources may repeatedly count consistent information and give it disproportionate influence, while weakening complementary evidence that provides additional information. Consequently, such correlation-unaware fusion may not only limit classification performance but also produce miscalibrated uncertainty estimates. To address this issue, we propose Correlation-Aware Fusion of Evidence (CAFE), a framework for trusted multi-view classification. CAFE applies complementarity-based trust discounting to the evidence produced by view-private experts and introduces a correlation-aware constraint to reduce the repeated contribution of similar evidence. It then learns sample-specific weights from the global multi-view context to adaptively combine the correlation-adjusted private evidence with cross-view collaborative evidence. Extensive experiments on six real-world datasets demonstrate that CAFE consistently improves classification and calibration performance in general settings, with particularly significant gains when strong correlations exist among views.

What Is Redundant at High Resolution? Efficient Attention and Feed-Forward Design for All-in-One Image Restoration

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multi-scale architectures are widely adopted in all-in-one image restoration (AIO-IR) to capture larger-scale context at low-resolution stages and refine details at high-resolution (HR) stages. However, processing spatially large feature maps at HR stages incurs substantial computational costs and memory traffic, making efficient HR processing challenging. To address this challenge, we propose High-resolution Global-Local Restoration (HiGLR), an efficient multi-scale AIO-IR network grounded in an analysis of spatial redundancy in self-attention and convolutional feed-forward networks (ConvFFNs). Specifically, this analysis identifies three key redundancies: per-query readout at the finest-scale stage, key routing across neighboring queries at intermediate stages, and spatial mixing in the expanded feature spaces of ConvFFNs. Accordingly, HiGLR replaces costly self-attention at the finest-scale stage with a learned input-independent kernel. At intermediate stages, we propose a Block-Routed Hybrid Attention, which combines coarse-scale dense attention with fine-scale block-sparse attention over value residuals relative to coarse summaries, and shares routing indices across neighboring queries. We further introduce a Dual-Space FFN that performs spatial mixing in a compact space and pointwise nonlinear transformation in an expanded space, enabling efficient kernel fusion. Combining these designs, HiGLR achieves state-of-the-art performance while delivering up to a $4.24\times$ inference speedup over existing methods.

InterConRank: Joint Node Centrality in Interconnected Heterogeneous Networks

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Two nodes with identical connections within their own layer can differ in importance if their neighbors in another layer occupy different positions in the network. We introduce InterConRank, a centrality algorithm that computes a separate node ranking for each layer of an interconnected network. Directed dependencies specify which layers supply scores to each receiving layer. InterConRank normalizes each type of link separately and updates all layers together, so a score can cross into a layer in one iteration and propagate along its internal links in the next. We prove convergence of this joint update and evaluate the resulting rankings on four scholarly networks. On all four networks, InterConRank gives the highest rank correlations with both h-index and total citations for nodes in the collaboration layer.

CHD-Bench: Diagnosing Congenital Heart Disease through Interactive Examination

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Diagnosis of congenital heart disease (CHD) requires understanding the spatial relationships between cardiac structures, which clinicians assess by scrolling through CT or cardiac magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) slices and rotating 3D reconstructions. Although automated methods have advanced cardiac segmentation, identifying congenital heart defects from volumetric scans remains an open challenge. The two existing approaches rely on handcrafted diagnostic rules limited to static anatomical abnormalities. We introduce CHD-Bench, the first benchmark for interactive CHD diagnosis, defining a multi-label task and evaluation protocol over 247 CT and MRI scans from three cohorts covering 38 CHD types. We also present CHD-Harness, open-source software that runs a VLM in a 3D Slicer environment through 22 tools, manages each examination, and records the resulting diagnosis and supporting views. You Only Look Multiple Times: YoloMT, a Real-Time Streaming Model on a T4

📅 2026-11-25 15:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We introduce a new model for real-time streaming captioning on a single GPU. YoloMT is a 449.8M-parameter streaming captioner that provides Pareto-frontier performance among generative vision models in its class. TACC: Target-Aware Carrier Compression for Dense-Context Novel View Synthesis

📅 2026-11-25 14:41
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recent feed-forward approaches mark a paradigm shift in novel view synthesis by eliminating the reliance on per-scene optimization, directly inferring 3D representations from input views. However, their reliance on global scene-token context incurs substantial memory and latency overhead, constraining their ability to handle increasingly dense source-view contexts. Compressing source representations offers an intuitive way to limit this token budget. Prior general-purpose compression methods are not tailored for novel view synthesis, as their fixed, target-agnostic nature fails to adapt evidence allocation to individual target views. To overcome this, we introduce TACC, a target-aware compression module tailored for feed-forward latent NVS. Specifically, TACC organizes each source view into a progressive carrier hierarchy. Driven by source-target geometric relevance, it adaptively retrieves tokens to dynamically allocate exact computational quotas. This selective retrieval directly yields a compact, target-conditioned representation. Extensive experiments on the RE10K demonstrate that under dense input settings, TACC achieves $9.23\times$ and $64\times$ higher Effective FPS than Full Context on LagerNVS and LVSM, respectively. Effective FPS excludes one-time source encoding and Carrier construction.

Controllable CAD Generation and Interactive Editing of Fluid Rotors

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Computer-aided design (CAD) provides the geometric foundation for rotors in fluid rotating machinery. However, creating models for different rotors requires different modeling processes, making it difficult to control geometry through design intent and reuse existing models. We introduce VaneCraft, an agent-driven framework for rotor generation, inverse recovery, and editing through a unified analytical representation. The agent interprets design requests and invokes geometric modeling operations. The representation defines geometric controls through meridional geometry and independent boundary angle laws. An anisotropic partial differential equation (PDE) maps these controls to the blade surfaces. Geometric parameter correction (GPC) uses a joint geometric encoder and a parameter decoder to refine controls initialized from rotor category and dimensions. Inverse recovery maps existing CAD to the same geometric controls for reconstruction and continued editing. Experiments demonstrate that VaneCraft supports anisotropy control and parameter editing and outperforms representative baselines in rotor CAD generation tasks. Project page is available on \href{https://github.com/shenchen333/vanecraft}{GitHub}.

Multi-view fnirs identifies neurophysiological subtypes in post-stroke aphasia

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Post-stroke aphasia is clinically heterogeneous, and conventional classifications may not capture differences in brain functional organisation. We tested whether resting-state and task-based functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) could identify neurophysiological subgroups alongside clinical assessment.Seventy-nine patients underwent unsupervised clustering using three fNIRS feature views: resting-state activity and connectivity, language-task activation, and rest-to-task connectivity reconfiguration. Similarity network fusion and spectral clustering integrated the views. Clinical and behavioural measures were excluded from clustering.Three clusters (n = 32, 17 and 30) were identified. This solution had the largest eigengap and was the only candidate significant after permutation testing and Holm correction (adjusted P = 0.001). It was stable under subsampling (mean adjusted Rand index, 0.773), but agreement fell to 0.217 when the resting-state view was removed. Clusters differed in most regional intrinsic-activity measures and all resting-state connectivity summaries, with few differences in task activation or connectivity reconfiguration. Their profiles showed higher right anterior prefrontal activity, distributed fronto-sensorimotor and parietal alterations, and reduced interhemispheric connectivity, respectively.Cluster membership was not significantly associated with conventional aphasia subtype (Cramér's V = 0.287), and baseline clinical language scores and task performance did not differ between clusters.These findings identify three neurophysiological subgroups distinguished mainly by intrinsic activity and resting-state functional connectivity, not clearly aligned with conventional aphasia classifications or baseline clinical measures. Multi-view fNIRS analysis may complement clinical characterisation, requiring independent and longitudinal validation.

LuxAvatar: Real-Time Relightable Head Avatars via Causal Neural Shading of 3D Intrinsic Proxies

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present LuxAvatar, a feed-forward framework for real-time head avatar animation and relighting from a single portrait. To achieve both 3D controllability and complex facial light transport, we factorize avatar synthesis into an animatable 3D intrinsic Gaussian proxy and a causal autoregressive neural shader. This proxy disentangles canonical geometry and reflectance attributes, rapidly rasterizing deformed Gaussians into view- and motion-consistent PBR buffers. Conditioned on these buffers, our neural shader formulates forward rendering as a conditional video diffusion process, bypassing explicit PBR computation to synthesize realistic shading effects under target illumination. We achieve real-time streaming through heterogeneous distillation from a pretrained multi-step bidirectional relighting teacher to a two-step causal autoregressive neural shader, inheriting broad synthetic lighting priors while minimizing conditioning and decoding latency. To further specialize these priors to human heads, we construct a hybrid relighting dataset for domain-specific fine-tuning, capturing authentic facial dynamics and physically grounded light transport. Extensive experiments show that our framework enables 3D-controllable, real-time portrait animation and relighting, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in visual fidelity, motion control, and lighting accuracy.

Diagnostically Meaningful, Privacy-aware Synthetic Chest CT via Class-Conditional Latent Sampling in Diffusion Autoencoders

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large, well-annotated datasets are essential for developing medical artificial intelligence models, yet data sharing is often restricted by privacy regulations. Synthetic images may help address this limitation if they preserve diagnostic information while reducing the risk of reproducing patient data. We developed a diffusion autoencoder-based framework for class-conditional synthetic chest CT generation that incorporates latent-space distance filtering to reduce the likelihood of regenerating training samples. A diffusion autoencoder was trained on the public RSNA 2020 Pulmonary Embolism (PE) dataset. PE-positive and PE-negative images were synthesized by sampling latent representations from class-specific kernel density estimates. Samples with a nearest-neighbor latent-space distance below a predefined threshold were rejected. To assess diagnostic utility, ResNet-50 classifiers were trained across 10 independent runs using real, synthetic, or mixed real-synthetic datasets. Performance was evaluated on a held-out real test set using the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC), with DeLong 95% confidence intervals (CIs). Models trained on synthetic data achieved an AUC of 0.862 (95% CI, 0.851-0.874), only slightly lower than models trained on real data (AUC = 0.872, 95% CI, 0.861-0.883) and mixed real-synthetic data (AUC = 0.865, 95% CI, 0.855-0.876). These differences were statistically significant (p < 0.05) but small in absolute terms. A nearest-neighbor copy-detection analysis of 38,000 synthetic slices found no direct copies of real training images. These findings indicate that the synthetic CT images preserve diagnostically meaningful information for image-level PE classification while incorporating measures intended to enhance privacy awareness and reduce similarity to real patient data.

Random Voronoi Networks

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Nearest-neighbor methods are local, simple, and interpretable, but their non-parametric form makes them memory-intensive, expensive at inference, and difficult to integrate into differentiable neural architectures. We introduce Random Voronoi Networks (RVNs), a family of structured three-layer MLPs that express nearest-neighbor geometry through sampled Voronoi comparisons. The first layer computes Random Voronoi Features (RVF) from affine pairwise separators between selected training samples, the second layer aggregates these comparisons into Voronoi cells or top-k neighborhood scores, and the readout layer produces classification or regression predictions. We show that, when all samples and pairwise comparisons are included, step RVNs recover exact Euclidean k-NN, while ReLU RVNs provide a violation-score realization for 1-NN that can be relaxed into a differentiable soft weighting. Subsampling yields approximate RVNs with explicit control over the number of cells and features. We study several unsupervised sampling methods that trade off random coverage, directional diversity, coordinate sparsity, and empirical feature variance. Empirically, RVFs produce a highly effective data encoding strategy, providing performance boosts to downstream methods ranging from linear to modern tabular deep learning models. Finally, we showcase that RVF provides an interpretable feature map due to its k-NN based construction and ties to pairwise local directions. These results position our Random Voronoi formulation as a bridge between nearest-neighbor rules, random features, and modern tabular models.

WorldBridge: Bridging Scattered Views for Coherent 3D World Generation

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generating coherent and explorable 3D worlds from scattered images with little or no visual overlap is a fundamental and long-standing challenge in computer vision, which existing geometry-aware methods fail to address. Meanwhile, existing image- and video-based methods may generate unseen regions but produce inconsistent geometry across views. To bridge this gap, we present WorldBridge, a framework for coherent and controllable 3D world generation under scattered images with little or no visual overlap. WorldBridge encodes scattered references into a global scene context to guide geometry and appearance generation across views. Our temporal and spatial reference attention mechanism further enhances the fidelity of temporal references along the target trajectory and supports flexible spatial references outside it. A plug-and-play latent-reference consistency strategy improves reference fidelity through reprojection-guided test-time optimization. We also introduce the WorldBridge benchmark suite to evaluate 3D world generation from scattered, low-overlap references, which is rarely explored by current benchmarks. Both qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate that our method enables controllable and coherent 3D world generation, surpassing the existing state-of-the-art solutions. Our project page is available at https://davidyan2001.github.io/WorldBridge/.

CIRKDV2: Cross-Image Relational Knowledge Distillation with Contextual Modeling for Efficient Semantic Segmentation

📅 2026-11-25 17:49
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Existing Knowledge Distillation (KD) methods for semantic segmentation often guide the student to mimic the teacher's structured information generated from an individual image. However, they ignore the global semantic relations across various images that are valuable for KD. This paper proposes Cross-Image Relational KD V2 (CIRKDV2), which focuses on transferring structured pixel-to-pixel, pixel-to-region, and channel-to-channel relations modeled from global images in the dataset. A large teacher network often constructs a well-structured feature space in terms of global contextual dependencies. CIRKDV2 makes the student mimic the better structured semantic relations of the teacher, thus improving the feature representational capability. Beyond relational distillation, we further propose contextual feature distillation by using context aggregation to augment student pixel embeddings to match the semantic level with the teacher. This avoids the semantic gap problem between the teacher and student occurred in traditional feature distillation studies. Experimental results on Cityscapes, ADE20K, Pascal VOC, and COCO-Stuff-164K demonstrate that CIRKDV2 outperforms the state-of-the-art semantic segmentation distillation methods by large margins across various network architectures. Our code and pre-trained models are publicly available at https://github.com/winycg/CIRKD.

Reading, Not Adapting: Unlocking Frozen Visual Knowledge for AI-Generated Image Detection

📅 2026-11-25 17:09
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generalization to unseen image generators has driven efforts to adapt pretrained visual representations for detection. Yet recent findings show that vision foundation models already encode transferable detection knowledge, while further adaptation can impair generalization. This motivates a shift from backbone adaptation to knowledge readout. Conventional linear probing offers a direct way to read this knowledge but is limited to final-layer RGB features. Multi-cue detectors broaden the evidence base, but their designs introduce other tradeoffs: adaptation may compromise pretrained transferability, heterogeneous encoders complicate feature integration, and late fusion precludes intermediate cross-view interaction. To overcome these limitations, we propose ReMoT, a Mixture-of-Transformers-inspired framework that reads complementary knowledge from spatially aligned RGB and wavelet views within a fully frozen visual foundation. ReMoT employs staged joint attention to enable controlled local and global cross-view interaction, while learnable fusion queries progressively read complementary evidence across Transformer depth without writeback. ReMoT achieves 91.2% average accuracy across eight benchmark groups with 3,074 trainable parameters, attaining the highest overall average among the evaluated methods. These results support structured knowledge readout as a promising alternative to backbone adaptation. Code is available at https://github.com/yunncheng/ReMoT.

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Deep learning models for prostate MRI segmentation often struggle to generalize when trained on limited heterogeneous data, leading to boundary errors and over-segmentation. We present an augmentation framework using mask-conditioned synthetic and diverse T2-weighted MRI volumes to improve boundary precision and reduce over-segmentation. We employ a 2.5D weakly mask-conditioned denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) via channel concatenation. The diffusion model conditions on stacks of adjacent binary masks from training folds. The model generates T2 volumes consistent with the conditioning anatomy while diversifying tissue appearance. We evaluate the proposed approach on two cohorts of data: (i) the LUND probe cohort (n=432) for strategy selection and training nnU-Net from scratch with a 23% synthetic data mix, and (ii) the Medical Segmentation Decathlon (MSD) prostate cohort (n=32) evaluated with a 20% synthetic data mix. Experimental results on both these cohorts suggest that mask-conditioned DDPM augmentation can reduce over-segmentation errors and improve boundary adherence in data-constrained settings.

High-Fidelity Image Matting with Pixel-Space Diffusion Transformers

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Diffusion models have shown strong potential for image matting by transferring generative priors learned from large-scale pretraining. However, existing approaches largely rely on latent diffusion models, whose compressed representations and VAE-based reconstruction introduce a mismatch with the high-frequency precision required for matting. We present PixelMatte, a framework that adapts pretrained pixel-space diffusion transformers for high-fidelity image matting. We show that strong image-conditioned matting capabilities can be acquired even from a pure text-to-image model without paired image-to-image pretraining. Our approach reuses the backbone's native patch embedding to jointly process the input image, trimap, and noisy alpha matte as spatially aligned tokens, enabling direct pixel-space prediction without additional condition encoders or adapters. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of pixel-space generative priors for precision-sensitive image matting. Project page: https://white-yc.github.io/pixelmatte/.

DynMF2: Rigid Motion Factorization by Expectation-Maximization for Monocular 4D Reconstruction and Motion Segmentation

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A single casual video leaves a moving scene under-determined: a point seen once from each place can slide toward or away from the camera and still match every frame. We constrain the reconstruction with the structure of the motion itself. Building on DynMF, we model a scene's motion as a handful of SE(3) trajectories, each rigid, and let every point follow one of them alone, never a blend. DynMF2 computes this factorization directly from 3D point trajectories, with no rendering, by Expectation-Maximization (EM). Its M-step fits, in closed form, every rigid motion to the points assigned to it, and its E-step reassigns every point to the motion that explains it with the least error. The background is simply one more motion, the camera's, so no masks are needed. Three tools then revise the factorization: requery adds trajectories where the rendering shows parts the tracker missed, merge joins motions that move together, and split separates those that do not. Thus the number of motions follows the scene. Without masks, DynMF2 clearly outperforms the mask-based baselines in view synthesis on iPhone and NVIDIA and in 3D tracking on iPhone. It recovers what masks leave out, from thin parts and details to small movers far in the background. Its motions segment the moving parts of MOVi-F and iTACO scenes competitively, although DynMF2 is never trained to segment. What moves in a scene is not decided in advance: it emerges from the rigid motions themselves.

Feed-Forward Adaptive Gaussian Splatting for Interactive Medical Volume Rendering

📅 2026-11-25 14:43
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Path-traced rendering shows CT and MR volumes with realistic lighting, but it restarts after every change of view, clipping plane or transfer function (TF), the map from voxel intensity to colour and opacity, which limits the frame rate. Feed-forward Gaussian splatting models such as RenderFM predict the Gaussians directly from the volume instead of fitting them to each scan, and are fast. RenderFM, however, handles only CT, runs its network again after every TF edit, has no clipping operator, and places its Gaussians on a uniform 3-mm grid that blurs organ boundaries and cut faces. We present RenderFM-Next, a feed-forward model that addresses these limits. One network handles both CT and MR. Its Gaussians are predicted once per volume: a new TF needs no pass of this network, and a clipping plane cuts them exactly, using operators from prior work. The model predicts coarse 3-mm Gaussians for the whole volume and refines them to 1.5 mm only where refinement improves the image. A small score head ranks the coarse Gaussians by how much refining them would reduce the rendering error; at render time the user chooses how many are refined, and those next to a clipping plane are always refined, which sharpens cut faces. We build a benchmark of about 2,000 CT and MR scans with path-traced references under clinical presets, TF edits and clipping. Our RenderFM-Next improves over RenderFM retrained on this benchmark by 2.8 dB on CT and 2.4 dB on MR and renders a 1600x1600 view in 4-6 ms. Refinement adds 0.30 / 0.69 dB over the same model trained without it (0.51 / 1.17 dB on cut faces), and refining a learned 10% of the coarse Gaussians matches a random 50% with 2.4x fewer Gaussians. Project page: https://gaozhongpai.github.io/renderfmnext/

Comparative Study of Deep Learning Architectures for Brain Tumour Classification

📅 2026-11-25 17:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Accurate and early classification of brain tumours from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is essential for treatment planning, yet many published deep learning studies are trained and tested on small, single-source datasets, reporting accuracies that may not hold outside these datasets. This study compares six deep learning architectures for four-class classification (healthy, meningioma, glioma and pituitary tumour): a baseline convolutional neural network (CNN), an attention-based CNN, ShuffleNet, and EfficientNet-B0, ResNet-50 and Xception augmented with the Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM). The models were trained on a corpus assembled from four publicly available MRI datasets. Because these datasets carry no patient identifiers, a leakage-aware pipeline was developed that removes exact duplicates using MD5 hashing (550 images) and near-duplicates using perceptual hashing (8,024 images), and assigns visually similar images to the same fold during 5-fold group cross-validation. A leave-one-dataset-out (LODO) test on a fully held-out dataset was used to measure cross-dataset generalisation. Under a conventional random split, ShuffleNet achieved 97.1% accuracy with 1.2M parameters. Under leakage-aware group K-fold cross-validation, ShuffleNetV2 (98.41%) and EfficientNet-B0 (98.23%) were statistically indistinguishable (paired t-test, p = 0.19), and both significantly outperformed the custom CNNs. On the held-out dataset, accuracy dropped for every model: EfficientNet-B0 generalised best (78.4%), while ShuffleNetV2 fell to 66.2%. Glioma-meningioma confusion was the dominant error mode, and Grad-CAM maps showed that predictions were driven by tumour regions. These results indicate that in-distribution accuracy alone overstates real-world performance and that leakage-aware and cross-dataset evaluation should be reported alongside it.

AnyCam: Anything Can Represent Camera for Video Generation

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Camera-controlled video generation re-renders a video along a target camera trajectory, yet moving the camera around an object, the most natural way to change the viewpoint of an object-centric scene, remains difficult. Existing methods condition either on camera parameters, which are scale-ambiguous in monocular video, or on explicit geometry such as warped point clouds or depth, which breaks down under large viewpoint changes. We present AnyCam, a method that represents the camera with an anchor object in the scene: we reconstruct it in 3D and render it along the target trajectory, a camera signal that is scale-aware, view-complete and easy to specify relative to the object. A video diffusion model transfers the content and dynamics of the source video to the new viewpoint. Its H-Concat layout aligns source and target frames in time, so the model can be distilled into AnyCam-AR, a streaming few-step generator for on-the-fly camera control. Both models are trained on paired videos with pixel-aligned anchor renderings from multi-camera videos and static-scene captures. Experiments on diverse datasets and trajectories show that AnyCam performs favorably against camera-controlled video generation and 3D reconstruction methods in camera control and visual quality.

DocDUR-Net: Degradation-Aware Unified Document Image Restoration

📅 2026-11-25 18:29
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Captured document images often suffer from diverse degradations caused by uncontrolled imaging conditions, which reduce readability and obscure fine text structures. Single-degradation restoration methods are usually trained and deployed for individual degradation types, and therefore require model selection before restoration. Unified restoration models alleviate the need to maintain multiple restoration networks by sharing one network across tasks, but representative unified document image restoration methods still depend on external task selection. This dependency limits automatic document image restoration. We propose DocDUR-Net, a degradation-aware unified restoration network that derives restoration guidance directly from the input image. It introduces a Degradation Perception Module (DPM) to extract degradation representations and predict degradation weights, and Degradation Prior-guided Adaptive Feature Fusion (DP-AFF) to combine learnable degradation priors with degradation features extracted from the input. The resulting guidance is injected into a shared restoration backbone, enabling adaptive restoration without external task selection. DocDUR-Net also supports a manual mode with explicit task selection. Experiments on document image restoration benchmarks show that DocDUR-Net achieves the best average PSNR of 29.405 dB and SSIM of 0.960 among unified methods while remaining competitive with single-degradation methods.

Stranger Things: When Objects Appear Without Their Typical Neighbors

📅 2026-11-25 18:39
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Visual context is a powerful cue for object recognition: scenes inform what, where, and at what scale objects are likely to appear. Yet over-reliance on context becomes a failure mode when models predict objects based on their typical neighbors rather than the objects' own visual features, which we call typicality bias. While extensively studied in the pre-Transformer era, it has often been assumed that architectural and scale improvements would naturally solve it, and a systematic diagnostic across the modern vision stack remains absent. We provide such a diagnostic framework. We measure typicality bias via normalized pointwise mutual information (NPMI) over object pairs, and introduce STRANGE-Bench (Synthetic Typicality-RANked Grounded Evaluation Benchmark): a photorealistic, layout-controlled benchmark spanning 17 sub-tiers from typical to never-co-occurring object pairs. Across five model families (specialist detectors, open-vocabulary detectors, self-supervised, vision-language, and multimodal large language models) and three recognition tasks (supervised detection, open-vocabulary detection, and classification), the bias is prevalent: gaps of 5-11 p.p. persist when objects appear outside their typical company, and do not consistently shrink with scale. To mitigate it, we turn to data augmentation: existing augmentations are designed for visual diversity, not for breaking co-occurrence shortcuts, and sample from the very distribution that produces typicality bias. We propose Anchor-Atypicality Sampling (AAS), a simple sampling rule for paste-based augmentations that uses the NPMI signal to tilt the training-time co-occurrence distribution toward atypical partners. AAS reduces the typicality gap by up to 1.6 p.p. on natural-image COCO splits and 23.0 p.p. on synthetic STRANGE-Bench across three model families, without sacrificing overall performance.

DosePET: An Open-Source Dataset with 2100 Radiation Dose Maps for PET/CT

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large collections of three-dimensional radiation dose maps linked to patient anatomy are needed to develop artificial intelligence (AI) models for personalized internal dosimetry in nuclear medicine, but access to such data remains limited. Here we present DosePET, an open-source dataset of 2,100 patient-specific absorbed-dose maps generated by Monte Carlo simulation of publicly available PET/CT examinations: 1,503 [18F]FDG examinations from ENHANCE.PET and 369 [18F]PSMA-1007 and 228 [68Ga]Ga-PSMA-11 examinations from PSMA-PET-CT-Lesions, acquired at five institutions with seven scanner models. For each examination, the CT was resampled to the native PET grid to build a voxelized patient phantom, the quantitative PET image defined the radionuclide source, and radiation transport was simulated with GATE version 9.4. Organ and tissue masks were generated from the CT with TotalSegmentator. Each study folder contains a NIfTI dose map integrated from scan time to infinity, 117 binary organ/tissue masks, a report of regional dosimetric quantities, including mean absorbed doses, dose conversion factors and scan-time dose rates, and simulation metadata. The total processing time was 1,542.4 hours. Python tools support selective downloading and conversion to scan-time dose-rate or dose-per-decay maps, and an agent skill guides pairing with the source PET/CT images. DosePET is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/eltzanis/DosePET.

Who Is Ego? Scaling Cross-View Camera Wearer Identification and Segmentation beyond Humans

📅 2026-11-25 14:48
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A first-person (ego) image reveals what a camera sees, but not who or what carries it. Given a synchronized third-person (exo) image, camera wearer identification asks who is ego: which candidate carries the ego camera? We extend this task beyond humans to robots, vehicles, animals, and drones, and broaden its spatial scope (where is ego) from selecting a provided candidate mask to wearer segmentation with absence prediction and mutual camera localization across ego-exo views. We introduce FindEgo-1M, comprising 1.16M real and simulated ego-exo pairs across 12 wearer types, 17 domains, and 111 test settings. Our approach exploits a geometric relation: the ego camera's projection into the exo image, i.e., the epipole, lies on or near its wearer. We introduce EgoRay-ZS, the first method to turn a frozen feed-forward 3D reconstruction model, VGGT-$Ω$, into a training-free solution for wearer identification and segmentation. Its geometric readout ranks candidate masks or segmentation proposals by angular offset and distance to the ego camera, while the relative pose provides mutual camera directions. We then develop the full EgoRay model through pose-supervised training on FindEgo-1M and label-free test-time adaptation constrained by candidate masks. Without task-specific training, EgoRay-ZS achieves 78.0% identification accuracy on TF2023's original labels, versus 52.9% reported for the dataset-trained PEN. On FindEgo-1M, EgoRay achieves state-of-the-art chance-corrected accuracy of 87.1 and 77.7 on seen and unseen settings. On matched queries, EgoRay also outperforms GPT-6 Astra by 31.6 and 21.3 points on seen and unseen settings, respectively, while running $18\times$ faster. Data, code, and models will be released.

Blind spatially varying PSF estimation for fluorescence microscopy

📅 2026-11-25 15:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Optical aberrations degrade fluorescence microscopy images and measuring the underlying point spread function (PSF) often requires dedicated acquisitions or isolated emitters. Here we introduce DeepBlur, a neural network that estimates spatially varying two-dimensional PSFs directly from single images of biological structures. Trained entirely on physical simulations spanning aberrations, illumination and noise, it adapts to diverse imaging conditions without requiring user-supplied optical parameters. Using a deformable mirror, we acquire 1,290 images and assess blind PSF estimates against independent references. DeepBlur estimates agree closely with these references and improve deconvolution of experimentally aberrated images. Local PSF estimation also enables correction of spatially varying defocus and improves random-illumination super-resolution reconstruction in biological tissue. Available through DeepInv, a napari plugin and a web demonstration, DeepBlur can be readily integrated into computational microscopy workflows.

A Mesh for Every Moment

📅 2026-11-25 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present MEMO (a Mesh for Every MOment), a method that reconstructs the geometry of complete dynamic scenes, captured by multiple synchronized cameras, and extracts a mesh at every frame. Existing dynamic Gaussian splatting methods either model appearance alone or target isolated objects segmented by foreground masks, and all of them recover meshes by TSDF fusion or Poisson reconstruction, whose fixed grids limit the detail they can capture. At the core of MEMO is a per-Gaussian motion model inspired by Gaussian processes, which represents each Gaussian's position and rotation over time as a combination of temporal Gaussian kernels. The model keeps every Gaussian a valid surface element throughout the sequence and carries an implicit representation of the dynamic geometry, so a mesh can be derived at any time from a field defined by the Gaussians. Although common in real captures, large and fast motion poses a challenge that dynamic Gaussian splatting methods usually overlook. To account for it, we design a track-based initialization scheme that fits each Gaussian's initial trajectory to point tracks in closed form. We evaluate MEMO on Blender-Moments, a new benchmark of nine animated scenes with ground-truth meshes at every frame, and on two real-world datasets. MEMO reconstructs the most accurate geometry among state-of-the-art methods, and remains competitive in novel-view synthesis and training time. Project page: https://memo-4d.github.io

Benchmarking Segmentation Models for Scalable Mangrove Mapping with Sentinel-1/2 Imagery

📅 2026-11-26 14:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Mangroves are dynamic coastal ecosystems that contribute to carbon storage, coastal protection and biodiversity conservation, but are vulnerable to climate change. Very-high-resolution imagery such as Pléiades enables detailed mangrove mapping but it is costly and have a low revisit rate. Large-scale monitoring requires free and regularly available data such as Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 but their spatial resolution poses challenges for detailed mangrove mapping. In this work, we benchmark deep learning approaches for mangrove mapping from Sentinel-1 radar and Sentinel-2 optical imagery using reference maps derived from very-high-resolution Pléiades imagery. We compare four segmentation configurations based on CROMA and BigEarthNet-pretrained ResNet50 representations with different segmentation decoders. We construct a mangrove mapping dataset covering French Guiana, Suriname, and Amapá. CROMA + UPerNet achieves the best performance, with an F1-score of 0.9218 and an IoU of 0.8549. These results highlight the potential of pretrained multimodal Earth observation representations and open Sentinel data to support mangrove resilience through scalable monitoring.

Rank Before You Encode: Reading a Few Percent of a Whole-Slide Image

📅 2026-11-25 15:11
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Classifying a whole-slide image requires encoding thousands of high-resolution tiles with a pathology foundation model, which accounts for most of the inference cost. Existing efficient methods reduce this cost by selecting fewer tiles, but typically train a new model around their selection strategy. A largely unexplored alternative is to keep an existing full-bag model frozen and run it on selected tiles at the same encoder cost. We introduce this comparison, alongside random tile sampling at matched cost. To select tiles, we fit a lightweight selector with 3.3K parameters to the attention of a frozen model, without additional slide labels. The selector ranks regions from a slide thumbnail, encodes the top K regions at 5x magnification, and selects b for processing at 20x. We evaluate seven frozen MIL aggregators on six tasks and compare them with nine efficient methods trained on the same features and splits. At a budget of (K,b)=(1000,10), encoding costs about 6.6% of the full bag and is 10-14x faster, excluding tiling. The AUROC drop is no more than 0.005 in 33 of 36 aggregator-task pairs. At (500,10), the cost falls to about 4% and inference is 13-22x faster, but AUROC drops by up to 0.035 on CAMELYON17, where small metastases are easily missed. Selection improves over random sampling by about 0.10 AUROC on tasks with focal lesions, while random sampling performs well on diffuse tasks. In our comparisons, the frozen model at (500,10) is ahead of nine efficient methods in 55 of 60 paired comparisons. Giving an existing WSI model fewer tiles is thus a baseline that a new efficient model should be compared with at matched encoder cost.
